# Chapter 1: The Kasavu Bride

Four exits. The stair behind her, the wall-walk running east, the wall-walk running west, and the drop to the pepper terraces, which only counted if she didn't mind breaking something.

Neela counted them again while the sun went down red behind the Kettamala rampart. Then she held out her left hand, because a man in a grey mundu was waiting to put a bangle on it.

"Your wrist," Devan said.

He didn't take it. He waited for her to turn it over. That was the first thing she noted about him: he asked before he touched.

The ridged scar lay pale on the inside of her wrist, the shape of a drawn-out thumbprint. Resin had made it, and a hundred nights like the one coming. His eyes stopped there for exactly as long as it took to decide not to ask. Then he slid the plain gold bangle over her hand and closed the hinge. The metal was warm from his palm. It covered the scar to the last ridge.

"It sits wrong," he said. "I had it made from a measure the council sent."

"It sits fine."

"You haven't tried to move your hand."

She flexed her fingers. The bangle slid a thumb's width and stopped. "Fine," she said again.

Something in his mouth might have been the start of a smile. It went nowhere. Behind them the whole fort stood in the last light, a few hundred faces in the courtyard below, and an old woman with a lamp stood closest of all, breathing hard through her nose.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


Twelve.

That was the number she held under every other number. The twelfth contract, and she was Seventeen in a ledger a day's ride off, a girl in the book of the Resin House with a page that could be read aloud. Twelve contracts earned release. Release meant the page burned and a birth name handed back to her. She had never known that name. Not one syllable of it.

The Monkey Pass closed in about forty days. The rains came and sealed the cart road, and nothing she did after that would matter to anyone who'd paid for it.

So: forty days. A man who drilled alone at dawn. A fort with one great gate and sixty-odd soldiers. A wedding that cost somebody a great deal of money.

"Thirty-two," the old woman muttered, close enough to be heard, far enough to pretend she hadn't been. This was Janu, steward of the fort, who had been dabbing at her eyes with the end of her shawl since the first vow. "Thirty-two, and thin as a pepper vine in drought. I told him. I said, you'll eat at your own wedding, and look."

"Janu," Devan said, not turning.

"And her." Janu's whisper climbed. "Twenty-seven and not one word. Not a word through the whole rite. A girl that quiet is either holy or sulking."

"Neither," Neela said.

Janu's mouth shut with a click. Devan looked at the sky, and the corner of his mouth moved again.

"Then you can speak," he said.

"When there's something worth saying."

"I'll note it," said the warden of Kettamala, "for the council."


<center>\* \* \*</center>


The feast ran long, as feasts did.

Neela sat on the dais in her ivory kasavu, its gold border heavy across her shoulder, and collected names. It was an old habit and a stupid one, and she had never been able to put it down. The guild had given her a number. The least she could do was refuse to give the same to anyone else.

Kannan was the goat-boy, nine or ten, plastered against a pillar with a garland he'd been told to carry and had clearly decided to keep. Chinna ran the kitchen door, a broad woman who slapped the leaf plates down like a judge handing out sentences. Raiu held the corridor torch outside the warden's chamber, a young guard with a bad haircut and an excellent memory for who had gone past him and when.

Four exits from the rampart. Seven from the hall, if you counted the roof. She counted the roof.

Devan ate little. She watched him do it. He moved rice from one side of his leaf to the other and spoke to nobody who wasn't brought to him. When a captain with a laugh like a dropped pot clapped him on the shoulder, he allowed it the way a man allows weather.

"Captain Muthu," he said, when she asked. "My second."

"Does he sleep in the fort?"

"Yes. Do you want his hours?"

She hadn't expected that. "Do you offer them to everyone?"

"Only to people who ask." He took a mouthful of rice, chewed, swallowed. "Mostly he's in the lower barracks. He wakes me for the dawn watch, twice a week. He will not tonight."

"No," Neela said. "I suppose he won't."

Later, when the lamps burned low and the singing had turned to the kind that needed no words, he walked her down the corridor to the warden's chamber. Raiu stood at the turning with his torch.

"Thank you, Raiu," she said. "Mind the draught by the stair. It'll gutter your flame."

The young guard flushed to the ears and said something that wasn't quite a word.

Devan had stopped. He didn't stop for much, but he'd stopped for this. He looked at Raiu, then at her, and the careful flatness of his face gave way to something she could only call attention.

"Nobody presented him to you," he said. "You never went near him at the feast."

"No."

"He's been on this corridor since dusk. How did you learn his name so fast?"

She could have said that Janu said it. She could have said she'd overheard. She said, "How long did it take you to learn it?"

He was silent a moment. Raiu looked at the ceiling.

"Four months," Devan said at last. "I had to ask twice."

"Then ask me again tomorrow," Neela said, and went into the chamber ahead of him.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


She'd meant to be quicker about it. But the guild's rule was to learn a room before you needed it, and she stood just inside the door and did that while he latched it behind them.

A high room, whitewashed over laterite. One broad bed. A low lamp, sesame-scented, on a stand. A brass kindi beside the pillow, its spout bright. A sword on a rack and nothing else on the wall. And across from her, taking up the whole west side, two tall teak shutter leaves, closed against the dark.

Behind the shutters, she knew, was a ledge broad enough for a crouching body. The guild's drawings had said so. They hadn't said the wood was old and the ledge worn smooth by weather.

Chathu's voice came to her then, the way it did when she was careful and alone. It was soft as always, the patient voice of a man reading her a column of figures she had no choice but to accept. *Like a leopard kill, child. Throat and floor, and tell the rest as the beast would. And not before the marriage writ comes back sealed from Kodiyur, mark that. Not after the rains. The buyer is particular.*

The tooth was sewn into her waistband. It was a real one, yellow and worn, and she was to leave it in the wound. It pressed against her hip bone, a small hard question.

"You can ask," Devan said.

She turned. He'd taken off the wedding cloth and stood in his plain mundu, shoulders square, looking at her across the width of the room as if it were a distance he intended to respect.

"Ask what?"

"Whatever you've been counting since you came in." A pause. "You do count. Your lips move."

*Damn.* "How many guards on the stair at night?"

"Two on the west stair. Four at the padippura. Six on the wall, in pairs, changing at the cistern chain."

"The chain?"

"It clanks on the hour. They change on the third clank after the middle watch."

She stared. "You're telling me."

"You asked."

"You're the warden. Wouldn't you rather I didn't know?"

"I would rather you asked me than found it out." He said it flatly, without warmth, which made it somehow harder to dismiss. "Is there anything else?"

She made herself ask the real question, wrapped in a harmless one. "Why did you agree to this? A bride from a Kodiyur pepper house you've never met."

"The council pressed the match. I couldn't refuse without saying why." He stopped. "I won't answer the rest of that."

"Which rest?"

"The why."

It wasn't comfort. It wasn't even courtesy, exactly. It was a closed door with a plain label on it, and she found, to her annoyance, that she would rather have that than a smooth lie.

"The shutters," she said. "Do they stick?"

"The third slat creaks." He glanced toward the window. "I've never fixed it."

"Why not?"

"I like to know when the wind comes in."

It was an answer, and it told her nothing. She filed that away, too.

The bed was wide. There was no way to ask which side he took without the question meaning something, so she looked at the floor. He said, "The left is mine," and went around to it without looking at her. She sat on the right, where the lamp's reach ended. Her hands lay in her lap, the left one heavy with gold.

Then he did something she hadn't planned for.

He took the sword down from its rack. He drew it half an inch, checked the edge with his thumb, and slid it home. He knelt and laid it on the floor on her side of the bed, along the edge of the sleeping-mat, with the hilt toward her hand.

"What is that for?" she said.

"I sleep badly."

"That isn't an answer."

"It's what I'm going to say." He rose and went round to his own side. "If you wake and find me restless, don't touch me. That's all."

She looked at the hilt. Plain wire grip, worn at the thumb. It lay within reach of her right hand, and he had put it there with his own, in front of her, with the lamp still lit.

Either he was a fool, or he wanted her to think so, or he wanted to see what she did with it. Chathu had taught her which of those to bet on.

"Good night, Devan," she said.

"Good night, Neela."

He pinched the lamp himself. Dark rushed in, with the smell of hot oil and a draught from the third slat.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


She lay on her back and counted his breaths.

One to ten, steady. Eleven to forty, slowing. By a hundred he had gone still in the particular way of a man who has given up fighting the dark and let it put him down. Twice his breath snagged and smoothed. At two hundred and twelve there was a catch, a hitch like a bucket knocking a well-wall, and she held every muscle until it passed.

Two hundred and sixty. Two hundred and ninety. Three hundred.

Not yet. She gave him thirty more, because thirty was cheap.

There was no choosing tonight. The guild kept a watcher in the pass village, and every dawn he walked the hill edge and read which of an odiyan's marks had gone fresh. A first night with none read as refusal. Refusal went to Chathu, and Chathu answered refusal with a balance sheet. So the other one had to be on the hill before light, and Neela had to get her there past a sleeper.

She rose slowly, one hand flat on the mattress to take the weight, and stepped over the hilt of his sword. It stayed where he'd left it. He did not stir.

The floor was cold. She crossed it in her bare feet and stopped at the west window with the dark all round her and her pulse in her ears. The shutters were old teak, oiled and dark, held only by an iron latch. The long teak bar hung unused on its hooks beside them. A man who expected nobody at his window, then. She lifted the latch, eased the first shutter, then the second.

The third slat she did on purpose. She laid two fingers on it and leaned, and it creaked, a small dry crack like a knuckle. Better to learn now, with her clothes on, whether the sound woke him. She stood with her hand on the frame and counted twenty. Behind her the breathing went on, long and even, unbroken.

*Asleep,* she told herself. *He's asleep.* And she believed it, because she needed to, which was the kind of belief she'd been trained not to trust. A man who has trained himself to sleep awake does not change his breathing either.

Beyond the shutters lay the ledge and the night. The sky was black and full of stars, and the wind came up from the pass smelling of wet red laterite and pepper leaf. Somewhere below, the cistern chain clanked the hour. A dog barked once and was hushed.

She slid the bangle off her left wrist and set it on the sill. It was still warm. For a moment she looked at it, a small round weight of gold that a stranger had closed around her like a promise. Then she took the corked flask from the fold of her cloth and drew the stopper.

The smell struck first. Scorched tamarind and pine sap, bitter as a mouthful of ash. It was the smell of the Resin House and of every night she had ever been good at her work. It was the smell of being useful.

She shrugged off the wedding cloth and folded it on the sill. The wind raised gooseflesh along her arms.

One rule Chathu had drilled harder than the knife. Anyone whose eyes were on her for the next minute became a witness. A witness needed one word, said in her hearing while she wore the other shape and the night still held, and she would wear it for life. She already carried two witnesses, Chathu and Ammu, the way she carried her own name for the dead. A third would hold a cage in his mouth every night she slept under his roof. If the man behind her was awake, that was what she was handing him.

She tipped the flask against her fingers and the resin ran black and slow. She drew it across the ridged scar on her right wrist, then the left, then up the line of her throat where the oldest marks lay.

For a breath, nothing.

Then the burn took her.

It came in like a hot wire drawn through each scar, from wrist to elbow, from throat to sternum, and she bit down on her own lip until she tasted blood. She had done this a hundred times and never once grown used to it. Heat crawled up her arms. Her knees went, and she caught the sill. The stars smeared. Her vision narrowed to a long dark tunnel with the wind at the end of it, rich with a thousand smells she had no name for.

Behind her, in the room she'd mapped, a man lay breathing or pretending to.

Her spine began to bend.

# Chapter 2: What the Warden Saw

Devan had been ready for a knife.

He lay on his left side with one hand open on the sheet, because a fist wakes the whole body, and he counted her breaths past three hundred. He had built the night to give her every chance. The sword on her side of the floor, hilt toward her hand. The lamp pinched out by his own fingers. A man asleep, a wife awake, and the council's gift laid out like a plate.

She stepped over the sword.

She didn't look at it. She stepped over it the way a woman steps over a sleeping dog, and crossed the floor without a sound, and he understood that whatever she had been sent to do, it would not be done with steel.

Through his lashes he watched her lift the latch on the west shutters. The third slat creaked. He kept his breath long and slow. She stood very still for a count of twenty, and he gave her nothing to hear.

Then she set the wedding bangle on the sill and uncorked a flask.

The smell reached him across the room. Scorched tamarind, pine sap, something bitter underneath. He had smelled it once before, from a sealed jar passed hand to hand in a cold classroom when he was twenty and being taught what to do about people like her. He had not expected to know it at once.

She folded the kasavu on the sill. She drew black resin over her wrists and up her throat, where the old scars lay in pale rows, and for a breath she did nothing at all.

Then she bit her lip, and it began.

He had been told it took about a minute. He had not been told what a minute was. She braced both hands on the stone and her back bowed, and the bones of it moved under the skin like something turning over in sleep. Her fingers spread and shortened. Her shoulders rolled forward. A sound came out of her that was not a cry, only breath forced through a closing throat. Gold came up through her skin in patches, then in spots, dark rosettes on a pelt that rose over her ribs like water filling a footprint.

It was the most private thing he had ever seen. He did not look away. That was the job, and it was also the crime.

At the end of the minute a leopard crouched on his window ledge.

It was long and heavy, too big for the stone and perfectly at home on it. The tail hung once, then drew up. Its head came round slowly toward the bed. Two eyes caught what starlight there was and held it, green and flat, and the nostrils worked.

Devan lay like a man carved on a tomb.

The cat looked at him for three heartbeats. He counted them because counting was all he had. Then it gathered itself, flowed over the edge of the ledge, and vanished into the dark.

The bangle sat on the sill beside the folded cloth, small and bright, with nobody to wear it.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


He did not say it.

Not aloud, not under his breath, not shaped on his lips. He lay with his tongue pressed flat behind his teeth like a man holding a coal, and made himself go through the lesson in order, because the lesson was all that stood between him and the thing he wanted to do.

Anyone who watches an odiyan turn, at dusk or at dawn, with his own eyes, is her witness. Not the one who sees only the beast. The one who sees the change.

A witness who speaks her night-shape aloud, while she is in shape and before dawn and in her hearing, cages her. She stays that animal for life. Her mind goes out over about a year, like a lamp left without oil.

Intent does not matter. The instructor had said it three times, tapping the table. Anger cages her. A joke cages her. A fever or a word spoken in sleep cages her all the same, and nothing on earth undoes it.

Devan was a witness now. He had a live weapon in his mouth, and he was going to carry it to bed beside the woman it was made for.

When the cistern chain clanked the next hour, he rose. He went to the window barefoot and stood in the dark beside the open leaf of the shutter, where he would be hidden until she reached the ledge. The air smelled of wet laterite and pepper. Below, the terraces lay silver and empty. There was no sound at all, which he supposed was the point of her.

Then he sat down on the cold floor with his back to the wall, and waited, and let himself think about the west stair.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


Asha had signed every note with a little parrot. A quick five-stroke bird with a whole beak, always laughing at something. She had left them on his table, under his cup, inside his boots. *Eat something. A.* She had been twenty-two and fast and funny, and afraid of exactly one thing, which was the dark.

They found her at the foot of the west stair, below the carved parrot with the broken beak. The wounds were a bear's. Shoulder to hip, deep, the work of claws as long as his fingers.

There were no bears inside the walls of Kettamala. No bear could have come up the cliff, or through the padippura, or past two men on the stair. He had stood in her blood that morning and counted the gates one by one, and when he reached the end of them, he had known what kind of thing had done it. Someone had paid. Something had worn a bear for a night, then put on a face and walked away.

Six years. And now one of them slept, or didn't sleep, in his bed.

His hands had closed on his knees. He opened them.

It would be so easy. She would come back along the ledge in the grey, a beast with a woman's mind caught inside it, and he would be standing there. One word. He could watch her understand it. He could watch her stop being anyone.

The rage was not hot. It was cold and level and quiet, and it felt like clear thinking, which was why he distrusted it.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


So he made himself think properly.

A caged odiyan tells nothing. That was the first fact, and the plainest. If he caged her, he would own a handsome animal and no answers, and he had married her for answers. Someone had put her in his bed. Someone on the council had pressed this match, and tokens with genuine council wax were going out through the Monkey Pass to Thurai. He did not know whose hand was on that wax. But a woman who could speak could be made to say, and a woman who was a cat on a chain could only be looked at.

Next: Muthu. Muthu would want her dead. Not caged; dead, on the day. He would say it cheerfully, by every rule of the border, and by the rules he would be right. A killer in the warden's own bed was no matter for a warden's feelings.

Third: whoever had sent her had planned this with care. A bride, a ward of a Kodiyur pepper house he had never heard of until the council wrote. A date set before the rains. He had been the bait and the mark at once. If the buyer wanted word that she had failed, a caged cat on the warden's ledge would send it. If Devan stayed silent, someone would sooner or later come asking why the warden was still alive, and he wanted to see who. The safest thing was to know more.

All of that was true.

And under it, where he did not usually look, lay the real reason, older than the council or the wax.

He had a pair of hands that could end a man in a breath. He kept them closed. Eight years he had kept them closed, and there was a reason, and the reason had a face, and he did not think about it in the dark. He did not spar. He did not strike first. He gave orders to men who did, and he drilled alone at dawn, so the one thing he was good at would never be pointed at a living person. Power was safe in his keeping because he did not use it.

The word was the same. One sentence, and he would be a man who had used it.

Not safe, then. Merely not spent.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


The chain clanked the last hour of the night. Grey crept up behind the pass, thin as milk in water.

He got up. His knees had stiffened, and he stood a moment at the window with his hand on the sill, near the bangle. Then the wind swung the open leaf of the shutter, and the third slat began to complain.

He caught it. He laid his palm flat against the wood, as he would on a horse's neck, and held it until the wind dropped and the sound died in his hand. He left the shutters exactly as she had left them. She would come back to the room she had mapped, with nothing moved.

He crossed to the bed. He lay on his left side, the hand open on the sheet, and lowered his lids to a crack.

She came back with the light.

A shape on the ledge first, low and tired, one paw set after another along the stone as if the stone were hot. The leopard folded down onto its belly and lay there, flanks heaving. Then the pelt began to go.

It went the way it had come, and slower. Spots thinned and ran together and faded to skin. The long spine drew in. A shudder went through the whole animal, and then there was a woman on the ledge on her hands and knees, her hair stuck dark to her neck.

She was shaking. Not a little. Her arms would not hold her, and she let herself down onto her side on the stone and lay with her knees drawn up, breathing through her teeth. The scars at her throat stood out wet and pale. He had a good view of them. He wished he didn't.

She looked very small.

That was the thing he had not planned for. All night he had thought of her as an edge, and the thing on the ledge was a person who had not eaten since the feast and had just been turned inside out. She crawled to the sill. She dragged the folded kasavu around herself with fingers that kept missing the cloth, and her wrist, when she reached for the bangle, tilted toward the light.

She put the gold on. It slid over the scar to the last ridge, the way he had made it do. She held that hand against her chest with the other one, hard, for a count of ten.

Then her head came round toward the room.

Devan shut his eyes.

He heard the cloth whisper. He heard bare feet on the cold floor and the shutter drawn home, and the third slat gave its dry crack as it closed. He kept his breath even and slow and let it drag a little at the end, the way a sleeper's does. She stopped, and he felt her stop. It was a long way to the bed.

Then the mattress dipped on the far side, and a body lowered itself into the dark as carefully as if the bed were made of glass. She lay with her back to him. She was still shaking. He could feel it through the frame.

She did not touch the sword.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


The chain clanked dawn.

He rose without waking her, which was not hard, because she was past waking. She lay on her side with the sheet to her chin and one hand fisted under her cheek, the bangle bright against the pillow. He stood a moment looking at her, taking in what he saw, the way a man takes inventory of a debt.

*Witness,* he thought, and did not say.

The corridor was grey and cold. Raiu had gone off watch, and Janu was on the other side of the door with a brass pot of water and her mouth already set for scolding. She stopped. She looked at his face.

"You didn't sleep," she said. "I knew it. A man doesn't sleep in his own wedding bed. He lies there like a log, thinking of the pass."

"Janu."

"Don't *Janu* me. I raised you." She pushed the pot at him. "She is thin. Thinner than you. She sat at that feast and ate two bites of rice and left the rest, and I watched, I watched the whole time."

"I know," he said. "Leave rice in the chamber."

Janu's mouth stayed open for a moment, which had not happened in his life.

"Rice," she said.

"And plantains. A good many of them. Set it on the low table, where it can be seen from the bed." He took the water pot. His voice was perfectly level. "I'm hungry."

"You," said Janu. "Hungry."

"Is that so strange?"

"It is the first time since Asha." She put a hand flat on her chest as if to hold something in. Her eyes were suddenly wet. "The wedding. I said it would do him good. Didn't I say? Rice and plantains, and a little jaggery, and I'll find the good ghee."

"Thank you."

"Hunger," she said, almost to herself, and went down the corridor with her shawl flapping, already shouting for Chinna.

Devan stood with the water in his hands and the door shut at his back.

He had never lied to Janu. He had not known he could do it so well. He thought of the woman asleep behind the door, and of what he would say when she asked him something he could not answer.

# Chapter 3: Closed Shutters

Her hands would not hold still.

Neela woke on her side with the sheet at her chin and a hollow where her stomach should be, as if someone had scooped her out with a spoon. Weak tea light leaked through the shutters. The left side of the bed was empty. She held her fingers up in front of her face and watched them tremble.

The morning after. Eleven years of it, and her body still treated each one like news.

She sat up too fast. The room tilted, righted itself, and showed her the low table by the wall. A banana leaf lay on it, heaped with rice, a stack of plantains, a dark knob of jaggery, and a spoon of ghee going gold at the edges. A brass pot of water stood beside it, beaded with cold.

Janu, she thought. The old woman had stared at her plate all through the feast.

Neela meant to cross the room like a person with all day. She got there in four steps and went down on her knees. The rice was hot. She ate with her right hand, fast, ghee running to her wrist, and did not taste a grain of it. At the Resin House the orphans had eaten in rows, and the child who slowed was the child who got less. Nobody had told her that eating was a weak thing to be caught at. Nobody had needed to.

The door opened.

Janu stood there with a basket on her hip. Neela's hand stopped halfway to her mouth, and her face went flat and polite. It was a useful trick. It did nothing for the rice on her chin.

"Thank you for this," she said, when she could. "It was kind."

Janu looked at her for a long moment. Her mouth opened, then shut. "Well," she said. "Somebody had to feed you." She set down a second leaf, and a third plantain, and did not say another word about it. "Wash. Then I'll show you the fort. A warden's wife who can't find the privy is no use to anyone."

Neela washed. Her hands steadied somewhere around the second mouthful of plantain. She counted it as a small debt owed, and filed it where she kept the others.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


The tour began at the pepper terraces, which Janu introduced as if they were relatives.

They climbed in steps up the inner slope, each terrace a ledge of red earth walled in laterite, and every ledge was green. Vines wound up their stakes with the berries hanging in tight strings, unripe and hard. The smell of crushed leaf was sharp enough to sting. A small boy sat on the third wall with a stick across his knees and six goats arranged around him like a council.

"Kannan," Neela said.

He nearly fell off the wall. "You know my name?"

"You carried the garland." She held out her hand. "Is the white one yours?"

"She's Pepper. She bites."

"Then I'll stand back."

Janu made a noise like a kettle. "Don't encourage him. He's learned two new words since the wedding and both of them are about you."

Neela looked at the boy and the goats and the walls, and kept walking. She kept count as she went. Gaps in the laterite where a person could drop from one terrace to the next. A drain big enough for a child. Two guards on the upper wall who passed each other at the far end every hundred and ten steps, and turned on the same foot.

The cistern stood at the heart of the fort, a great iron-banded tank under a tiled roof, with a wheel and a chain thick as her forearm. A bent man in a faded mundu heaved a lever as they came up. The chain went over the drum and the sound hit the courtyard like a dropped anvil, and every pigeon in the fort went up at once.

"That's the hour," Janu shouted over it. "Gopan keeps it. Nineteen years, and he hasn't missed one."

"Not once?" Neela said.

"Once," said Gopan, with dignity. "In the fever year."

She learned the day watch changed on the sixth clank after dawn. She learned Gopan's knees hurt in the damp. She learned the sergeant on the gate was called Bhaskaran, and the thin guard at his side was Unni, and that Unni's mother sold salt fish in the lower village and had opinions about wardens.

The kalari lay below the east wall, a sunken pit with its lip worn smooth by sitting. Sesame oil lamps stood in niches around the rim, unlit now, their wicks black. The red earth inside had been swept in long arcs. In the middle was one set of bare footprints, sharp as a seal in wax.

Only one.

"Every day before light," Janu said. She was looking at the pit, not at Neela. "Eight years. He used to have a partner. Now he has the earth." She sniffed. "I stopped asking him."

Neela stood at the lip and looked at that single trail of steps. It went round and round. It never crossed itself. A man who drilled like that, alone, with no one at his back, had decided something about his hands.

That was worth knowing. It was only worth knowing.

The padippura came last, the roofed gatehouse with its great double door and its four guards in a row. Neela said all four names. Captain Muthu came ambling out from the guard room with a laugh already in his mouth.

"The lady knows my men better than I do," he boomed. "I'll have to ask her their mothers' names."

"Unni's sells fish," Neela said.

Muthu's laugh broke in the middle. Then it came back, bigger than before. "Janu, she's going to run this fort."

"Pity the fort," said Janu, and meant it kindly.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


She found him in the afternoon on the highest terrace, with his back to her and a handful of green berries in his palm.

Janu had been summoned by a scream from the kitchen. Neela had said she'd find her own way down, which was a lie of the plainest sort. She'd come up here because from the top terrace you could see the whole road to the pass.

"You walk quietly," Devan said, without turning.

"You listen for it."

"I do." He turned the berries over. "These are early. The vines think the rain is close."

"Is it?"

"Forty days, give or take."

She came up beside him. The wind off the pass pushed at her kasavu and she let it. Below them the road fell away in loops, red between the green, and far down a cart the size of a seed was creeping toward the gate.

"May I ask you things?" she said.

"You may ask me anything."

"That's not what I said."

"No." He glanced at her sidelong. "It's what I meant."

All right, she thought. Let's see.

"Did you sleep well?"

"No. Not much."

"Why not?"

"I sleep badly. I told you."

"Did I wake you?"

"You didn't wake me."

It was flat and plain, and she turned it over and found no seam. Her pulse settled. "What do you eat for breakfast?"

"Rice. Whatever Janu sends." A pause. "More, lately."

"Do you like me?"

That got her a longer look. "I don't know you yet."

"Do you trust me?"

"No."

She laughed. It came out of her before she could check it. "You could have said yes."

"It wouldn't have been true."

"People say untrue things to wives all the time."

"Do they?"

"It's most of what a wife hears."

"I wouldn't know." He shook the berries off his palm into the earth. "I don't say them. It saves effort."

She decided to push. "Say the sky is green."

"The sky is blue."

"It's a game. Say it."

"No."

"Say you're glad of me."

He considered the question as if it were a stone he'd been handed. His fingers worked at the empty air, square hands with old white nicks across the knuckles, slow and careful, as if even a question deserved to be held properly. Neela caught herself watching them. She took a step back, and the wind filled the space where she had been.

"I'm not sorry," he said. "I can say that."

"Say it was the council's idea and you hated it."

"It was the council's idea."

"And the rest?"

"I won't answer that."

"Then I'll ask the one that matters." She kept her voice light. "Why did your council choose me?"

The wind moved the vines. He stood very still, and for the first time since she'd met him his face showed effort.

"I won't answer that," he said.

"You could lie."

"I could."

"Nobody would know."

"I would." He said it without weight, the way another man might say he'd had breakfast. "Ask me something else."

She looked at the cart on the road. It had not moved at all, as far as she could tell.

She had known men with power over her. Chathu, soft and patient, who lied the way he breathed and gave every lie a column of figures to hide in. The masters before him. The buyers she'd never met. Every one of them told her what was convenient, and she had learned to read them by the hole it left. This man left no hole. He told her the sky was blue and that he didn't trust her, and when he couldn't tell her, he put up a plain wall and stood in front of it.

That should have comforted her. A man with no cunning had nothing to hide behind. Instead it made her careful. A man who would not lie was a man who would notice hers.

"Tell Janu thank you," she said, "for the rice."

"I will," said Devan.

He nodded once, like a man settling a small account, and turned back to the vines. Indifference, she decided. Almost a relief.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


He was closing the shutters when she came back at dusk.

The chamber had gone amber. The sun sat low and swollen over the pass, the sky round it bright as a washed bowl. Devan stood at the west window with one leaf drawn in, and as she stopped in the doorway he pulled the second to. The third slat creaked. He lifted the long teak bar from its hooks on the wall and dropped it into the iron brackets, and the whole window shut like a mouth.

"Rain," he said.

Neela looked past him, at the thin gold line still showing between the slats. "The sky's clear."

"Yes."

"You said rain."

"Rain comes off the pass fast." He rested his hand on the bar a moment, as if checking it was seated. "I like it shut."

Last night there had been no bar across them at all. Tonight there was, and a man who barred a window looked at its bar.

That was all. He did not turn the lamp up. He did not explain. He crossed to the table where the supper leaf lay waiting and sat, and began on his rice with the same slow thoroughness he brought to everything.

A fussy habit, she told herself. Some men kept their boots in pairs. Some counted their coins every night. She would put it with the sword on the floor and the creaking slat: the small private rules of a man who lived alone and slept badly.

But she stayed in the doorway, because the bar was a good one. A hand's width of seasoned teak, seated in iron, and the only way to lift it was from the inside. A leopard could not do that. Outside the door a guard stood all night. If the warden were found dead behind a barred window, the fort would not say *beast*. It would say *who was in the room with him*, and there was only one answer.

The way in she had planned all week had just been shut. The hand that opened it again would be hers.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


She counted his breath again.

One to ten, steady. Forty, slowing. A hundred. At two hundred and twelve he caught, that same hitch like a bucket knocking a well wall, and she let it pass. A man with a habit. She was starting to collect those.

At three hundred she gave him thirty more. Then she slid out from the sheet and stepped over the sword.

The bar was heavier than it looked. She took it in both hands and eased it up out of the brackets an inch at a time, and set it down against the wall on the side where it would not fall. The shutters swung under her fingers. The third slat cracked.

She froze with her hand on the wood and counted twenty. His breathing went on, long and even.

Out on the ledge she knelt in the cold dark and thought it through, the way she thought through any job. Last night nothing had barred the window, and nothing was missing from it when he woke. Tonight he had barred it himself, and he would look at it in the morning, as a man looks at anything he has set with his own hands.

If she left it lifted, he would see. And if he saw, he would ask. A man like this would ask, and he would listen to what she said, and he would know exactly what the answer weighed.

So it had to go back before he woke. Not from the ledge. From inside, with her hands, as a woman, in the grey, with the shaking still in her arms.

She drew out the flask, and put the bangle on the sill, and did what she did.

The burn took her. The wind rushed in, loaded with a thousand smells. She did not think in words after that. The other one ran along the ledge, dropped to the terrace wall, and went along the stone as if it were a road. She learned the pairs on the wall and how they walked. She learned the third clank after the middle watch, and what a dog smelled like through a kennel door, and that the yard below the padippura lay open and pale. Somewhere in the hours she learned that the chamber door had a guard in front of it and the window had a bar behind it, and that between the two no kill by beast had room to stand.

She did not know which of them was the problem, the man or the bar. She would work that out in daylight.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


The chain clanked the last hour, and the sky went grey behind the pass.

She came back along the ledge with her legs like wet rope, and the turning went out of her in a long ragged shudder that left her on her knees on the stone. The hollow was back. It was worse than yesterday, because tonight a task waited at the end of it.

She put the bangle on. She wrapped the kasavu round herself with fingers that kept missing. Then she stood, which took two tries, and drew the shutters in behind her one at a time.

The room was dim and smelled of sesame oil and sleep. Devan lay on his left side with his back to her and one hand open on the sheet, breathing slow. Rice sat on the low table, a fresh leaf, covered by a cloth. Neela did not look at it. She would be sick if she looked at it.

She lifted the bar. It weighed what it had weighed before, and her arms had less in them. She set the end in the left bracket first, then walked it round to the right, slowly, a finger's breadth at a time, because the iron was cold and the wood would knock if it went wrong.

The bar slid home.

The third slat answered it. A dry crack, sharp as a knuckle, loud in the quiet.

Neela stayed where she was, her palm flat on the teak and her breath held behind her teeth. She did not turn. She counted.

At nineteen she gave up, and looked.

He lay exactly as he had lain. The sheet rose and fell, rose and fell. His hand was open on the cloth. Nothing in his back had changed.

But she had not been watching his back when the slat cracked, and she could not tell whether the breathing she heard now was the breathing from before.

# Chapter 4: Kennelled

"They've shut the hounds up again," Kannan said.

He said it to the goat. Pepper went on chewing and watched Neela with one yellow eye, like a small judge.

Neela had climbed to the second terrace with a bowl of Janu's rice. The hollow in her belly would not let her sit still. Besides, a goat-boy on a wall saw everything that moved in a fort. She sat on the laterite beside him and held out the bowl. He took a fistful without looking, like a boy who had been fed by a steward all his life.

"Which hounds?" she said.

"All of them. Fourteen." He wiped his hand on his knee. "They sleep in the long kennel behind the stables. Can you hear them? Every night since your wedding. They howl till the middle watch, and my loft is right over that wall."

"They didn't howl before?"

"They weren't *in* before. They ran the yard from dusk to dawn. Since forever. Nobody crossed it in the dark unless the hounds knew his smell." He scowled. "Then the warden says, kennel them. Chinna asked why. He said, 'I like it quiet.' Quiet! In a fort!"

"Maybe he sleeps badly."

"Janu says he's slept badly eight years, and the hounds ran anyway."

Neela ate the rest of the rice slowly. Her face stayed polite. Behind it she was already counting.

She had checked the bar first thing that morning, before Janu's rice, before anything. It sat in its brackets, seated as she had left it. Devan had come in from the pit and said one thing about the night. "The third slat creaks," he'd said, to the shutters. "I've never fixed it."

That was all. It was a fact about wood. It might also have been a question, or a door held open for her to walk through. She had said, "It's an old house," and he had said, "Yes," and gone to wash. She still could not read it.

That was the first piece. The second was the yard.

She had crossed it last night. Sixty paces of open ground between the padippura and the stables, pale as a threshing floor, and nothing in it. She had noted it the way she noted everything, and had been glad. She had not asked why. Now a nine-year-old with a goat had told her it had never been empty before.

So. A gift, or a trap.

A gift, because a man who barred his window against clear sky was a man of small, odd rules. Perhaps he truly wanted quiet. Perhaps he was a husband with a new wife and a poor night's sleep, and the hounds were one more noise. Careless men did leave doors open. She had walked through a hundred of them.

But this man wasn't careless. She had watched him pick up berries one at a time. A warden with a bride the council had chosen, and tokens leaking through his pass, might very well empty a yard on purpose. Then he would put men on the wall to see who walked into it. And a slat that he had named aloud, the morning after...

She could argue it either way until the chain clanked noon. She tried. It came out even.

Both readings ended in the same place, which was in the yard, with her eyes open.

"Are you going to eat that?" Kannan asked, looking at the bowl.

"No," she said, and gave it to the goat.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


The hooves came an hour later.

Gopan hauled his lever, the chain went over the drum, and a patrol clattered through the padippura in a spray of red mud. Seven riders. Neela was at the hall door before she'd decided to be, with Janu hissing behind her about wives who ought to be sitting down.

The hall was long and cool, with pillars of black teak and a floor of polished red earth. The riders came in with their boots still dripping. The sergeant at the front was Bhaskaran, who held the gate and had ridden out at dawn when a herdsman swore he'd seen men at the second ford. He had a satchel in his fist, stiff with dried blood, and he slammed it onto the long table hard enough to jump the lamps.

"Raider, sir. At the ford. One of his friends put an arrow in him, and we found what he was carrying."

Devan had come in from the east door. He didn't hurry. He took the satchel by its strap, turned it out, and picked through what fell: a knife, a twist of dried fish, a coil of cord. Then a flat tally the size of a palm, strung on a leather loop and capped with a thumb of dull red wax.

Captain Muthu had come up beside him, big and easy, with his arms folded. "Another one?"

"Another one."

Devan held it up. The hall went quiet in a way she knew from the Resin House, the quiet of people waiting to be told how bad it was. He set his thumbnail under the wax and pressed. It split cleanly into two halves. He turned each to the window.

"The wax is genuine," he said. "Council wax. Council seal, pressed from the true ring."

Somewhere behind her a lamp wick spat.

"It isn't copied," he said, in the same flat voice. "I'd know. This was made in the council house, by someone with the right to make it."

Then the hall broke open.

"A clerk," said Muthu, loudly. "Some wax-house clerk with a bad debt and a wife."

"A Thurai spy in the council," said a young guard near the door. "They've got them everywhere."

"A lord," said Bhaskaran. "It's always a lord. A man who never has to stand at the gate and look into a carter's face." His voice cracked on *face*.

Neela, behind her pillar, made her own wager. She didn't believe in clerks. A clerk made a mark; a clerk could be caught making it. This had a hand behind it that nobody searched. She watched who said nothing, and it was the warden.

Bhaskaran saw it too. "The tally number," he said, and his voice dropped, and that was worse. "Sir. I know this number. It's a cart I passed. New moon, a salt-and-cloth cart from the coast. I looked at the wax. I *looked* at it, and I waved them through. I'll ride to Kodiyur myself and open the whole council with my sword."

"Bhaskaran," Devan said.

"Sir, I will."

"Sit."

Bhaskaran didn't. Two of his men had already taken a half step toward him. He was a big man, red in the face, and his hand had found his hilt. Neela slid her weight onto the balls of her feet without meaning to.

Devan didn't raise his voice. He came around the table and stopped an arm's length from the sergeant. He put one palm flat on the tabletop, where Bhaskaran could see it, empty.

"You looked at the wax," he said. "It was true wax. You were meant to pass it. That's what the wax is for."

"Sir..."

"If you'd turned that cart away, I'd have asked you why. You'd have said it looked wrong. It didn't look wrong. It was perfect." He waited. "That's the whole trouble. Do you see it?"

The sergeant's chest went in and out. "Yes, sir."

"I need every tally that has passed your gate since the last rains. Numbers, carts, faces, whatever you remember. You're the only man who can give me that. Will you?"

The hand came off the hilt. "Yes."

"Then sit and eat first. You smell of the ford."

A thin laugh went round. Bhaskaran dropped onto the bench like a sack set down at the end of a long road, and Janu was already there with a cup. The whole thing had taken less time than it takes to boil water.

Neela's pulse had gone quiet, the way it did when a fight ended before it began. She had watched men handle men at the Resin House, but they used the rod, or the ledger, or the threat of both. This one had used the truth and a free hand.

Her own hand lay against the black teak of the pillar, palm flat, fingers open and empty.

She looked at it. It was his hand, the one he'd laid on the table for Bhaskaran, and she had made it without being told. She took it off the wood and tucked it into the fold of her kasavu.

"Neela."

She started. He was looking straight at her across the length of the hall, and so was everyone else.

"Your house trades pepper under these tallies. Come and tell me if this wax is what it looks like."

It was a test, or it was courtesy, and his face didn't say which. She crossed the red floor with the polite smile on and took the two halves from his palm. They were warm from his hand.

Dull brick red. A pressed mark of a pepper sprig, crisp at every leaf. She raised one half to her nose, because a Chirayil ward would, and sweet clove rose off it, faint and green.

"It's new," she said. "A month old, not more. The wax-house pours with clove oil so it doesn't crack in the damp. It's still in the wax." She turned the half over. "And it's good work. Better than good."

"That's what I thought," Devan said.

He took the halves back. For a moment his fingers were against her palm, dry and rough at the pads. Then they weren't. She went back to her pillar with her hand held very still.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


She caught him on the outer stair, where the hall's shadow ended and the court began, and fell into step beside him as if she had been going the same way.

"Your sergeant thinks it was his fault."

"He thinks wrongly."

"You told him so."

"I told him the truth. It's easier than arguing." He didn't slow. "Your nose is good."

"My guardian taught me to judge a pepper by smell before I could read." That was a line she had learned in the Resin House, with the rest of Chirayil. It came out smooth.

"Useful."

They walked a few steps. Below them the court was full of men leading in tired horses. She let a pause go by and then asked it plainly, as a wife might.

"Who sells them?"

He glanced at her. It wasn't suspicion. He looked like a man who has been asked the weather and is surprised anyone cares.

"Someone on the queen's council."

"Which one?"

"If I knew," Devan said, "he'd be in a cell."

She waited for the rest. For the turn: *and that's why you're here*, or *and I've reason to think you can help*. It didn't come. He was looking down at the horses.

"The tokens go to Thurai raiders," he said. "So far they burn grain stores and a mill or two. Soon it will be the villages on the coast side. Your house's carts will want the pass open in harvest. I mean to see it stays that way."

"That's all?"

"It's a great deal." There was dry humour in it, very low. "I would sleep better."

She had come out of the hall braced for her own name. A husband holding a hostile bride might circle the subject of her for weeks; she had planned for that. Instead he had told her his hunt in six sentences, and her name wasn't in any of them. He wasn't hunting her. He was hunting somebody else, and he wanted him badly, and it had never crossed his mind to use her as a lever.

That made him easier to listen to, which was a bad sign.

"I hope you find him," she said, and was startled to find she meant something by it.

"So do I," said Devan, and went down into the court.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


He barred the shutters at dusk. "Rain," he said again, and again the sky was washed clean.

She said nothing. She watched his back and set the two things side by side.

Everything else he told her fit the world. The wax was true, the sky was blue, the cell was waiting for a man he couldn't name. When he couldn't answer, he said so and kept his hands where she could see them. Only here did he give her a word that answered nothing. Rain, every night, at this window, under a sky with no cloud in it.

Hounds shut up for a bride. Shutters shut for weather. Both were about the dark, and the dark was hers.

She did not know what it meant. A man who held back one thing so plainly had a reason, and she did not like that she wanted to know it. She ate her rice, counted his breath to three hundred, and stepped over the sword.

The bar came up in her hands. The third slat cracked. She counted twenty and he gave her nothing to hear.

The burn. The bright rush of smells. The other one went down the wall on silent claws.

The yard lay open below.

It did not hurry. It took the terrace face in three drops and came to the corner of the stables, where the kennel smell rolled out thick: dog, straw, wet fur, fourteen angry noses turned toward it through the planks. They knew it was there. They could do nothing about it. One began to whine and the others took it up, a low grumble, and a guard at the far wall turned his head, and turned it back again. Dogs in a kennel at night were only dogs.

The yard stretched out silver and flat. The leopard crossed it low and slow.

It learned the padippura from the shadow of the water trough. Four men, and one with his chin on his chest. It went past the open end of the guard room close enough to touch the doorpost. It learned the kitchen door, where Chinna snored on her mat, and the granary, whose lock was a farm lock, and the well. It learned that the pairs on the wall met at the same stone and turned the same foot. Two men stood the west stair, as Devan had said. There was a gap, a count of thirty, under the far wall when the lower pair swung inward.

The cistern chain went. The third clank after the middle watch, and the pairs changed, and the yard took no notice of it at all.

This was the easiest fort it had ever walked. That was what lifted the fur along its spine. Nothing had stopped it. In all its life nothing had left it alone without wanting a price.

It circled back, past the cold stones of the kalari rim, toward the west wall. At the corner the wall pair parted. One man went on along the far walk with his lantern. The other turned back and paced the short stretch of rampart above the warden's window, alone, up and down, up and down. It went flat in the shadow and let him pass.

Then it lifted its head and looked up.

The window was a dark square in the laterite, one shutter leaf drawn back where it had left it. Nothing showed through the gap, but the smell of him came down the stone: sesame oil, old iron, sleep. And on the still air, the hitch in his breath.

It gathered itself. The wall was thirty feet, an easy climb. The ledge was wide. The sleeper was close enough that it could count that breath.

And above the ledge the sentry stopped. He leaned on the parapet and looked out at nothing, and his boots were two spans over its head. When he moved, the lantern light swung over the ledge like a hand. A shape that went in at that window would be seen going. It would be shouted at before the teeth closed, and the whole wall would run. A beast out of the dark was a thing the fort could blame on the hills. A beast that walked into the warden's room under a sentry's eye was a hunt, and a sealed gate, and a skin on a pole.

The sentry coughed and went on. The moment stayed, wide open, and so did the window.

There was a cheaper way. She could wait a night, until the post above was bare, and take the throat in the quiet. The pass left thirty-seven nights, and she would be spending one of them on nothing.

But the other one did not weigh nights. It weighed an open palm laid flat on a table where a furious man could see it, and a voice that never rose. *Who is he hunting?* It had no words for the question. There was only the shape of the wanting, and the long cold climb that did not begin.

For the first time since the wedding she did not know the answer she had come to give: whether this man died by her hand.

It turned from the window and went down into the shadow of the wall. It lay under the water trough until the sentry's boots found their old beat, and watched the dark square, and waited out the night.

The sky began to grey behind the pass. The chain clanked the last hour.

Above her, the shutter moved.

Not a crack. Both leaves swung back against the laterite, wide, and stayed there. No lamp. No face. Only an open window thirty feet up, where she had left one leaf ajar, and the black square waiting like a held door.

The first burn of the dawn turning ran up her legs. Someone had opened it for her. Or for whatever came to the sill next.

# Chapter 5: The Man Who Doesn't Spar

She had come back shaking again.

Devan had lain with his eyes shut and listened to it. At the grey of dawn he had thrown both shutter leaves wide and stood a moment at the empty sill. Down in the yard the shadow under the water trough lay thicker than a shadow should. When he looked again it was gone. She had not come to the sill. She had turned back in the yard.

Some time after first light he heard the stair door, then bare feet, then rough wool dropping to the floor. A saddle-blanket from the stable, by the smell of horse. Then the long breath of a woman who had run all night and was trying not to show it. A hand missed the bed-post twice before it found it. The bed creaked.

He had opened the shutters because a woman who had spent the night somewhere in his yard ought to see one way back that was not guarded and not a trap.

He counted her breaths until she slept. Then he went out to the kalari, because that was what he did, and a man who did something else on the fourth morning of a marriage would be noticed.

Three nights. She had gone out each one, and each one she had come back. Each time she had come to the bed shaking and lain with her back to him, as far off as the mattress allowed.

He did not know what that meant. He lit the sesame lamps around the rim one at a time and let the question sit in his chest.

The first form was slow. Feet in the red earth, weight low, the long staff turning over his shoulder. The second was faster. By the fifth the earth was spitting under his heels and the staff made a sound like cloth tearing. He drilled the way he had for eight years, against nobody. Every sequence ended in a strike, and every strike stopped a hand's width from the post. He had never once let it land. His arm knew exactly where to stop.

"Warden!"

Muthu came down the pit wall in two jumps with a practice staff in each fist, grinning. Red dust puffed under his boots.

"One round," he said. "One. I'll let you win. I'll let you win so loudly Gopan will ring the chain."

"No."

"I'll fight left-handed."

"No."

"I'll fight asleep." Muthu spun a staff and caught it. "Eight years, sir. I've counted. Every week I ask, and every week you say no, and it's the steadiest thing in Kettamala. The walls leak. The pass closes. But my warden says no at dawn on the seventh day, like the cistern chain on the hour."

"Eight years and a week."

"Is that all? I thought it was longer." Muthu laughed, the big laugh that made the pigeons go up. "At this rate I'll ask from my pyre. Burn me, Devan, I'll say, one round first. And you'll say no, and the whole fort will weep."

It was a good joke. It was the same joke, dressed a little differently each week. Devan watched Muthu's hand on the staff. The knuckles had gone pale, and he held the grip long after the laugh ended.

Muthu had been his partner in this pit once. He had taken every bruise Devan gave him and asked for more. Then one day there had been a captain on his knees with his hands open, and a strike, and a silence. Devan had put down his staff and not lifted it against a living man since.

He couldn't give Muthu the reason he had stopped. Muthu had been there. He knew what had happened. He had never been told what it had done to Devan.

"Put the staffs on the rack," he said.

"Sir."

"And thank you for asking."

Muthu's face did something quick and unguarded. Then the grin came back over it like a lid. "Next week," he said, and went up the wall, whistling a little off key.

Devan stood in the empty pit with the second lamp guttering and finished the form. The strike stopped a hand's width from the post.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


After the day watch changed, he took her out to the terraces, because a warden walking his wife through his pepper was the most natural thing in the world. It also kept her where he could see her hands.

She wore a day kasavu from her trousseau and the gold bangle. She took the steps up the slope without looking at them. A woman who counted her feet, he thought, and pushed the thought away, because he had decided to look at her as the Chirayil ward the council had sent.

"Tell me what you see," he said, at the second terrace.

She didn't hesitate. She picked a string of berries and rolled one between finger and thumb. "Early. The vines are confused. They've set fruit for a rain that's still a month off." She pressed the berry with a nail. "Hard. Good. These will dry heavy. Drop a handful in water and the sinkers are the ones worth the weight." She looked along the row. "You've let the east side go too dense. They'll mould if the monsoon comes early."

"Janu said the same."

"Then Janu's right. Is she often?"

"Always. It's tiresome." He walked on. "Your guardian. What was his name?"

"Kunjan Chirayil." She said it smoothly. "He kept the north warehouse in Kodiyur. Do you know it?"

"No."

"You'd smell it from the water. Wet sacks, and cloves from the Thurai side. He'd stand in the door and tell you the grade of a cargo with his eyes shut." A small shrug. "It's what he was good at."

Devan had read the council's papers on the Chirayil house three times, and nothing in them was false. Kunjan Chirayil was real. The warehouse was real. She spoke of both the way a person speaks of a place they have been taught.

"Were you fond of him?"

"Were you fond of yours?"

"My father?" He considered it honestly. "I respected him. Fondness came later, after he was dead and couldn't object."

"Did that make it easier?"

"No. Did it make it easier for you? With Kunjan?"

"Do you miss the hills?" she said. "Janu says you were away once."

"Two years, at the border school. Where did you learn your letters?"

"Where did you learn to be so patient?"

A cart creaked somewhere below. He nodded as though she had given him a fair answer, and filed it with the others. Ask her what a pepper weighs and she would tell him to the grain. Ask her where she slept as a child, and a question came back at him, light and quick and always shaped like a door closing.

It was neat work. It was also the first time all morning she had sounded like herself.

She reached up to pull a runner free of its stake, and the gold bangle slid back along her wrist.

Under it, the skin was pale and ridged, in rows, like a field the plough had gone over too many times. He knew the pattern. He had smelled a sealed jar of the stuff at twenty, in a classroom, while a lean teacher described exactly what it did to the skin of those who used it. He had seen these scars by lamplight on the wedding night. He had not seen them in sun.

In sun they told him something new. The ridges lay thick, layer over layer, one scar healed into the next. That was not a season's work. That was more than a decade of it, and nobody burned herself that many times who had begun as a grown woman. She had started young. Whoever had put her to this had done it while she was still a child.

Her eyes caught his, and the bangle slid down in one smooth motion.

"A childhood burn," she said, lightly. "I was clumsy."

It was no child's burn, and they both knew it. He said, "The stakes want mending on this row," and went to look at them.

Behind him the bangle clicked against a stake. He didn't turn around.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


The yelp came from above them, and then a boy's furious wail.

Kannan sat on the third wall with his right hand clamped in his left, and the white goat stood beside him, looking mildly satisfied. Blood ran between the boy's fingers and dripped off his wrist onto the laterite.

"She bit me!"

"She does that," Devan said. "It's in the name."

Neela was already there. She hadn't run, exactly, but she had crossed the terrace in the time it took him to start. She knelt, took the boy's wrist, and turned the hand to the light.

"It's clean," she said. "A good bite is cleaner than a bad scratch. Hold still." She looked up. "Warden, there's a water pot on the wall."

He brought it. She rinsed the wound under the pot, steady, murmuring to the boy about nothing. When it ran clear she pulled the gold-bordered edge of her kasavu through her fingers and tore a strip off it in one motion. The cloth was trousseau cloth, gold thread and all, worth a month of the boy's keep.

She bound the hand with quick, firm turns and tied it off.

"Tell Bindu to wash it again tonight with boiled water," she said. "And to sing while she does it. She has a good voice and you'll forget the sting."

Kannan stared at her. "How do you know my mother sings?"

"The women at the well were talking about it this morning." She tapped his nose. "Keep Pepper off the vines. She's eating the best of them."

"She isn't," said the boy, who was nine, and incapable of lying about a goat. "Well. Only the low ones."

Devan stood with the water pot in his hand and did not move.

Asha had done this. She had come to the fort at nineteen, and in her first month she had gone through the recruits with a slate and a bit of chalk, writing down names and villages and mothers. Everybody's. He had teased her. *You'll be the only second in the border guard who can address a conscript's grandmother.* She had looked at him, grinning, chalk on her chin. *They'll fight better if someone knows who to write to.*

He set the water pot on the wall. He said, "I need a moment," and walked down the terrace steps without looking back.

It was true, and it was all he would say. The wall by the cistern was near enough that he could stand there, palm flat on the cold laterite, until the chain went and covered the sound of his own breathing.

An instrument did not tear a wedding-gift cloth for a goat-boy.

He had been so sure what she was. The thing that had been sent. The thing like the one that had come down his west stair on a night six years gone, and taken his sister apart with a bear's claws, and left her there for Janu to find in the morning.

He did not know what to do with a person.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


Supper was rice and a dark fish curry, and Janu served it herself and sat down at the end of the table to scold them while they ate. Muthu had come in from the gate and taken the bench opposite, heaping his plate. The hall smelled of tamarind and lamp oil.

"The old woman at the lower ford says there's a cat on the hill," Janu said.

"A cat?" said Muthu, with his mouth full.

"A big one. She heard it cough in the pala grove." Janu pulled her shawl straight, enjoying herself. "It reminded me. When I was a girl, a leopard came down to the village below my mother's house. Every night it took something. First a hen, then a kid. Then the headman's calf, and it left the bell, mind you, neatly, on the doorstep, as if to say it had no use for the noise. The men sat up with spears for a week and never saw it once. They said it knew which house it wanted. It was patient. It waited till the lamps were out, and—"

Devan was not looking at Janu.

Across the table, Neela held her cup with both hands. She had been drinking water, steadily, in small sips. Now her hands were round it as if she were warming them, and the knuckles had gone white as the rim.

Her face hadn't changed. It was the pleasant, attentive face of a bride listening to an old woman's story. But the cup trembled by the width of a hair, and she pressed it to the table to stop it.

He knew that stillness. He had seen it a hundred times on the faces of recruits who were told the thing they feared was coming.

*Janu, enough.* The words were in his mouth. She would have stopped at once, and sniffed, and looked at him with the hurt he had never been able to bear.

He did not say them.

Neela did not know he had seen her turn. It was the one thing he held that she did not. A warden who cut off a leopard story for his bride's sake would be telling her, as plainly as speech, that he had joined the two in his head. She would know it by morning. Every night after, she would run with that knowledge at her back.

So he sat, and let the old woman tell it, and watched the white knuckles, and ate rice he could not taste. He wanted to stop it for her. He didn't.

"Is it true?" Neela said, when Janu ended. Her voice was light. "Did you see it?"

"Heavens, no. I was a girl in bed with the shutters closed." Janu patted her hand. "Don't mind me. It's a story."

"I like stories," said Neela, and set down the cup.

She excused herself soon after. Devan watched her cross the hall, back straight, steps even, and counted the times she looked at the doors. Two. The west door and the east.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


He let Muthu finish his meal. Then he walked him out under the arch of the hall, where the torchlight did not reach the corner and the pigeons had gone to roost.

"Captain."

"Sir."

"Take the night man off the west wall. The one who walks the rampart above my window."

Muthu's chewing stopped. "Off it? That's the closest stretch to your chamber, sir."

"I know."

"The raiders have been at the ford. Sir, with respect, if any wall wants a man on it—"

"I know," Devan said again. "Pull him."

Muthu looked at him in the dark. The cheerfulness was gone for a moment, as if something had been set down. "Any reason I should give the sergeant?"

"I won't answer that."

A pause. Then the big shoulders came down, and Muthu clapped him once on the arm, like a man shaking off a thought. "Then I'll tell him you've got a bad stomach and want the quiet," he said. "That'll keep the barracks busy till harvest."

"Tell him whatever you like."

"Sir." Muthu hesitated. "You'd say, wouldn't you. If it was bad."

"If I could."

It was the nearest thing to a lie he allowed himself, and it was not one. He watched Muthu go down the steps toward the barracks, and then he climbed to the chamber.

Neela was not in it yet. The bed was made. The sword lay on the floor on her side, hilt toward the pillow, plain wire grip worn at the thumb. He barred the shutters, as he did at dusk, and stood looking at the teak.

His first reason was a plain one. Whoever had sent her would want a report, and a leopard that ran the hills left marks. Each dawn he could read the wet laterite by the trough, see where the paws came from and where they went, and learn who she ran to.

The truer reason was the rule. A man on the rampart two spans above that ledge, with a lantern in his fist, could watch her turn. He would be her witness from that moment, with the word in his mouth for the rest of his life. Devan had decided never to use what he knew. He could not decide that for a sentry.

Then he lay down with his back to the door and shut his eyes.

She came in late, in her day cloth, and said nothing. She lay down at the far edge. He counted her breaths, slow and careful, a woman pretending, until at last she rose. The bar lifted from its brackets. The third slat cracked.

He did not move. He kept his breath even, with its small hitch.

Then the cold thought came, too late to take back. With the rampart bare there was no one up there to shout. There would also be no one to swear, if the worst came, what had or had not passed in this room. If she turned from the hill toward the bed, nobody on the wall would hear a thing.

Soft as a dropped cloth, her weight landed on the ledge.

# Chapter 6: Green Wax

Janu held the sandalwood box out at arm's length, the way she held a fish she didn't trust.

"The queen's own wax," she said. "I've never seen it so close."

It sat in the middle of the storeroom floor between a rice sack and a chest of Chirayil linens. Neela had known what it was from the doorway. Everything else the wedding had brought was brown paper and string. This had a thumb of green wax over a knot of red cord, pressed with a small bird, wings spread.

"A rider brought it before noon," Janu said. "Sat in my hall and ate for three. Sudhi, he said his name was. I wanted to hit him with a ladle."

"Sudhi," said Neela, and put it away with the rest. Maniyamma and Thankappan, who had carried the chests. The two Chirayil cousins who had sent the bolt of yellow cloth and not come. She had learned them all while Janu talked. It was a bad habit for a woman who was meant to pass through a house like water. She had never been able to stop.

"Open the lamp first," Janu said. "It's the only one from your own people, and you've been polite to everything else."

The lamp came out of its straw heavy and cold, a standing lamp of brass with a bird worked into the stem and five cups for oil. Someone had paid for it and chosen it, and wrapped it so it would not scratch. It was part of her family. She had no family. A stranger in the Chirayil house had sat down and thought, what would a ward carry to her husband's fort? And had decided on a bird.

She rubbed a thumbprint off the rim with her sleeve.

"Handsome," said Janu, grudging. "Put it by the window. It'll catch the evening."

The warden's step sounded in the passage. He didn't knock. He stopped in the doorway and looked at the box before he looked at either of them.

"You sent for me," he said.

"I sent Kannan to tell you there was a box," said Janu. "From the palace. I thought you'd want to cut it yourself."

"I do." He crossed the floor, crouched, and drew his belt knife. The red cord parted. He slid the blade under the green wax and lifted, and the whole seal came away in one piece, the bird still crisp on its face.

Inside, on a bed of dry cloth, lay a betel set in silver. A tray, a lidded box for lime paste, an areca cutter with a sunbird for a handle. Janu made a sound of pure greed.

Devan barely glanced at it. He turned the seal over on his palm and tilted it to the slit of window. He ran his thumbnail along the underside, then the edge. He held it close to his face and breathed in, once, the way Neela had done with the council's wax in the hall. She had caught the smell herself when Janu set the box down: crushed leaf. Not clove.

He was at it longer than he'd given the silver or the cord, and the cord was cut through.

"Well?" Janu said. "Her Grace's own hand chose it."

"Her Grace's staff chose it."

"It's *lovely*."

"It is." He closed his fingers over the wax. "I'm not going to eat from it."

Neela's mouth moved before she'd checked it. "You don't like the gift."

"I don't trust gifts from the palace."

He said it plainly, to the floor, in the same voice he'd used for the weight of a berry. Janu made a noise like a kettle. "Devan! In this house! Under my roof!"

"I'll say it under hers, if she asks."

Neela knelt very still among the straw. She thought of the Resin House, and of sweets. Before every contract, Chathu sent a tin of them to her room. Jaggery and sesame, soft, still warm from the kitchen. *For luck, child.* She had eaten all eleven tins. Around the fifth, she had learned that being given something was the start of a bill.

"Then we agree," she said.

He looked at her at last. It was a short look, and a surprised one. "Do we?"

"I was raised by a pepper house. Anything given free costs double." She kept her voice light. "Ask any trader."

Something eased at the corner of his mouth. It didn't become a smile. It only seemed to be thinking about one. "I will," he said.

He turned the seal over once more. Then, to her surprise, he held it out across the box, green wax flat on his palm. "What would a trader say it cost?"

She looked at the bird, wings spread, and not at him. "The price of what they'll ask for next."

He nodded once, the way a man nods when a weight matches his own scale. He closed his hand and tucked the wax into his sleeve. "Janu, lock the set in the granary with the hill seed. Nothing from that box goes into our chamber or our mouths."

"A queen's silver in the *granary*—"

"Yes."

Nobody in this fort had asked what she thought of anything that wasn't pepper. She had been asked now, and some small, stupid part of her had wanted to be. She put the feeling with the other things she would have to ignore.

"One more thing," said Janu, who could not let a quiet go by. "Warden. The cat."

"What cat?"

"The one in the pepper store! Two sacks torn open this week, and droppings I could use for a doorstop. I've told the boys to sweep and it comes back. Turn the hounds loose again, a night or two. They'd have it out by morning."

"The hounds stay in."

"Then what am I to do?"

"Leave the cat," said Devan. "It keeps the rats down."

His eyes were on the box lid as he said it. Neela's hand had been resting there, one finger tracing the grain. It stopped.

*The cat.*

Her heart gave one hard knock and went on. She kept her face where it was.

It was Janu's word. Janu had said it three times this week, of the pepper store, and the old woman at the ford had said it, and Janu's story at supper had used it for a creature half a hill away. Everyone in this fort said cat. A man who answered a steward's complaint used the steward's word. Of course he did. And a cat in a pepper store was a cat in a pepper store.

She lifted the finger. The lid closed on its own weight.

"I'll carry the lamp up," she said, "before it's lost under all this."

She avoided his sleeve, where the green wax was. She avoided his face. She took the lamp by its bird and went out into the passage, and counted her steps up the stair to forty, to be sure they were even.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


At dusk the lamp stood at the window with a thin line of evening running down its stem. The shutters were barred. The sword lay on the floor, hilt toward her. The man who owned both was already in the bed with his back to the door, breathing slowly and evenly, which she had learned meant nothing.

She counted him to three hundred. Then she went to the window.

Thirty-five nights. Thirty-five, and tonight she was not going to spend one watching a wall she'd already measured.

The bar. The third slat, a crack like a knuckle. The resin on throat and wrists, black and bitter, with its old burn lighting under the skin, and the rush of smells that came over her in a flood. The other one took the ledge in a single flowing step. It looked up, and the rampart over the window was empty. The rampart had been empty two nights now. Neela had stopped calling it luck. She noted it and pushed it behind her.

The leopard didn't go to the yard this time. The yard was learned. It went along the foot of the west wall and dropped to the first pepper terrace, then the next, and over the lip of the last into the dark of the hill.

The hill was loud with small lives. A hare screamed somewhere and went quiet. The animal's hunger came with the night, and it was not hers, and it ate at her anyway. The leopard passed a hollow where a hare breathed in the grass, a fast and tiny pulse. It let the hare breathe. It was there to work.

*Find the weak hour.* The contract needed it, and she couldn't get it by looking down from a ledge. She went back over what she had. The chain at the cistern, and Gopan, who kept it. The pairs changing on the third clank after the middle watch. Raiu holding the torch in the passage outside the chamber, a boy who nodded off over his own elbow by the end of the night. Chinna snoring at the kitchen door. The cooks who woke when the sky went grey. The men on the wall in the last long hour before first light, when the night pairs had stood longest, with their lanterns burned low and their heads gone heavy.

The last dark hour. Just before first light the whole fort was at its poorest.

The hour had one flaw, and she found it as she ran. The man she was paid to kill woke in that hour. He rose before the light and went to the pit alone, and for as long as he drilled he was awake and armed and in the open air.

She checked herself mid-stride and made herself be honest. She knew it only from Janu's grumbling and from the pit lamps lit below the east wall. She had never once been in the chamber to watch him rise. At that hour she was always out on the hill. It was a thing she had been told, not a thing she had seen.
