She would not take him in the pit. She would take him in the bed, before he woke, or not at all. Which left the hour before the hour. A narrow door, but a door. She would look at it again at dawn, with her own eyes.

She was a good way up the second spur, in the pala grove, when the wind changed.

The scent came down the slope on it, and the leopard stopped dead with one forepaw lifted. Under the green smell of wet leaves, under dung and old rain, there was a sharp thread of musk. Warm. Fresh. Neela knew it the way she knew the dormitory blankets at the Resin House, thin and grey and smelling of one particular child.

*Jackal.*

The leopard went up the slope low and fast. At the top of the spur stood a split slab of laterite, knee high, with a lightning scar through the middle and a pale cup cut in its top. Every meeting stone in the guild was cut with a cup. A mark was left in it, or a thing was left under it, and no one who passed would see anything but a rock. She had been told to look for this one before she left the Resin House. Now she had found it.

Wet at the base, where the pink earth had been splashed. Fresh. The jackal had stood here at dusk, or an hour after, and said her piece in the oldest way a jackal could.

Ammu was close.

She put her face to the mark and breathed it in. Anyone else would call it a jackal's mark on a stone. It warmed her like tea taken hot.

The leopard could not speak. It could not leave a word. It could only press its cheek against the laterite and drag it, once, slowly, from one end of the slab to the other. A leopard's answer, with its own scent laid down over hers.

*I'm here. Come.*

She stayed until the wind moved, then turned for the fort. The grey was a long way off still. She ran the paths home with her head up, and the hunger forgotten.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


She took the terraces at a lope and the wall in three bounds and was crouched on the ledge before the chain rang the last hour of the dark. She hadn't thought of it as the last. Now it was. The sky behind the eastern wall was the colour of a dove's back, and the shutter stood open the width of her shoulders.

The bed was behind her. She could hear him from here. Slow. Level. His breath did the small hitch that it always did, a little stumble a third of the way through each long count. She took it for the sign of a man in the deepest part of sleep.

Still abed, then. At first light, again. She set it beside what Janu said about the pit lamps, and the two did not fit. She would count it again tomorrow, and the night after.

The grey came up. The other one went out of her all at once.

It always hurt, and it always hurt the same. The bones took it first. Then the skin of her wrists and her throat peeled hot and ran cold, and she was a woman crouched naked on a stone sill with her teeth chattering, one hand flat against the wall for balance. The hunger came after, hollow and shaking, scooping her out from the inside.

She breathed. She looked up for the sentry's lantern, and there was none, and in its place sat a cat.

It was on the parapet above her, in the very spot where the boots had stood. A scruffy ginger tom, one ear gone to a notch, his coat in ropes. A dead rat hung from his jaws. He looked down at her with the flat hostile calm of a creature who had held this wall long before she had, and he was covered in pepper. It lay on his paws and whiskers and on the top of his head like flour. He looked at her for a long moment.

Then he sneezed.

It was a tiny sneeze, outraged and very wet, and the rat jumped in his mouth.

The laugh came out of her before she knew it. It came out loud, ringing off the laterite, and it was a real one, not the careful bell of a Chirayil ward. She clapped her hand to her mouth, and the laugh went on behind it, shaking her shoulders, hunger and cold and all.

*Leave the cat. It keeps the rats down.*

That was the cat. That was the whole of it, a pepper-dusted tom with a notched ear. She had stood in a storeroom and let a word go through her like a nail, and it was a ginger ruffian with an opinion about Janu's sacks. She leaned her forehead on the stone and shook with laughing, silently now, tears in her eyes.

Behind her, in the dark of the chamber, the breathing hitched.

She froze, one hand still pressed to her lips, and counted. One, two, three. The breath settled and ran level. A man turning in his sleep.

But the hitch had come on the very beat of her laugh. Not a third of the way through, like every other night. She filed it. A loud sound wakes anyone halfway, she told herself. Sleepers stumble. It was chance. She kept her eyes off the bed.

The tom looked down at her with great contempt. He picked up his rat and walked away along the parapet, his tail kinked at the tip.

"Thank you," she whispered, and climbed in.

She dressed in the dark, quickly, with her teeth shut on the chatter. The cold bangle went on last, over the ridged skin. She lowered the bar into its brackets with both hands. By the time she turned, she had half decided she'd have two helpings of Janu's rice, and was thinking very hard about rice.

Then, from the tree line past the lowest terrace, a jackal cried.

Three short yips. A pause long enough to count. Then a single long note that slid up and cracked at the top, very thin on the cold air.

She stood with her back against the shutter and her skin gone tight. She knew the call. They had practised it in the yard behind the sheds, eleven years old and nine, bent double with laughing, until Master Chathu came out to tell them the Resin House had no use for dogs. Three and one meant *tomorrow*. It meant *I will find you*.

The call came again. Three. A pause. One.

She did not answer. She could not, and it did not matter. She stood in the half-dark of a stranger's chamber, with the sword on the floor and the sleeper in the bed, and heard her sister tell her, over the length of the hill, to hold on one more day. And behind the call, quieter, the question she had filed and could not unfile: had he meant the cat?

# Chapter 7: There's a Way, Chechi

The tune reached Neela before the girl did.

It came from the far end of the lower market, under the tamarind, thin and cheerful and a little flat: four rising notes, a drop, the same four again. A fishing song. *Pull and pull, the tide is coming in.* Ammu had hummed it through every bad night of their childhood, and Neela would have known it through a wall.

She did not turn her head. She finished with the lamp-oil man, who had shorted his measure by a thumb, and paid him the fair price with a smile that cost him the other thumb. Then she said to the boy at her elbow, "Unni. Isn't your mother's stall by the fish racks?"

Unni went the colour of a plum. He was thin as a ladle and not much past twenty. "Janu said I wasn't to leave you, lady."

"Janu said you weren't to lose me. The whole market can see me, and I can see the whole market. Go and let your mother feed you. I'll call it guarding."

He went almost at a run.

Thirty-four nights. She had counted them on waking, with the hollow shake of the morning still in her hands. She walked toward the tamarind slowly, a warden's wife with a market basket, and let the song get louder.

The stall was three sacks and a scale on a plank. Behind it sat a girl in a patched cotton wrap, her hair oiled flat, her face burned brown by a coast sun that had not been hers a month ago. A tally string hung at her belt. She looked up, and her whole face broke open and was put back together as a trader's.

"Chechi!" said Ammu. "Lady, I mean, lady. Come, come. For the warden's wife the good sack."

In the market, a poor girl might call a richer woman elder sister and nobody would blink. Neela took the stool Ammu kicked toward her. "What's your name, girl?"

"Valli, lady. Coast-side, up with the last cart before the passes shut." Her eyes went over Neela's face, her throat, the high collar, and came back satisfied. "Black pepper. Heavier than hill pepper. Lady has a nose for it, I hear."

"Who says?"

"The whole market. They say the warden's wife can grade a berry blind." Ammu began scooping into the scale, and her voice dropped under the clatter without losing its sing-song. "They say the warden is tall. They say he never smiles and never raises his voice, and the women at the well are split on whether that's a husband or a wall. Which is it, chechi?"

"He tells me the guard rota."

Ammu's scoop stopped. "He what?"

"Over supper. Unasked."

"The old man will faint with joy." Ammu choked the laugh down into the scale. "A handsome man who tells you where all the knives are. Chechi, you've married a gift."

"I've married a ledger." Neela turned a berry in her fingers. It was the coast kind, well dried. "Say what you came to say."

Ammu weighed the pepper twice, which no honest trader does. When she spoke, the sing-song was gone, and the voice underneath was soft and patient and a little sad. It was an excellent copy.

"*Tell Seventeen the writ is on the road.* That's him, word for word. *After it lands, every day counts double. If the account stands open when the pass closes, I'll put the resin on her myself, and at dusk I'll read her page aloud where she can hear it. I would grieve, child. I always do.*"

Neela did not move. She had been braced for it since the jackal's call in the dark, and it still went through her like cold water.

Forced resin was not a thing you forgot hearing described. The scars tore open. You bled for days. You turned because the sun went down, with a man standing in the room, and then he read the page.

She counted the sacks. Three. She counted the coins in her sleeve. Seven.

"The marriage writ?" she said.

"He didn't say. He said it like it was a harvest." Ammu tied off the pepper. "Give me something for the cart, chechi."

There was a great deal she could have given her. That the sentry above the window had vanished two nights ago. That the hounds were kennelled at dusk. That there was a goat-boy's hand in her torn cloth, and a man at supper who had not stopped an old woman's leopard story. That a cat on a parapet had made her laugh until she cried, and a breath behind her had stumbled on the beat.

Every one of them was a thing she could not explain. Ammu would call the sentry luck and the hounds a quirk, and then she would carry them to Chathu, and Chathu would tell Neela what they meant.

"The weak hour is the last dark one," Neela said. "Before first light. I haven't proved it. I need ten days."

"Ten! He said—"

"He says what he says from a chair in the Wood. I'm the one in the room."

Ammu looked at her a moment longer than a trader should. Then she shrugged and reached for the cloth to wrap the packet. "Ten, then. I'll make it sound like nine."

She was humming again, tunelessly, the four notes over and over. Neela watched her fingers on the cloth. They were quick and square and had a nick across one knuckle that was new.

"Kirri's letters," Neela said. "Has she answered?"

The humming stopped.

"I wrote her again," Ammu said brightly. "That's three. Nothing yet. She's out on the water at dawn, hauling nets. She'll never have the hands for a pen again, she'll have rope-hands. And she couldn't spell *tide* when she was the big girl teaching us our letters." She tucked the packet into the basket with a flourish. "Busy, chechi. That's all. A woman with a boat has no time for sisters."

It was the right sort of joke, and it was a half beat too fast. The laugh after it was too round.

Neela said, "Three is a lot of letters for a woman with a boat."

"I'm a sister who pesters."

"You are."

They looked at each other over the pepper. The word *released* sat between them, and neither of them picked it up. It was the thing the twelfth contract bought. It was the door at the end of the road, the burned page and the birth name and the boat on the water. Neela had never in her life asked what stood on the other side of it, because there had never been anything to ask.

Her own voice came out low and flat. "If anyone ever saw me, Ammu, I'd be finished. Release or no release. You know that."

Neela hadn't known she was going to say it. A breath had hitched, on the beat of a laugh. She wanted someone to tell her it was nothing.

Ammu's hands went quite still on the cloth. "Not always," she said.

"What?"

"Not always. There's a way, chechi." Her voice had gone very small and very fast. "It costs, it costs a great deal, but there's a way if the one who sees you is—"

She stopped.

She was not looking at Neela. She was looking past her shoulder, and her face had gone blank and pale.

Neela did not turn. She had spent twenty years not turning her head. She let her eyes go idle across the lane and came to the next stall along, three paces off, where a woman was selling turmeric in yellow heaps. A big man in a captain's plain coat was leaning on her plank with a hand full of her best root, laughing at something she had said.

Muthu.

"—costs two panam the measure," Ammu said loudly, "and that's cheap, lady, that's near robbery on my own mother!"

Neela leaned in over the scale, as if to haggle, and mouthed it. *What way?*

"Buy the pepper."

"Ammu."

"Buy the *pepper*, chechi." The brightness in her eyes was something close to fear. Neela had seen it twice in twenty years. The last time was the night of a first turning, when a fourteen-year-old girl had held her friend's hand in a locked room and watched what she became. "It's a market story. It's nothing. I talk."

It was not nothing. There were few things the Resin House punished harder than failure, and one of them was telling a member what the masters had not. Whatever Ammu had been about to say, she had learned it somewhere she was not meant to, and she had nearly said it three paces from a captain of the warden's own guard.

"Lady Neela!" Muthu had seen her. He swung round, still grinning, turmeric yellow on his palm. "Down among the honest thieves. Where's your shadow?"

"Eating fish," said Neela. Her voice came out as pleasant as a bell. "I let him off."

"Poor Unni. He'll talk about it for a year." He looked at the stall and at Ammu, and Ammu bowed her head and became the least interesting girl in the market. "Who's this?"

"Valli. Coast pepper. She's honest, which is rare."

"Is she? Then I'll take four measures for the mess." He dropped coins on the plank without counting them. "The warden hates market day, did you know? He says it's all noise. I say that's the point of it." He winked. "He'll be along. Bet you a copper. He's heard I'm down here with the garrison's purse and he can't bear it."

"Why would he come down for you?"

"Oh, not for me." Muthu laughed his big laugh and the pigeons went up from the tamarind. "Valli, four measures, and don't skimp."

Ammu measured. Neela watched her, and the girl did not look up once, and under her breath she hummed. It was the fishing song. Pull, and pull, and the tide was coming in.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


He came at the foot of the lane, as Muthu had said, in his plain brown coat with no sword and no escort. He stopped at the edge of the market and looked at nothing, the way a man looks when he has been told where to find something.

Neela was a long way from the stall by then, with a basket of lamp oil, turmeric and pepper on her arm, and she had told herself she would not look back. She looked back. Ammu had a customer, an old man with a goat, and her head was bent.

"Warden," she said.

"Neela." He fell into step beside her. "Unni says you let him off."

"You've been speaking to him."

"He was coming up the road with a pomfret. He looked as if he'd been given a kingdom." A pause. "I had business with the salt factor."

"Down here."

"He keeps his tallies down here."

It was a good answer, and it was true as far as she could tell. It also did not explain why a man who hated market day had left the fort with no escort on the one afternoon she was out of it. By any count she made, there were three ways he could have known where to find her, and only one was an accident.

*Followed*, she thought. And then: *or he came for his captain.* And then, coldly, because it was how she had been taught: *or he came to watch whom I talked to.*

Her fingers tightened on the basket. He glanced at them, then at her face, and held out his hand for it.

"I can carry it," she said.

"I know." His hand stayed where it was.

She let go a beat too late. He took the weight without comment, but she saw him note the beat, and she saw that he had decided not to mention it. She noticed herself noticing, and walked on.

They went up the road through the lower village, where the houses were laterite and thatch and the cattle moved aside for no one, and a child stood in a doorway with a stick in her fist, guarding a hen.

The woman was at the third house. Neela knew her at once, without having been introduced, because she had stood next to her at the well for a quarter of an hour two mornings ago. Parukutty. Mid-thirties, a bad shoulder from the water pots, a daughter of six and a son of nine. Her husband had been a spearman on the wall, and he had been dead since spring.

A man stood in front of her door with a slate under his arm. He had the soft voice and the sad, level face of a collector. "I'm not unfeeling," he was saying. "The factor isn't unfeeling. But it's the fourth month, Parukutty. It's six measures and a half, and he can only carry a thing so long."

"After harvest," she said. "The queen's gift comes after harvest."

"The factor doesn't wait for harvest."

The warden's step slowed. He said, "Wait here," as if he had remembered a small errand, and put the basket back into her hands, and walked up the lane.

He did not hurry. He did not raise his voice. He spoke to the collector for no longer than it takes to say a name, and the man's face changed, and he took a cord of coins from Devan's hand without looking at the amount. The slate came out from under his arm. A thumb wiped it. Parukutty stood in her doorway holding her shoulder and watched the slate go clean, and did not understand it. The collector said something low and quick, and bowed, and went down the lane the other way.

The warden was already walking back.

He passed the doorway without a glance. The woman called after the collector once, bewildered, and then went in, and a moment later a child's voice rose, high and delighted, about the hen.

"Raman," Neela said, when he reached her. "That was her husband's name."

"Yes."

"Six measures and a half of salt."

"And a tally fee." He took the basket again, and this time she let it go on time. "The factor adds a charge when a debt passes a month."

"The queen's death-gift would have paid it."

"At harvest." He walked. "It's three months till harvest."

The road was steep here, and red dust rose around their feet. She was thinking of tins of jaggery sweets, warm from the kitchen, sent before a death. *For luck, child.* She was thinking of how every gift she had ever been given had arrived with its price written in a hand she couldn't read.

"Do you know every widow's debt?" she said. She meant it as a test, and made it light so it would not sound like one.

"Four widows on the wall rolls owe the factor," said Devan. "That was the largest. The other three I will see to by the new moon."

"By the new moon." She kept her eyes on the road. "You've done your sums."

"Yes."

"And you'll tell no one, and this one won't know whom to thank."

"That is the arrangement."

"A tidy arrangement," she said dryly. "It leaves nobody anything to hold against you."

His step checked, once, and went on. He did not look at her. But the corner of his mouth moved, as it had over the green wax, and she understood that she had named the thing he'd left unsaid, and that he knew she had. It was not a comfortable thing to have been understood by.

"Why?" she said.

He didn't pretend not to follow. "He died on my patrol."

"That's a date, not a reason."

"It's the reason I have."

He said it as he said everything: exact, unpadded, with nothing in it to hold on to. If she had thanked him he would have gone out of the room. She had known him six days, and she already knew it. He had not looked behind him once to see whether the woman saw.

It was the fourth thing he had done like that. The pulled sentry. The hounds. The shutters barred at dusk. And now this. Each time she had put it down to a hunter's craft, a man laying out a long patience for a long purpose. A hunter did lay out patience. She had never known a hunter to wipe a stranger's slate and tell no one.

*He is keeping a book*, she thought. But Chathu kept a book too, and his was the kind that came to collect. This one, she was beginning to think, was the sort a man keeps against himself.

It was inconvenient, and she did not want to think it. She had thirty-four nights and a man to kill, and a pair of hands that were still not quite steady from the dawn.

"You're quiet," said the warden.

"I'm counting."

"What?"

"Steps." It was half true. She'd lost the count at the well.

He nodded as though that were a reasonable thing for a bride to do. Up the road ahead, the laterite wall of Kettamala rose red against the sky, with the padippura dark at its foot. The cistern chain clanked the hour across the fields.

At the gate he stopped, and held out the basket. She took it. Her fingers closed on the handle and on the pepper packet wedged beside the oil jar, and found Ammu's knot through the cloth. Not a trader's single tie. A fisherman's double hitch, the one two girls had once learned from a net-mender behind the sheds, and had tied on everything for a summer.

*There's a way, chechi, but it costs.*

The chain clanked again. The warden went in under the arch, and Neela stood a moment in its shadow with her thumb on the knot. She did not undo it.

Tonight she would take the second spur, and she would leave a written question under the meeting stone, whatever it cost Ammu to answer.

# Chapter 8: The Unlit Lamp

"Leave it dark," Devan said.

Raiu stopped with the taper half raised. The corridor lamp hung in its iron bracket across from the chamber door, a clay lamp with a fresh wick and a full belly of oil, filled every dusk since the wedding. The boy looked at it, then at the warden, then at it again.

"Dark, Warden?"

"It saves oil."

"Janu fills it herself. She'll say—"

"She'll say I never counted oil in my life. She'll be right, and it will still be dark."

Raiu's mouth worked on that. He was nineteen, with a cowlick no water had ever beaten, and Devan had needed four months and two askings to learn his name. He had not lost it since. "Where do I stand, then?"

"Head of the stair. If I want you, you'll hear me."

"And the lady, if she wakes and wants a light?"

"The lady has a five-cup lamp at her window and a husband with a flint." Devan took the taper from his hand, pinched the flame out between finger and thumb, and gave it back. "Go on."

The boy went, wondering. Devan stood in the blue passage and listened to the fort settle. The chain clanked the hour at the cistern. A pot rang in the kitchen.

It did not save oil. A lit lamp laid a gold bar under the door and across the floor, and at first light that bar would reach the window corner, where a woman came over the ledge shaking, with nothing on her, to find her clothes. At the watch change a guard with a lit lamp would also try the latch, as guards did. Devan wanted no lamp near that door at that hour. Not Raiu's, nor Raiu's shadow stirring in it.

He had lain through every dawn since the wedding with his eyes shut and his breath counted. He had learned the sound of her coming in, which was almost none.

Inside, Neela stood at the window with a taper of her own, trimming the bird lamp's five wicks. She did not turn.

"Raiu's gone from the passage."

"I sent him to the stair."

"Why?"

"So the passage stays dark."

She looked at him over her shoulder, head a little on one side, and he watched her take the answer and set it down with the others. There was a row of them by now. He knew she was keeping them. He did not know what she thought the row added up to.

"Then mine can keep it company," she said, and pinched out the wick.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


Muthu came to the hall with the morning meal still on his chin.

They had the end of the long table to themselves. On a slate Devan had laid two squares of beeswax, each warmed the night before and pressed over a seal. One came from the tally in the dead raider's satchel. One came from a true tally out of Bhaskaran's gate chest. Beside them lay a stack of old council tallies, a year of brick-red wax that smelled of cloves.

"Pepper sprig," said Muthu, squinting. "I've seen a sprig."

"Look at the stem."

Muthu looked. Devan watched him go from cheerful to attentive. It was a quick road, and one reason he had made him captain.

"The council keeps seven tally seals," Devan said. "Each is cut with a private mark at the stem, so a clerk can tell whose box a tally left. Four have a bar. Three have a dot with a split under it." He set a fingertip on the forged impression. "This is a dot with a split."

"So it's one of three boxes."

"Or a copy of one."

"A copy's hard. The dies were cut by the palace engraver, and he's older than the queen." Muthu sat back. A laugh started low in his chest and came out big. "Chief. Come. You know what the treasury clerks are. They'd press a tally for a cousin and take a measure of arrack for the trouble. Some boy lent his master's seal to oblige a friend, and the friend sold it on. It's carelessness. The whole council runs on it. I once saw a minister sign a writ on the back of a fish."

"The wax is a month old and poured with clove oil."

"Then a careful careless clerk."

"Careless doesn't deliver to Thurai."

The laugh ran down. Muthu turned a square of beeswax over with one thick finger. "Three boxes," he said. "That's a lot of councillors to insult on a guess."

"It isn't a guess."

"No." He slid the slate back, gently. "Who else knows?"

"You."

"Then I'm the lucky one." He grinned, a shade late. "I'll keep it behind my teeth. Who would I tell?"

It was the only odd note in the whole talk, and too small to hold. Muthu liked to talk, and no man likes to hear that his masters might be thieves. Devan put it by.

"Four days to the seventh day," Muthu said, rising. "One round in the pit. I'll let you win."

"No."

"Eight years of no. One day I'll catch you on a yes."

"Eat something," Devan said, which was how he said *thank you for asking*. Muthu wiped his chin and went out whistling.

Devan stayed with the wax. Three seals. Three councillors, or three people with keys. Not one name yet, but the circle was thinner than the fort wall had been that morning. He began to put the squares away in a cloth and found he was thinking about a bride.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


At noon the sun stood straight over Kettamala and the red stone gave the heat back. Devan sat in the shade of the parapet's turn with his sword across his knees and watched the pass. A cart crawled up it. On the far cliff the langurs argued, loud and unconcerned. When strangers came, they would go quiet. They were not quiet.

"Janu says you haven't eaten."

Neela came along the walk with a covered brass dish held out in front of her like a thing that might go off. She wore the day kasavu from her trousseau, the gold border mended with small neat stitches where she had torn it for Kannan's hand.

"She said it to me, to Chinna, to the goat," she went on. "I came to see if you were alive."

"I am."

"I'll tell her. She'll be disappointed." She set the dish on the stone between them and sat on the parapet, a yard off, with her feet drawn up. Rice, fish, a green mash he didn't know. He ate. She watched him do it as she watched everything, the way a woman watches a lock.

"Can I ask something?"

"You will."

"Why do you never laugh first?" She tipped her head toward the yard below. "Muthu laughs at everything. Janu laughs at her own scolding. At supper someone says something funny and everyone looks at you, and you wait."

"I wait to learn whether the joke is at someone's expense."

"And then?"

"Then I know whether to laugh."

"That isn't a reason. That's a ledger."

"It's both."

She studied him a moment. Something moved behind her face, quick and wary, and then it put on a different look, almost sly. "Then here is one at mine," she said. "Safe to laugh at. I have a signed letter from the Chirayil house that says I can haggle a lamp-oil man down to his last thumb. Yesterday I won a thumb. Today Kannan's goat looked me in the eye, took a bite out of my sleeve, and I paid her in full. Nine generations of pepper traders, undone by an animal named after the stock."

For a breath he only looked at her.

Then it came up out of him, short and rough from disuse, and he heard it ring off the laterite. He put the back of his hand to his mouth. It went on a little behind it.

She was staring. Not guarded, not counting. She looked as if she had found a coin in a coat she had thought was empty.

"That," she said, "is the first time."

"It's the second."

"When was the first?"

"A long time before you." He lowered his hand. "I'll tell you the goat bit hard, though. I've seen her take a man's boot."

"You're laughing again."

"I won't answer that."

"Your ears are."

Asha had laughed first at everything. She had laughed at the quartermaster, at a bad bridge, at Devan himself, and then looked round to see who was in on it. Since the west stair he had waited, and waited, and been very sure whose expense it was at. He had not noticed how long it had been since he laughed at a thing because it was funny.

And he had been given hers once already, by theft. He thought of the ledge in the grey, and a sneezing ginger tom, and a woman shaking with silent laughter while he lay with his teeth in his lip and his breath gone wrong. He had hidden then. Now he could sit in the sun and be answered.

She ate a piece of fish from the dish with her fingers, and he saw the question come up in her, and saw her weigh it. The lower market. A man in a plain brown coat at the foot of a lane, with no escort and no sword. He had stood in the shade of the tamarind wall for the better part of a quarter hour before Muthu's laugh drew every eye and gave him cover to walk in. He had watched a girl with a pepper scale weigh the same measure twice. He had watched his wife listen with her back like a drawn string.

The business with the salt factor had been real. It had also been the second reason.

If she asked, he would say, *I won't answer that.* It was the truth, and she would hear it as a door.

She wiped her fingers on her sleeve. Her mouth opened, and she closed it again.

"Is that all the green stuff?" she said.

"It's all of it."

"Janu will ask."

"Tell her I ate it."

"Did you?"

"I will now." He took the dish, and neither of them said anything about anything else. It was the kindest silence anyone had given him in years, and the most dangerous.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


Unni came up the steps two at a time, red in the face, with a leather case held against his chest in both arms.

"Warden! Rider from Kodiyur. Registry blue. He wouldn't climb, said his orders were the gate, so I said I'd carry it, and he said sealed and into your hand and no one else's, and I said I'm *a* hand—" He ran out of breath.

"Well done," said Devan, and took the case.

The seal on the cord was council wax, dull brick red, the pepper sprig pressed clean. He broke it with his thumb. Inside was a single folded sheet of registry paper, thick and creamy, covered in a clerk's even black hand. The marriage writ, back from the palace record with the registrar's stamp at the foot.

Neela had come to stand at his shoulder, as a wife would. He tilted the page so she could see. He had never hidden a page from her, and he would not begin with one that carried her name.

It was a long writ and a dull one. Her ward-name, Chirayil, and the house's witnesses. His own title. The date of the sunset rite. The settlement of cloth, brass and four hundred panam, and the usual sentence about the wife's duty to the household. He read down it without hurry, as one reads something already seen done.

Near the foot, in the same flat clerk's ink, ran a clause that stopped him.

*...and the said wife, being lawfully wed, shall hold the pass-seal of Kettamala in trust from the hour of the warden's death, until the council shall name another in his place.*

He read it a second time.

The custom, as he had always known it, sent the seal to the captain of the fort when a warden died. There was an old line in the charter that sent it to a wife instead, if the warden had one. No warden had married in office in living memory. He had never given the line a thought, as a man gives no thought to a door in a house where he lives alone. Now it stood in black ink with the registrar's stamp under it. He was married, and a stranger would keep the seal.

A stranger. Chosen by the council. A ward from a house nobody at Kettamala had met, who had come up the hill with a bangle and a lamp and a quiet manner. The council whose wax sold passes to Thurai had pressed this match. They had pressed it in a hurry, and were pleased with themselves. The chain ran in his head: the council, the match, the widow, and the coast girl who had weighed twice.

If he died, the seal did not go to Muthu. It did not go to Bhaskaran. It went to whoever slept beside him.

The cold of it ran down his arms. He had thought of the marriage as a door the council had left open for a knife. He had not thought the marriage might itself be the thing they wanted done. A wife who was an odiyan, sent into his bed. A dead warden with leopard marks and a widow in black, calm, with the pass-seal in her lap. A widow could be steered. A widow could be set aside, afterwards, with no one the wiser.

And the woman at his shoulder either knew it, or she did not. If she did not, she was a blade never told whose hand she was in. If she did, then he had laughed beside her on a hot wall an hour ago, and she had let him.

He could not make himself believe the second. He marked that, as he would have marked a weak place in the wall. Either way, she had to stay alive and near. Dead, she told no one anything. Alive, she knew the name of the hand that held her.

Unni shifted his feet, waiting. A langur barked and was answered. Devan raised his eyes from the page to say something light, so that she would not see him read it a third time.

Neela was looking at the writ. Her face had gone still, as water goes still just before it takes a shape. For the first time since the wedding, she was not watching him at all, only the page, and he could not tell whether she was reading it or remembering it.

# Chapter 9: The Goat Path

Janu had got as far as the yellow eyes when the warden put down his cup.

"—and my grandmother swore it came down the pass every dry-season dusk," Janu said, ladling rice onto Neela's plate without being asked, "big as a cart-pony, with a coat like spilled lamp oil, and it took a man off the road above the second ford. Just took him. Left his sandals standing side by side, neat as a prayer. And the next night it came for—"

"Not tonight, Janu."

He said it quietly, with his eyes on the rice. The old woman's ladle stopped in the air. For a moment Neela thought she would argue. Janu argued with the cistern chain.

"No," Janu said. "No, well." She set the ladle down. "It's a bad tale for supper anyway. Eat, child. You're all wrists."

Neela ate. She watched the warden over the rim of her cup.

He did not look up. He tore his bread and ate it. Two seats down, Muthu opened his mouth for a joke and thought better of it, which was strange in itself. She turned it over and found the simplest reading. Some men could not abide a story. Her own guardian's accountant had been the same, and had walked out of rooms if anyone began a song.

*He dislikes stories*, she decided. She set it on the shelf with his other dislikes: market day, bells, gratitude. There was no harm in a man who hated stories. It was a good thing to know about the man she was going to kill.

She did not think about the pass-seal. She had read that clause twice at his shoulder, kept her face as still as water, and put it away where she kept things she could not use yet. Nor did she think about how it had felt when he went quiet beside her.

"The council has written," Devan said, to the table in general. "They expect the warden's wife to be shown to the pass villages. They want her seen." A pause so small she would not have heard it a week ago. "I'll take her down to the lower waystation tomorrow. I need to inspect it anyway."

"Both birds," said Muthu. "One stone."

"Eat something, Muthu."

Neela said, "Will I be inspected as well?"

"Only by the goats," said Devan.

Janu laughed so hard she had to sit.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


Thirty-two nights.

She woke to the count, to the hollow shake in her hands, and to the thought that today she would watch him work. Watching was her work. For eight days she had mapped his chamber, his rota and his hours, and he had handed her half of it unasked. She had not once seen him do his real work, on a road, away from walls.

By the first mile she knew three things about him, and the first was that he was slow.

He did not take the cart road at a trot. He sent Unni ahead, then called him back, then sent him ahead again when the ridge showed bare. At every bend he stopped his pony and sat listening. He checked his own girth, and then hers, without comment. He had her walk the last stretch before the first village, so that people would see a woman on foot with her husband, and not a bride carried in on a beast.

"We'll be there by noon at this rate," Neela said, "if the monsoon holds off."

"It will."

"I've seen a snail with more ambition."

"The snail isn't carrying anyone." His gaze stayed on the road. "Nobody is asked to be fast on this road. They're asked to arrive."

She thought it was caution of the dullest kind. A man with that many men and that many walls had no business creeping like a thief. She made a note of the pauses anyway. He stopped at every bend, always for the same count of ten. At the third ford he went across upstream of the usual crossing. He never used the same place twice. A man who wished to be waited for would be easy to wait for. Without meaning to, he had given her a lesson in how not to be.

She did not like how much she wanted to ask him why.

The villages came and went. Women at the wells looked her over and decided, and she knew most of the names by the second house. Saroja, who sang at the well. A grandfather called Kesu with one eye. A girl who held up a hen as a gift, and was told by her mother in a furious whisper to put it down. Devan stood by with his hands behind him, silent, while Neela said everything that was needed. She sensed him watching her do it. Twice he nearly spoke.

The langurs started in the early afternoon, on the cliff above the narrow gorge below the last village. They had been barking all morning, a hoarse and tireless racket that bounced off the rock until you stopped hearing it.

They stopped.

All of them, together. The silence came down the pass like a cloth dropped over a cage.

Devan's hand went up. Unni's pony stopped dead. Neela, who had her hand on the bridle already, felt the back of her neck prickle in a way that had nothing to do with him.

"Strangers," she said softly.

"On the road, or above it." He did not turn his head. "Both of you stay."

He went forward alone. She watched him go. He did not draw his sword. He walked his pony to the bend and stopped for ten counts, and then he made a small flat gesture to Unni that said, quite plainly, *back*. It occurred to her that the slowness had been a tool for a day exactly like this one, and that she had been laughing at the wrong end of it.

The rockfall lay beyond the bend.

It had come off the cliff and filled the cart road wall to wall. Red-brown boulders the size of oxen, slabs of sheared laterite, a drift of loose stone to the knee. Fresh dust still clung to the biggest, and the air tasted of it.

"Dry week," Devan said, when she came up. "Dry cliff. Nothing rains stone on a dry week."

"Rock falls."

"It does. Not at the one place on the road where a cart has to stop." He glanced at the rim, where the langurs sat motionless in the thorn. "Somebody wants us standing here, looking at it."

Her body had gone cold and easy, the way it did before work. She counted the ways out. The way they had come was open. A line of boulders at the foot of the slope made a good place for a bow. The cliff offered a ledge she would have chosen herself.

"Unni," said Devan. "Back to the fort. Tell Bhaskaran the road is shut at the gorge, and I want twenty men with bars. Go by the high ford."

"Warden, I'm to guard the—"

"You're to ride. Go."

Unni went. His pony's hooves faded up the track, and the silence closed behind them.

Devan turned to her. He was working out how to ask her to wait somewhere safe, she could see, and also working out that there was nowhere safe. For the first time she saw him honestly at a loss. It did not look like weakness. It looked like a man laying two bad choices on a scale.

"There's a path," Neela heard herself say.

"I know of none."

"Left of the split rock. Up the scree and over the shoulder, then down through the thorn to the old well. Goats take it." She was already moving. "Give me the pony."

She did not know why she said it. Her feet knew. They had turned toward a gap in the thorn that she could not see yet, and she followed them, and there it was, a threadbare line of dung and beaten earth no wider than a hand, rising behind a boulder cloven by lightning. She had not looked for the rock. It had been where she expected it.

*Left of the split rock.*

Nobody had told her that. She had read the scouting notes on the pass until her eyes burned. There was no goat path in them.

She went up the scree with the pony's bridle in her fist. Behind her she heard the warden's boots, steady, a half pace slower than hers on purpose. He did not ask until they were over the shoulder and the thorns closed in.

"How do you know this?"

"How do you know where a ford is shallow?"

"I walk it."

"Then you know what I'd say." She kept her back to him. "Mind the root. It's on the left."

There was a root on the left. She heard him step round it.

"That isn't an answer," he said.

"No. It's a question, and you've asked it the wrong way round." She glanced back, and his face was calm, but his eyes were on her feet. "Would it help you to know, Warden? Or would it just be something else you kept?"

He considered that, with the same patient weight he gave every question. "I won't ask again today."

"That's generous."

"It's not generous. It's tactics." The path bent, and he followed. "I would like you to keep on knowing it."

The old well was where she had known it would be. They came down through the thorn into a quiet fold of the hills, and below them, a long bowshot off, stood the lower waystation. They had come around the rockfall in the time it took the langurs to start up again, all at once, loud and bored and scolding. The strangers, whoever they were, had gone on.

Neela stood still a moment with the bridle in her hand. She had a strong wish to sit down.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


The waystation was a low laterite house with a well, a long carters' shed, and a yard trodden to powder. No one lived there between the seasons. A spiked board by the door said that the warden's tally was needed for the pass. The gate stood open.

Devan went into the yard like a man entering a sickroom.

He crouched. He did not touch anything. He studied the ground for the better part of a hundred counts, and she stood at the gate and watched his eyes travel, left to right and back, the way a clerk's do down a column.

"Six men," he said. "Two nights back, not three. They came in from the north on foot and left to the north on foot, before dawn. One of them dragged his right foot a little. Here, and here. Two lit a fire in the shed, with the door shut so the glow wouldn't show. They slept in shifts." He bent over a smear of ash. "They ate a hot meal, so someone fed them. Rice. This was a rest, not a raid."

"How do you know it was rice?"

"Because one of them dropped the husk." He held up a pinch of it. "Raiders from the coast live on dried fish. These men weren't hungry. They were waiting for something."

"For what?"

"A cart." He stood. "I don't know yet. Tell me what you see."

She had not meant to go in. She went in and crouched at the edge of the churned dust, where the clay was damp at the well's lip, and the prints were sharp as stamped wax. It was not tracking. She could not have followed those men over a hill. This was the other thing, the thing the guild had drilled into her until it stopped being a skill and became a habit of the eye. It was the study of feet.

A hundred patrols had walked past her window, and she had learned them by their boots.

"They aren't raiders, or not the way you mean," she said. "Look at the heel. This one is ground flat, straight down, in a ring. He's stood a lot of watches in one spot. Soldier. And this pair." She tapped a print. "The outer edge is worn thin, left more than right. That's a man who rides. Somebody trained them, and fed them, and these soles aren't a month old. A town cobbler did the stitching. See the double row?"

"I see a line."

"It's two lines, close together, in thread that was waxed. Coast work. Nothing from up here. They've been mended for a long road by someone who could pay for it." She looked up. "These men didn't walk from the sea. They were brought most of the way, and set down."

He was quiet. Then he said, slowly, "I'd have said six men on foot."

"I'd have said six men."

"I'd have missed the boots entirely."

"I'd never have found the husk." She heard herself add, drily, "Or the foot."

"The foot was easy."

"For you."

The corner of his mouth moved. She watched it, helplessly, as she would have watched a knot come loose.

They stood in a dead yard with a well and a fire-black shed, and between them they had made the thing whole. One of them would have had half of it. Neither could have had the rest. He had read where they went and how, and she had read who they were. In twenty years she had never met someone whose skill ended where hers began.

She did not like it. She was careful to be clear to herself about that.

"Whoever sent them," Devan said, "wants me to find this, or wants me to find nothing. I can't tell which." He nodded at the shed. "Neither can you."

"No."

"Good." He seemed to mean it. "I'd distrust you if you could."


<center>\* \* \*</center>


They rode home at dusk by the cart road. The twenty men with bars had reached the fall by late afternoon and cut a lane through it, wide enough for ponies led single file, and the langurs had greeted the sight of them with a roar of professional outrage.

Neela rode alone beside the warden. The men and their bars had gone back an hour ago.

The light was going. Over the western ridge a long bank of cloud had been building all afternoon, the first heavy cloud she had seen that year, bruise-purple underneath and gold along its edges. The wind had changed. It carried a smell she had not met since the spring, of dust waiting for water.

She had no resin.

She had not carried it. No woman carries a flask to inspect a waystation. It lay in its fold of cloth, in a chest, in a room with a lamp she had pinched out last night. The sun was going down on a road two miles from a gate, and for the first time since the wedding night she was nothing but what she looked like: a woman on a pony, with a tired back, beside a man.

No ache would count tonight. One night did not matter. She would take the resin when she reached her room, and be gone before the moon.

Not yet.

*One mile*, she thought. She let it be a small bargain, with no one to witness it. She would have this for one mile.

He rode half a length ahead, as he always did, so that his shoulder covered hers. He had said nothing for a long time. He did not fill silence. She had once thought that was a trap, a man leaving a gap for her to fall into. Now it seemed more like a courtesy. He was leaving her room.

"You were right about the boots," he said at last.

"I was."

"You'd make a good tracker."

"I'd make a bad one. I'd find the shoes and forget the road." She watched the cloud. "You'd make a good thief."

"Why?"

"You know where people put their feet. That's all stealing is."

He laughed, once, short and low, like the one on the wall. She felt it in her chest.

She was not afraid. Out on the hill tonight she would have been a quarter mile from the fort, belly low, ears forward, every hair a nerve, a creature that did not trust the wind. Here she could hear the pony's hooves and a man's breath and her own voice with nothing behind it. She could smell clay and thorn and the coming rain. The man she was paid to kill rode a length ahead with his back to her.

She did not look at the back of his neck. Not for any good purpose.

The first mile ended at a milestone, a squat worn thing with a pepper sprig cut in its face. She saw it come and she did not look away. The second began.

The wind pushed a long cold breath down the pass. High up, the first drop struck her hand, big and warm.

"Rain," said the warden, and then, after a moment, "A real one."

She almost laughed. She didn't know why.

Ahead, the lamps of Kettamala came on, one at a time, up the red wall. She counted them without meaning to. Eleven. Then she started the road again from the beginning, as if she had something to learn by it, and could not think what.

She still had no idea how she had known the path.

# Chapter 10: The Broken Beak

Forty-four steps.

Neela counted them up the west stair in the wet red morning, and at the landing she kept going. Forty-five. Forty-six. She stood with one hand on the cold rail while the number ticked over in her head like the cistern chain, and she could not make it stop.

It had begun at the bottom.

She had come to map an approach. The stair climbed the west face of the keep from the lower court to the passage outside the warden's door. Two guards on it by night, Devan had told her himself, and Raiu at the head since the lamp went dark. She wanted to know where the stone rang, where the shadow lay, how many steps a body could cover before a man on the landing heard. It was ordinary work. She had done it in a dozen houses and slept well after.

The newel post at the foot was a carved parrot, a forearm long, its wings cut flat against its sides. Someone had loved the thing once. The beak had snapped off at the root long ago, and years of passing palms had worn the stump to a river stone. She had laid her hand on it as anyone would, to start the climb, and her fingers had closed round the stump as if it had been shaped for them.

Her stomach dropped, like a foot that finds no stair where the stair should be.

She stood ten counts with her hand locked on a wooden bird. Nothing happened. A pot rang in the kitchen. The court steamed in the sun. Then she let go and wiped her palm down her kasavu and told herself it was hunger. The morning after the other one was always a hole in her, and holes made the mind jump.

So she had climbed, and counted, and now she was on the landing counting air.

She made herself look. A pala-wood rail. Steps dished in the middle by a hundred years of feet. At the eleventh step, a stone that rang hollow under her heel, over a drain slot. That was useful. A man at the head would hear that stone. She put *eleventh* away like a coin and went down, and at the foot her mind went on without her. Forty-five. Forty-six.

Names, then. It was what she always did. Kannan. Pepper, who bites. Chinna at the kitchen door. Gopan at the chain. Bhaskaran, Unni, Raiu with the cowlick. By Gopan the number had thinned to a mutter. By Raiu it was gone.

Janu stood at the cistern well, a good ten paces from the foot of the stair, with a cup in each hand. She held one out and did not come nearer. Neela crossed the court to take it.

"Buttermilk," Janu said. "You were up and down that stair like a squirrel. I watched from the kitchen. What are you hunting?"

"The best view of the pass."

"The best view is from the wall, where a woman has something to lean on." Janu drank and studied her over the rim. "You're grey. Did he keep you up?"

"The rain did."

"Mm." The old woman was not fooled, though she was fooled in the wrong direction. She lowered her voice, which for Janu meant that only the cistern, the kitchen and two sergeants could hear. "Since I have you alone. He's barred the shutters again."

Neela's cup stopped halfway. "Has he?"

"Don't play simple, child, they're your shutters. Every dusk since the wedding. Thirty years I've run this house, and that room stood open every night it didn't storm. His father's rule. A room should breathe. Not once barred, not the winter his mother died. Now the bar goes across when I light the evening lamps, and the whole fort knows it. The girls talk of nothing else. Chinna has a wager."

"On what?"

"On why." Janu counted on her fingers like a clerk. "Bhaskaran's lads say a man with a new wife wants his privacy. Gopan says it's my grandmother's tale. A grown man, warden of the border, afraid of a story. I told Gopan the boy was never afraid of anything but quiet. Chinna says nothing and takes coin on all of it."

"And what do you say?"

"I say it's the damp. It gets into a man's head up here."

"He doesn't strike me as damp."

"He doesn't strike you as anything, he's a locked chest. That's why I'm telling you." Janu reached out a dry hand and pinched Neela's wrist, hard, as women do who feed people. "Ask him."

"I'd sooner ask the goat."

"The goat would answer." Janu took back the empty cup and looked her over again. "All wrists," she said, with deep dissatisfaction. She went back to the kitchen, giving the stair a wide berth that she did not seem to know she was giving.

Neela stood in the steaming court. The whole fort knew.

That was the part that turned in her like a key.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


She did her sums in the warden's chamber, with the door shut and the shutters open to the afternoon.

The bar lay along the wall on its two hooks, seasoned teak, thick as her forearm. She could lift it one-handed. She leaned out over the ledge, set her palm to the slats and pushed. They held. A bar does not care what has claws.

She laid it out on her fingers.

The hounds, kennelled since the wedding, who had run the yard loose for years. The sentry pulled off the rampart above this window. The corridor lamp left dark. Raiu sent to the head of the stair. Four things, and every one of them let a hunter in.

Then the fifth. The bar, every dusk, known to every maid and goat-boy in Kettamala. It shut a beast out. It left exactly one living creature in the fort who could lift it from inside.

She sat on the ledge and said it plainly to the afternoon. A beast's work, found in a room no beast could enter, and beside the body a woman with a registry paper in her hand. The pass-seal in trust until the council named another. Two days ago she had read that clause at his shoulder and put it away to use. It had a second face. A widow in black with the seal in her lap. The council's men would not need an hour.

The crown would hang her for it, and that was the lesser ruin. Chathu would lose his best tool, and he balanced every loss in some other coin. Ammu was the nearest coin he had.

So. On purpose, or by chance?

By chance, he was a man who slept badly and feared the dark. Janu had said as much. Hounds shut in to spare their howling. A sentry pulled because Muthu complained of wall duty. Oil saved. A bar for a bad dream. Each was an ordinary order, and every one had a plain answer if she asked.

On purpose, he had distrusted the match from the start, as any man would. He knew what the council might send up his hill. And he had built a cage out of small orders, one at a time, each with its excuse, so that if the bride was what he feared, her kill would hang her. It was how she would have done it herself.

Neither reading cost her a seam. She tried them again from the other end and they held just as well.

She did not like not knowing. She liked even less how much she wanted to.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


Thirty-one nights.

She counted them at dusk while he sat at the table with his cloth of wax squares and she sat at the window trimming the bird lamp. When the light went he got up and barred the shutters himself. She watched him do it. He lifted the bar, dropped it into the brackets, and the wood knocked once, softly. He did it like a man closing a purse. He did not look at her.

He lay down on his side of the bed. She waited, and counted his breath, and heard the small hitch come a third of the way through each long count.

Then she lifted the bar herself, in the dark, and stood it against the wall. She uncorked the flask. The smell was scorched tamarind and pine sap. She drew the cold smear over her throat and wrists, and the burn came up through the old scars like a name being called, and then the long pull, and the floor tilted away.

The hill took the hunger out of her first. She worked it down for hours, wet-bellied and quick, the way you work a cramp. Near the small hours she came back along the parapet where no sentry stood. The ginger tom on the wall gave her a long insulted look and left. She went in through the shutters she had unbarred and dropped to the floor without a sound.

The room came at her in smells. Sandalwood soap. Old iron from the sword that lay on the floor on her side of the bed, hilt toward her, as it had lain since the first night. She had never understood it. The cold, clean smell of the brass kindi on her side, spout toward the pillow. Full again. Janu's girls, she supposed. Janu filled everything.

She crossed to his side.

The bed was low. His throat lay a hand's length under her jaw, with the pulse moving in the notch of it, slow and even. The animal in her knew precisely what that was. The hunger said what it said. Her mind ran the kill to its end, one pass, quick and clean, and it was good work.

Then it ran on, as her mind always did, to what the kill would cost by morning.

Her eyes went down, away from the throat, and found his hand.

It lay on the blanket, palm down, fingers loose. The knuckles were ridged with old scars, pale and flat as split seeds, the kind a man gets from years of hitting wood and stone. Seven across the right hand. She counted them. A thin white line crossed the base of the thumb. It was a hand that had hit things for a long time and then stopped hitting anything that hit back, and she could read the stopping in it, the way she could read a path in a yard.

She did not know how long she stood. When she came to herself the count in her head had gone past sixty and she had done nothing.

*Think.*

No beast could carry this kill. That was why. She told herself so twice, and the second time she was still looking at his hand.

She lowered her head toward it. Slowly. Her whiskers trembled in the warmth coming off his skin, and she stopped a hair short, with her muzzle level with the scars and her breath moving the fine hair on his wrist.

His breath hitched. The fingers curled a little tighter against the blanket, as a sleeper's will, and eased, until they lay a finger's width from her nose.

She waited. He did not wake.

She drew back. She went across the stone floor, past the kindi and the hilt-first sword, and leapt to the sill. At the ledge she looked back once, which she did not do.

His hand still lay open on the blanket.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


First light found her on the ledge, human, grey with cold and shaking from her teeth to her heels. The hollow came behind it, the sick wide hunger of the morning after. She went in over the sill with nothing on her and nothing in her, and crossed the room on a floor that felt like ice.

The kindi was where it always was. She drank from the spout with both hands, cold water running down her chin, and then she stood with her forehead against the brass and let her breath come back.

Behind her he slept.

Beasts were out, then. What was left was hands.

The guild had taught her the points before it taught her the other thing. There was one at the base of the skull, a thumb's width below the bone, that the old masters called the door. A man struck there with the right weight did not cry out, and he did not look like anything but a man who had fallen. A fall in a pit, in the dark, at the hour no one was watching. No bar. No beast. No bride at a window.

She pictured it as she would any job. The pit, the dark, her fingers finding the door. And in the picture his right hand came up unasked, the seven pale scars, to close on her wrist. Or it did not come up at all, and lay open on the red earth, and she was the one who had ended it.

Tactics, she decided. A man with that hand would block fast, and she should allow for it. She filed it where she had filed *no beast could carry this kill*, and it sat there badly.

Janu had given her the hour herself, and the pit lamps had shown her the place. He went down to the kalari under the east wall in the last dark hour, alone, with no guard and no witness.

The ache could count one night. Tomorrow she would leave the flask in its fold of cloth.

It was a good plan. She told it to the kindi and to the grey window, and no one argued.

Then she turned, and saw the window plainly, and the plan snagged.

It was first light. The sky in the shutter slats was the colour of dishwater, and the cistern chain had not yet clanked the hour. And he lay a yard from her, abed, breathing like a man with all morning to spend.

Janu said he rose in the last dark hour. This was the fourth first light she had come in off the hill and found him exactly so. Each time she had found a reason. A late night. A bad one. Janu's word against her own eyes, and she had let Janu's word win for a week.

Either the old woman was wrong, or he rose later than the fort believed, or he had stopped for a reason.

And if she was right, the pit was his. He had drilled in it ten thousand dawns. He would know every stone of the floor. She would have one strike, and no second.

So she would not trust a story. Tomorrow, human, in the dark hour, she would go down and see the pit for herself, and find the way to the door.

Behind her the hitch came, a third of the way through the count.

# Chapter 11: Dawn Drill

The smell caught her halfway down the east path, and she was sick in the dirt before she knew it was coming.

There was little in her to lose. She knelt in the dark with one palm flat on the cold red ground and heaved up water and bile, while the thing that had done it hung in the damp air around her. It was sharp and green and rank, like a crushed bug on hot rubbish. Lantana. It grew down the whole slope under the east wall in tangled banks higher than her head, with flowers like small orange fists, and her feet had trodden a mat of its leaves into the path.

Neela had hated that smell for as long as she could remember, and had never found a reason. In the Resin House sheds she had been the one to stand upwind of any hedge being cut, a cloth tied over her face, and nobody had asked her why.

She wiped her mouth on her wrist and told herself her hands shook from the cold.

Thirty nights.

Last night she had lain beside him as nothing but a woman, the flask in its fold of cloth and the shutters barred. She had listened to him breathe. The small hitch had come a third of the way through each long count, over and over, until the last dark hour. Then the breathing had changed. The bed had moved by the width of a man leaving it, and there had been no other sound. She had counted sixty and followed.

Rising was something she had only ever heard of, from Janu and from the lamps in the pit. Now she stood up out of the lantana with the stink in her hair, put the back of her hand over her nose, and breathed through her mouth down the last of the slope.

The pit lay below the east wall like a dropped coin. It was a round hollow, twenty paces across and shoulder-deep, its sides cut into the red earth and shored with laterite blocks. Lamps burned in niches all round the rim. The air above it smelled of sesame oil and, under that, of hot clay. Steps were cut into the north side. She crouched at the lip behind the last of the bushes, where the lamplight did not reach, and looked down.

He was already working.

Barefoot, bare to the waist, his mundu hitched high, he moved across the floor of the pit with no sound except the snap of cloth and the hiss of breath. She had braced herself to see skill. She had seen skill. Vellan was skill, all of it packed into a body like a bear's. This was something else, and for a moment she could not name it.

It was arrival. The strike was over before she knew where it had been aimed. A turn, a drop, a palm that stopped a finger's width from nothing. His forearm cut across at the height of a throat. His heel came round at the height of a knee. Between one count and the next he was ten feet away with his weight low and his hands loose, as if he had stood there all along. Vellan hit harder. This man simply got there first.

All of it was aimed at a man who was not there.

She watched his hands and began, against her will, to read them. The temple. The hollow under the ear. The soft place beneath the ribs on the left. The notch under the skull that the old masters called the door. Each blow landed on a point, exactly, on an enemy only he could see. He had put every one of the points in the air and killed it, over and over, in a pit with nobody in it.

*Eight years*, she thought. Janu had said it in the first week, at the lip of this same pit. *He used to have a partner.* Eight years of rehearsing the death of a stranger, and no one ever allowed to stand in the place where the stranger went.

She counted his turns. At the sixth she saw it.

He turned to the right, fast, with the right hand sweeping high. And the left hand came down, only half a hand's width, to rest at his hip. It was the most natural thing in the world. It would also open him from the cheek to the belt for the time it takes to say a name. In the whole pit there was nobody to see it but her.

She had not come to be useful. She had come to kill him. She went down the north steps anyway.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


He stopped before her foot reached the third step.

Not startled. He simply stopped, in the middle of a turn, and the stillness was worse than a shout. For one count she felt how far she was from any wall.

"Neela."

"Warden."

He stood with his chest rising and falling slowly. Sweat ran down the old scars on his ribs. He did not reach for anything, because there was nothing to reach for. He studied her the way he studied a ford, patiently and from every side at once.

"It's early," he said.

"So are you."

"I'm always early."

"I couldn't sleep." That was true. It was a pleasure to say a true thing.

"The lantana's bad on that path." His eyes went to her face, and then to the damp on her sleeve. "Gopan keeps meaning to cut it."

"It's only a smell."

"Mm."

She came down the last steps and out onto the pit floor. The earth was warm from his feet and came off red on hers. She went to stand a few paces from him, where a bride out for a walk might stand, and took care that her hands were empty and in plain sight.

"Show me a form," she said.

"Which?"

"One with two people in it."

"No."

He said it without heat. It was the same voice he used for *Not tonight, Janu*.

"I don't spar," he said.

"Janu says you did."

"Janu says a great deal."

"For how long have you not?"

"Eight years in the spring." He gave the figure as plainly as a tally of grain. Then, after a moment, as if he owed it to her: "Muthu asks me every seventh day. I say no every seventh day. He's a patient man."

"Is that a kindness to him, or to you?"

"I haven't decided."

She did not smile, though she felt the place where a smile would go. She looked at the swept ring of clay where he turned and said the next thing as lightly as she could.

"Then let me look at you. I won't touch you with anything but my eyes. Your stance is wrong."

For the first time something moved in his face. It was not offence. It was a kind of astonished interest, the look of a man who has been handed a bill and finds it correct.

"Who taught you to look?"

"A man who guarded pepper." It came out smooth. Kunjan Chirayil's warehouse had kept two guards, and one of them had once stood on a quay with a cudgel while she did not need him. "He thought a girl in a counting house should know when a man was about to lie to her, or hit her. He said they started in the same place."

"Where?"

"The feet."

He weighed her. She had given him a true thing again, wrapped in a false one, and she wondered whether he could tell which was which.

"Go on," he said.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


"Take it from the turn."

He went back to the place where he had stopped. She walked a slow circle round him, at the distance of a long arm, and said *again* twice, and the third time he went through the whole movement at half speed with her at his shoulder.

"Right turn. Hands high." She watched them. "There. Your left hand drops. Not much."

"I know my hands."

"You know where you put them. Again."

He turned again. The left hand came down.

"You see?"

"I see you telling me that I have something I don't."

"Then I'm wrong, and you can laugh at me later." She moved in behind him. "Your head's wrong too. It rides forward when you turn. May I?"

The silence in the pit was exact. A lamp spat. Somewhere above them, behind the wall, a goat coughed.

"Yes," said Devan.

She laid her fingers on the back of his neck.

His skin was hot and damp, with a ridge of old muscle under it. She pressed the head back an inch, where it belonged, and he let her. His chin came down. His neck stood straight and bare in the lamplight, the short hair clipped close to the nape.

At the base of the skull, a thumb's width below the bone, her fingers rested on the door.

Her hand knew it before she did. It had made this exact shape on sandbags and sheep, in practice, and once not in practice. The heel at the right angle. The weight dropping through the wrist, not the arm. A quarter-turn and a push, quick and clean. He would not cry out. He would fall as a man falls in a pit in the dark when no one is looking. By the time the day watch came on, there would be a widow in black with a seal in her lap and every honest man in the fort to say that the warden had always been careless in his own kalari.

She felt the pulse under her fingers. It was slow.

"One," she said, so low it was hardly a word.

The lamp flame leaned. A bar of grey had come into the sky above the east wall. She could do it by the next breath and be up the steps and in the chamber before the chain clanked. There was no bar to explain, no beast, no cage. This was the plan she had made, and it was a good one. She was free to be a woman who had finished her twelfth contract. Her page would burn and she would have a name.

"Two."

He had not turned his head. He had heard the number, and the next, and he knew what they were counting down. His hands hung at his sides, open, the palms half out. She knew that hand. It had lain on a blanket in the dark with seven pale scars across the knuckles, a finger's width from her nose, and it had not closed then either. His shoulders sat low and quiet under her palm, like a man's in a chair he trusts. She thought him a fool, or this a trap, and waited for the trap to close, and it did not. He only stood and breathed.

Then she thought of nothing at all. That was the strange part. The pit went quiet around her the way the langurs had gone quiet at the gorge, all at once, and her mind did not run the kill to its end. It stopped short of it, like a hand at a closed door.

"Three."

On the third breath he let the air out of his chest, slowly. He did not draw the next one at once.

He was waiting. He had heard every number, and he knew what her hand was resting on. She understood it with her whole body in the space of that held breath, and still she stood there with her fingers on his neck.

She lifted her hand.

"Your guard drops on the left," she said. Her voice came out level. "When you turn right the hand comes down half a palm. Somebody fast enough would use it."

Devan did not move at once. When he did, he turned slowly, as a man turns when he does not want to be mistaken for a threat. The lamplight lay on the plain, tired look of his face.

"You'd have had me," he said.

"Yes."

That was all. She had expected him to ask her something, and the question hung ready in the lamp-smoke between them. It never came. He regarded her as if she had given him a figure he had expected, and he was quietly checking it against his own count.

She did not explain. She had no explanation to give him that she could stand to hear herself say.

"The left," said Devan, at last. He said it to himself, in the voice of a man filing a rule.

Neela turned, and went up the north steps.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


Her knees did not give out until the top.

She stood at the lip with one hand on the cold laterite and the other flat against her ribs, and the shaking came up from her heels in long waves. Her hands were wet. She looked at the right one, the one that had touched him, and could not make it close.

Below her, he had not gone back to his forms. He stood in the middle of the swept clay with his head bent and one hand lifted to the back of his own neck, exactly where hers had been. He held it there as if he were testing a bruise.

She went into the lantana with her breath held, and the smell found her anyway. At the bend she gagged again. Nothing came up.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


The chamber was empty and the shutters were still barred.

She lifted the bar and set it against the wall. Grey light came in through the slats and laid ruled lines along the floor. She sat down on the edge of the bed and put her hands between her knees to hold them still.

*Reasons*, she thought. *Make a list.*

The east wall, first. The pair on the walk above the pit were never off it at dawn. If she had struck, a man might have looked down and seen a bride bending over her husband. That was the first reason, and she turned it over and found it sound.

It stayed sound for about as long as it took to remember that the east wall pair faced the hill. She had watched them from the lantana. Neither had once turned round.

The flask, then. She had come with no resin and no beast. If the strike went wrong she had nothing behind it. But the door did not go wrong, and she knew it better than she knew her own name, which was a poor joke, because she had never known her name.

The ache, then. One night off the flask already counted against her. A woman starting that count should not take chances. That was almost reasonable. She tried it twice and it would not stay put.

Something else would not stay put either, and she made herself pick it up. Four first lights in a row she had come in off the hill and found him abed. Last night she had stayed in, and he had gone down in the dark. On the nights she was out, he lay there until she was back. On the nights she was in, he went to his pit. She laid the two side by side and they fit like halves of a tally stick. It could be chance, or habit, or his bad sleep. But a man who rose by his own clock would not care where she was.

Was he waiting for her?

She had no answer, and the question was bigger than the one she had started with. She put it down unfinished and did the sum she had been walking around. Chathu's message had been plain. Once the writ landed, every day counted double. Thirty nights on the calendar were fifteen by his ledger. If the account stood open when the pass closed, he would put resin on her himself and read her page at dusk. And Chathu balanced every loss in some other coin. Ammu, humming her fishing song, with a nick on her knuckle.

Neela let out a long breath and said it aloud to the empty room. "It was the wrong morning. Thirty nights is plenty. I wanted a better one."

It was a good lie. It cost fifteen nights and a girl at an inn. She told it again anyway, and it sounded more like a plan. She got up and poured water from the kindi into the basin and scrubbed her hands to the wrist, over and over, until the skin went pink. The lantana came off, and the smell of him with it, clay and sesame oil and clean sweat, and then there was only water.

Men who are about to be killed flinch. She had seen it a dozen times. They flinch and they twist and they turn their heads at the last, whatever they have promised themselves. He had stood quite still and breathed out.

That did not go on the shelf with the rest. She had no shelf for it.

Above the east wall, Gopan's chain began to clank the hour, and she stood with her wet hands over the basin and counted every stroke.

# Chapter 12: Parrots

Devan put his hand to the back of his neck. The skin was still warm where hers had been.

She had had him.

He made himself say it plainly, the way he would write a report. She had come down the north steps with her hands empty. She had laid two fingers on the door of his skull, at the exact point, with her weight set behind the wrist. He had felt that weight gather. Three breaths. He had counted them, because counting was the only way not to turn round.

Then she had lifted her hand and told him his left was dropping.

It was. He had checked it four times since, alone in the pit with the lamps burning out, and she was right to the half-palm.

He had not decided to stand still. That was the part he disliked. The body had decided, and his mind had come up behind it and found itself holding a very bad wager. He could not say if he had been testing her. He could not say if he had wanted to be caught out.

For ten days he had watched her as a thing the guild had left in his bed. A blade with a woman's hands. A blade does not pull a strike. A blade has no mind to change at the last. He had looked at the woman on the ledge at dusk and thought only of the cat, and of the one word in his mouth that would close her in it for good.

But a thing that stops has a will. A will can be asked questions. A will can be bargained with.

He had one thing she needed, and it sat behind his teeth. She had one thing he needed, and it sat in her head: who had paid. He weighed it as he would a blade he did not like to carry. A man who held a word over a woman and called it a trade was doing what the guild did. He knew that.

He weighed it anyway.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


Janu was waiting in the kitchen passage, which she never did. She had a key on a cord in her fist, and she held it out the way she held out a cup to a child she suspected of being feverish.

"Asha's room," she said.

He did not take it at once. "Why now?"

"Because there is a wife in this house, and a house with a wife shouldn't keep a shut room like a grudge." Her mouth worked. "And because I can't be the one. I tried. Twice. I got to the door."

He took the key. The cord was warm from her hand.

"Open the shutters first," said Janu. "It's been dark in there since the rains she died. She never liked a dark room."
