She walked back to her pots without looking at him, and the pots took a beating.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


The room was at the end of the south passage, the last door before the wall. It smelled of dust and camphor and mice.

He opened the shutters. Light fell across a cot, a stool and a peg with a leather cap still hanging from it. He did not look at the cap. At the foot of the cot stood her chest, camphor wood, with a brass hasp gone green.

He sat on the floor in front of it. It seemed the only honest way to do it.

On top lay a slate. He knew it before he turned it over. Her first month at the fort she had written the name of every recruit on it, with his mother's village beside, because she said a man fights harder for a captain who knows where his mother lives. The chalk had faded to ghosts. *Unni, salt fish, lower village.* He read the ghost twice. The same boy, grown thin and tall, now held his gate.

Under the slate were notes. Forty or fifty, folded small, tied up with red thread. He slipped the thread and opened the top one, and there at the bottom was the bird.

She had signed every note with it. Five strokes: a round head, a hooked beak, one lazy tail. As a girl she had drawn it on the walls of the cistern house until Janu scrubbed them. The beak was always whole.

Something in his chest turned over and lay down again.

He read three of them. Reminders. A joke about Bhaskaran's boots. A list of what the garrison owed the baker. Then, in the bottom corner of the chest, wrapped in a square of oilcloth tied with the same red thread, he found the other thing.

A tally stick, split ash, notched for a cart. At its end a thumb of old wax.

He did not touch the wax at first. He held the stick by the oilcloth and studied it in the light from the shutters. Dull brick red. A pepper sprig pressed in it. He lifted it to his nose, and under the dust and camphor there was still clove.

Council wax. And folded into the oilcloth, a scrap in her quick hand:

*First one. Not yet. Need a second to be sure. Tell nobody at the gate. —* and the bird.

He had never known she had it. He had thought the first token lost, taken by whoever had taken everything else.

From the pouch at his belt he drew the square of beeswax he carried everywhere now, the impression he had lifted from the dead raider's tally. He laid it beside hers in the light. Under the stem of the sprig, a dot with a small split beneath it. Then the other, the same dot, the same split.

One of three seals in the council's keeping, then and now. Whoever held it had been selling the pass while she was alive, and was selling it still.

He sat very still. The cistern chain began to clank the hour below, far off, as if in another house.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


"May I come in?"

She stood in the doorway with her hand flat on the frame, as she had stood at the lip of the pit. She was grey about the mouth. The hollow-eyed look had not left her since dawn.

"Janu said you'd be here," said Neela. "She said not to bring anything, and then she sent a plate. I left it in the passage." A pause. "I'll go if you say."

He folded the oilcloth over the tally and put the whole in his sleeve. It was not a lie. She had not asked.

"Come in."

She crossed the room and did not sit on the stool. She lowered herself to the floor opposite him, with the chest between them, and looked at the cap on the peg and the faded slate.

"Whose room?"

"My sister's. Asha." He said it as flatly as a ration count. "Four years younger than me. She was my second here."

"Janu said you'd lost someone."

"Janu says a great deal."

"So I've heard." The corner of her mouth moved and went still again. "Tell me about her. Or don't."

He had not said her name aloud in that room since the rains she died. He found it did not break anything.

"She was fearless with soldiers," he said. "Forty men on a muddy field, and she'd give the order like a woman asking for salt, and they'd walk into a ford for her. She learned every recruit's mother's village in a month." He touched the slate. "She was afraid of the dark. Not of anything in it. Of the dark. She'd stand a night watch on the wall with a hundred spears behind her, then come in and ask Janu to leave a lamp."

"Everyone's afraid of something."

"Yes."

"What happened to her?"

"She was killed. Six years ago. Under contract."

He watched the words reach her. She did not move. It was a stillness he knew, because he had seen it in the pit this morning, the stillness of someone adding up a figure she did not want. He took it for what it looked like. A woman who had been sold into a house and had just learned that a wall in it had once been breached.

"Someone paid for it," she said, slowly.

"Someone did."

"How?"

"I won't answer that."

In his head, where it stayed, were the west stair at first light, and Janu's face when she came up from it. The long parallel wounds, deep and spaced wide. A bear's. Inside walls no bear had ever climbed.

Neela looked at him, and then she did what he had not expected. She left it alone. She did not press and she did not offer comfort she did not mean. She sat with her hands between her knees and let the question lie where he had put it, and the room did not feel emptier for it.

"May I?" She nodded at the notes.

"She wrote them to be read aloud."

She unfolded the nearest and tilted it to the light. Her eyes went down it. Then the thumb of her right hand came to rest on the signature, and stayed.

"She gives it a beak," she said.

"She always did. The one on the west stair has lost its own." He heard his voice go careful. "She said it was an insult to a bird, and drew it mended."

Neela's face emptied. The colour went out of it so fast that he half rose.

"You haven't eaten," he said.

"No. It's nothing. It's the morning." She waved the hand with the paper in it. "It's all right. Let me read."

She read. He sat back down.

Her lips moved on the last line, and then she laughed. It was a short, startled sound, surprised out of her, nothing like the dry noises she made at table. She put her fist against her mouth and said through it, "Oh, she's *good*."

"Which?"

"'Muthu says you're the best fighter in the pass. I told him you're the best at looking like one. I'll take your two best points on the seventh day, brother, and you may have my two worst.'"

Nobody had laughed in that room since she died. He put his own hand over his mouth, and under it his face did something he could not have stopped if he had tried. He let it. She had her head down over the paper and did not look.

"Did she win?" asked Neela.

"That seventh day? She took both points."

"Of course she did."

She folded the note on its old creases and set it back with the others, squared, the way a person puts away something that is not theirs. Then she asked, still looking at the thread.

"What would she want now?"

He could have said something pretty. He had a dozen.

"She'd want whoever sold the tokens found," he said. "Not hanged in a hurry. Found, and asked why. And she'd say I was doing it the slow way, alone, in the dark, because I'd decided no one else could carry it." He looked at the faded slate. "She'd be right. She was usually right. It was hard to live beside."

"Hard to live beside," Neela agreed, gravely, and he laughed again, once, through his nose.

They sat there. After some time he noticed that he had moved. He was no longer across the chest from her. His knee was nearly touching hers on the boards, and the chest stood at his shoulder, and he could not remember crossing the floor.

He did not move back. He was less alone in the room than he had been when he opened it, and he knew exactly why, and he knew what she was.

It was the second fact that made his chest ache.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


At dusk he went down to the padippura to count the watch.

The light had gone orange and low. In the gate yard a rider sat his horse with the palace green on his sleeve, Sudhi, who brought the royal post up every ninth day and took the fort's down. Muthu stood at his stirrup with a letter in his hand.

It was a plain fold, sealed in grey wax with no mark on it.

Muthu saw him coming across the cobbles. For half a breath he said nothing at all. Then the great laugh came out of him, the one that carried to the wall.

"My mother!" He held up the letter. "Don't look at me like that, Warden, it's the usual quarrel. She writes me once a month, I answer once a month, and every time she says her son will die of the pass, unfed and unmarried and unlettered. I'm the first captain in Varanad to be scolded by post."

"Unmarried?" said Devan.

"She hasn't heard about *yours*. I'll let her find out from the neighbours. Better a rumour than a letter." Muthu clapped the rider's leg. "Take it, Sudhi, she'll want it before the rains."

The rider tucked it into his satchel with the palace's own post and clicked his tongue to the horse.

Devan stood a moment. A plain seal, no mark, at the gate: Asha's last words to herself were still in his sleeve, and he made himself set them against the man in front of him. He had stood at Muthu's back for years. Muthu lied quiet. When the man had something to hide he went still as a stone in a stream, and this was the loudest laugh on the wall. That half-breath was a man embarrassed over a mother's scolding, nothing more. The wax in Devan's head was brick red. This was grey, and going to an old woman in Kodiyur.

"Tell her I'm sorry about the pass," said Devan.

"I'll tell her you're a hard man to feed." Muthu grinned. "Go and eat something."

He believed him. He went up.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


The chamber was empty. Neela was at supper in the hall, with Janu piling a plate in front of her; he could hear Janu's voice through the floor.

The shutters stood open on a last green strip of sky. He took the oilcloth from his sleeve and unfolded it. The tally lay in his palm. Beside it lay the last note from the chest, the one he had kept back and not shown her. He had found it folded under the camphor block at the very bottom, and it was not about boots.

*If you're reading this, I was right, and it's ugly. Say it anyway. The truth protects whoever finds it.* The bird, with its whole beak.

He read the line three times in the going light. It had not protected her. She had found it alone, in a fort that was too small for her, and told nobody at the gate, and it had come up the west stair after her in the dark.

He knew the shape of the offer now. On one side, his silence. On the other, the name of whoever had bought his death. Nothing more, and no lie in it.

The light went. Below, Janu's girls were lighting the evening lamps.

He folded the note into the oilcloth beside the tally and set both in the carved box under the bed. Then he lifted the bar, laid it across the brackets, and let it go. The wood knocked once, softly.

He would offer her the bargain. Once, and plainly, and knowing what it was.

# Chapter 13: The Cat on the Sill

The arrow only kissed him. That was why he rode home on it.

It came from the ridge above the second ford, a hundred paces off, and laid a line along the outside of his left forearm, shallow as a thorn scratch. Muthu's men ran down the two who bolted. Devan knotted a rag over the cut, gave the order to turn for the fort, and thought no more about it.

By the time the padippura came up out of the dust, the arm was a hot post. By the time he climbed down in the gate yard, Unni's face had gone strange and wide, and the cobbles had begun to lean.

"Warden?"

"It's nothing," said Devan, and sat down on the mounting block, because the ground was making another suggestion.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


By evening he had been bled twice.

Eravi, the fort's physician, was a stooped man with a voice like a dry gourd. He had opened the vein in Devan's right arm with great ceremony and filled a brass bowl. Now he stood over the bed with his hands folded, wearing the face of a man who has done everything and would like it noted. Red lines ran up the inside of the left arm toward the shoulder, thin as threads. The wound itself had gone the colour of a plum. Raiders smeared their arrowheads with dung for exactly this.

"It is in the blood now," Eravi said to Janu. "We draw what we can. Tomorrow's sunrise will tell."

"Tomorrow's sunrise," said Janu, "tells every fool in the country something. What does it tell *me*?"

Devan heard them as if through a wall of water. The ceiling swam. He knew the room, the bed, the kindi, the third shutter slat that never stopped creaking. He knew that dusk was coming, because his bones knew it. He tried to say so and got only a dry click.

Then the door opened, and the room changed its mind about the air.

Neela did not run. She walked in fast, with her sleeves already pushed to the elbow and her hair tied back with a strip of kasavu. She looked at the arm the way she had looked at his neck in the pit, as something with a place in it that mattered. It was not the same look.

"How much have you taken?" she asked.

"Two bowls, madam, a proper measure for—"

"He's had enough." She laid two fingers on the inside of his wrist. His pulse jumped under them, hard and sudden, and her eyes flicked to his face and away again. She counted under her breath. "He's thin in the pulse. You're draining the one thing he's fighting with."

"The poison sits in the blood, and bleeding—"

"Draws him down with it." She said it without heat. "Janu. I need a pot of water boiling hard, a clean cloth boiled with it, and toddy spirit, the sharp kind from the store. Turmeric and honey. A knife, the small one from the kitchen. Boil that too."

Janu looked at her. Devan saw it happen, through the swimming ceiling: the old woman's face, which had been braced for grief, found something to do instead.

"Chinna has the sharp toddy," Janu said. "I'll bring it myself."

"Tell Chinna it's for the warden. She'll hurry." Neela glanced at the door. "And have Raiu send up Kannan with whatever neem he can strip. Tell them thank you."

She said it as though it cost nothing and they would remember it. Devan thought he had known that about her already.

"Madam," Eravi began.

"Do what she says," said Devan.

It came out in a thick voice he hardly recognised. Every face in the room turned to him. He had not meant to give an order. He had meant to be quiet.

Neela's eyes met his, level in the failing light, and for a moment she looked as if she would say something. Then she looked away and took up the lamp.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


He lost some time.

He came back to the sound of her counting. She sat on the edge of the bed with his left arm across her knee, pressing the hollow behind his elbow with her thumb, then a place below the knee, then the inside of the other wrist. Marma. Healing points, the ones that pull heat down out of the body. He knew them. He had only ever used the other kind.

"Forty-one," she was saying, very low. "Forty-two. Forty-three."

Beneath the counting there was pain, and then there was the knife. He felt that only as a distant, orderly pressure, like a man being swept. The water was hot. The spirit was fire. She cut the dead edge from the wound and he heard a small sound go out of him that he would have preferred not to hear.

"I know," she said. "Forty-eight. It's nearly done. Forty-nine."

She was not speaking to him. She was speaking to the number. But he held on to it, and after a while the number became a rope.

Somewhere in that, the light behind his eyes went orange. Dusk.

His body had known it first. Then it came to the front of his mind with a horror so complete that for a moment he was clear.

The shutters were open.

Janu had opened them herself while he was gone. *Your father's rule*, she'd said. *A sick room breathes.* A last slab of copper sky lay across the floor, and the wide stone ledge outside the window stood out against it, empty and waiting.

He tried to say *bar them*. What came out was "No," and then, with great effort, "Janu."

"Hush," said Janu, from somewhere. "Hush, my heart. Lie still."

"Janu," said Neela. Her voice was easy and exact. "He'll take broth if it's thin. Would you? Not the first pot, the second. And ask Chinna for another jug of the boiled water, cooled. It will take a while."

Janu went. She went grumbling, which meant she was relieved to be given a job. The door closed.

Neela stood up.

For ten nights he had watched her do this through his lashes, with his face a sleeper's and his breath kept slow, and he had done it well. Tonight the fever had taken his lids away from him. They hung half open and would not be told. She had her back to him. She took something from a fold of cloth, a small corked flask, and drew the cork with her teeth.

Then she turned and looked at his face. He could not even close his eyes. She studied him for the length of one slow breath, the way she might look at a lamp to see whether it would burn till morning. If she read anything in the slit of his eyes, she did not show it. They were glazed, and the room was dim, and what she saw seemed to satisfy her. She turned away.

The resin went on at the throat and the inner wrists, the old pale ridges taking it. He smelled it across the room, burnt tamarind and pine sap. She climbed onto the ledge with her hands braced on the stone, and her shoulders drew together.

He had seen it ten times before tonight. It did not get easier. It got worse, the more of her he knew.

The bones moved. The spine bowed and lengthened. The sound was soft, a wet creak like a green branch bent, and then gold came up under the skin like a lamp turning, and she was gone, and the cat was there.

It stood in the last of the light. Its flanks were still pulling for air. It looked back at him over its shoulder, steady and level, and in the green-gold eyes was something so clearly her that he nearly said her name.

Instead he said nothing. There was only one word he could say, and it was the wrong one. It was right there in his mouth, whole, smooth as a stone in a sling.

On the sill the cat settled and curled its tail around its feet. It faced the room, not the hills.

Then the door opened.

"There, I've the second pot and Chinna says if—where's the girl gone?" Janu stopped. Her voice went high and thin. "Something's on the sill. There are eyes. Devan, there's an *animal*—"

The bowl fell. Broth ran across the boards.

"A great—" said Janu. "Mercy, it's the, it's the—"

The word came up in him like a swallowed coal.

It would have been so easy. He had the whole shape of it ready. His mouth began it without asking him: the tongue lifting to the roof, the first hard sound, *L*—

He bit down.

With everything he had, he bit on his own tongue, and the pain went through him white, and the taste of copper filled his mouth. The sound broke off against his teeth. The room sharpened. For a moment he could see Janu's hand on the doorframe and the dark shape on the ledge, and he knew exactly what he was holding.

"It's the cat," he said. It came out thick and wet and slurred through blood. "Leave the cat. Draw the curtain."

"That is no cat, Devan—"

"The *cat*, Janu." He had no strength for more. He put all of it into her name. "Draw the curtain."

She crossed the floor on stiff legs. She never took her eyes off the ledge, but her gaze stuck on the two points of light and slid off again, as people's gaze does. She dragged the cotton curtain across the window with a rattle of rings. The cat was gone behind it, and the shape of it, and the shine.

Janu stood there with her hand on the cloth, breathing hard.

"Eyes," she said, to no one. "Only eyes, in the dark. I'm an old fool. I've told those stories too long." She turned. "And where's that girl? She never said she was leaving."

"Sent her to sleep," said Devan. He made it flat and quick, the way a true thing sounds. His tongue was swelling. "Sit, Janu."

She sat. After a while she took his right hand, the bled one, and held it in both of hers.

He lay with blood pooling under his tongue, and he did not swallow it.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


The other one lay behind the curtain with her chin on her paws and listened.

The cloth had cut off the last of the sky. It did not cut off much else. The other one's ears took in every word in the room, and her nose took in the rest. Hot cloth, spirit, turmeric. Janu's old fear-sweat, sour and brave. And through all of it, blood.

Not from the arm. This was fresher, sharper, and it came from his mouth.

He was raving. She could hear the pulse in his throat tripping, and the words came thick and wrong. *The cat. Leave the cat. Draw it.* Then a stretch of nothing but breath, then *cat* again, and a slurred, sticky sound that began a word and gave it up. He'd bitten his tongue in the fever, she supposed. Men did. She had seen a man in the Resin House sheds, shaking with ague, chew his lip to ribbons.

He had told Janu she was asleep, too, in a voice so level it nearly fooled her. A fevered man's kindness, she thought. He did not like Janu to worry.

The cat. Janu's stories had put it in him, she thought. Or the notched ginger tom that walked the parapet in the mornings, whom the warden had been feeding off the table since the second week and had never mentioned. It was a small joke the house kept. He was raving about the tom.

She thought nothing more of it. She didn't have room.

The stone had been the plan: she was to be there by the middle watch. There was a flat of cut laterite in the pala grove on the second spur, and a cup in its top, and Ammu would be crouched at it by now with her ears flat, waiting for a mark. A report. *The warden is guarded; the opening is narrower than I said.* Twenty-nine nights. The hollow dawn hunger would come for her at the end of this one, as it had after every turning, and she had perhaps that many mornings left to finish. And Chathu's message, five days old, had said what would be done if the account stood open at the pass's closing.

She had come up on the ledge to go. She had stood there with the night full of hill scent and the stone a mile off. She had looked back into the room.

Under the pulse she had counted all evening, his breath had gone wrong. That was all. The wide, quick, shallow pull of it, the lines of red going up the arm. She had known that breath. She had heard it on a girl in the sheds, two nights before they carried her out.

A man dead of a raider's arrow needed no cat. No one would ask her anything. The guild would call it the world's work, and the account would close by itself, and she would be a widow with a seal in her lap and a name coming. She had weighed that on the ledge, quite coldly, with her tail twitching. She had weighed it and laid it down. Then she had sat.

It was the third time in two days she had not done the sensible thing. The other one had no shame about it. The other one only knew who was breathing in the room.

Janu slept at the second watch, upright on the stool with her hand still locked on his. Neela could hear the cistern chain clank the hours, and the dogs, and the creak of the third slat when the wind changed. Beside the bed, the kindi slowly warmed. Twice in the dark he said *cat* and once he said a name that was not hers, and it was *Asha*, and the other one lay with her eyes closed.

Toward dawn the sweat came.

She smelled it before she saw it: the sour break, the heat going out of him all at once. His breath slowed and deepened. The hitch came back into it, a third of the way through each long count, as small and steady as a thing she had memorised without knowing.

In the last dark, when the lanterns burned low, a jackal called from the second spur.

It was thin and far across the terraces. Three short yips. A pause. One long note, climbing.

The other one's ears turned toward it before she had decided anything. She and Ammu had made that call in the yard behind the sheds when they were eleven and nine. *Tomorrow, I will find you.* It had never once been used by someone who was not coming.

She did not answer. She could not have, with that throat. The call came again, longer, and went up and up, and then the hills took it.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


The grey came, and the change took her.

It pulled at the throat first. The bones shrank and slid, and she was on her hands and knees on cold stone, shaking, naked, with a hollow inside her where all the hunger of the night had gone to sit. The ledge scraped her palms. She got her cloth from the corner where she had left it folded and dragged it over her head.

Behind the curtain, the room smelled of nothing but a man asleep.

Janu had gone, leaving the stool crooked and a bowl of gruel cooling beside it. The lamp was out. In the grey light the warden lay on his back with the bandaged arm across his chest and his mouth a little open. A dark crust had dried at the corner of it.

Neela put the back of her hand to his forehead. Cool. Damp, and cool.

Her knees were swaying under her.

*Go*, she told herself. *Wash. Find Ammu. You missed the stone. Explain it.* Tomorrow, in the guild's tongue, meant whatever came after the dark you stood in. That meant this day. Ammu had waited all night with an empty cup and then come to the ridge to look. By the gate-bell she would be in Valli's market shawl, asking Bhaskaran for the warden's lady, and Neela would have to say what she had been doing instead.

She had a hundred sensible things to do, and the hollow place in her was louder than any of them. Her legs did not move. They folded, quite gently, and set her down on the boards beside the bed with her back against the frame and her knees drawn up.

The cistern chain clanked once. The first light came gold through the curtain's weave.

Above her shoulder, his right hand hung over the edge of the mattress, open, a finger's width from her hair. She felt it there as she'd feel a fire.

She did not touch the hand. She did not stand.

# Chapter 14: Varanad Coin

Neela heard the route before she understood it was an argument.

She was in the kitchen passage with a basin of boiled cloth. The warden's voice came through the hall door, level and a little too slow, like a man climbing stairs one at a time.

"Out the east postern before the first clank. Down to the second ford. Up through the lower narrows to the shelf, and home by the cart road before the day watch."

"No," said Muthu.

Muthu never said no. He said *Warden* and *come now* and *by my mother's pots*. The flat word sounded like a stranger's. Neela set the basin on the step and stood where the doorway shadow was deepest.

Devan sat at the long table with his left arm strapped across his chest. The brown of his face had gone grey underneath. Janu stood behind his chair with a cup of broth she kept lifting and lowering, as if the cup might argue for her.

"Eravi gave you a week in bed," said Muthu. His big face had no laugh in it. "Yesterday you sat down on a mounting block because the yard tilted."

"I sat because it was there."

"You were *put* down. By a poison. In front of the whole gate."

"The narrows are where Raman died." Devan said it without weight, the way he said every fact. "Spring, on my patrol, in single file under that shelf. His boy is nine. I'll not send eleven men through that cut and sit in a bed counting them."

"Then I lead it."

"You lead the second file."

"And if the arm opens on the shelf?"

"Then you'll have the pleasure of telling me so."

Neela did what she had learned to do in other people's houses. She listened to the road inside the sentence. East postern, first clank. The ford. The narrows. She had seen the cut from the east terraces, a notch in the far hills, and she knew the kind of going it held: a ledge of laterite along a cliff foot, one horse wide, the only dry way through. A rider on it could be reached from above by anyone with a bow and patience.

She caught herself measuring it. Her mind went quiet and cold and said, with no heat at all: *Twenty-eight nights. And a road that is nearly a gift.*

Valli had not come to the gate by the bell, either. Neela had watched it all morning with the patience she gave a lock. No grey shawl, no pepper trader's girl. She did not know what that meant, and she disliked not knowing more than she disliked the road.

"Madam."

Devan was looking at her. Muthu's ears went red.

"The whole passage heard you," said Neela. "I'm told the cistern chain did too."

"Then you'll have an opinion."

"Would it change the route?"

"No."

"Then I'll keep it for something that might." She crossed to him and looked at the strapping, not his face. The knot was Janu's, the dressing hers. It had held. "I'll see that arm tonight."

"I'll be in bed."

"Then I'll look at it in bed."

His mouth went still, the way it did when he was pleased and hiding it. Muthu barked a laugh in spite of himself. "She's said no to you in four lines, Warden. I've been at it eight years."


<center>\* \* \*</center>


By dusk he was in bed, flat on his back, his right hand on his chest and his eyes shut. Janu had banged the room into order and gone. The shutters were closed.

Neela lit no lamp. She unwound the dressing by feel and tilted the arm to the grey light at the slats. The red lines had faded to pink threads. The wound was clean at its edge. She wrapped it again, slowly, and he did not stir.

His breath hitched, a third of the way through the count, the way it always did. She found she had been waiting for it. *Chance*, she told herself, and put the thought away with the others.

She lifted the bar herself. She drew the curtain, took the flask from her cloth, and pulled the cork with her teeth. Resin at the throat and wrists. The burn, the old ridges taking it. She went up onto the ledge, and the dusk took her bones.

The other one came down the east terraces in the last of the light, low under the pepper vines, past the goat shed and the white goat who bit, and out over the wall by the lantana. She did not go to the stone. She went to the narrows.

She did not know why, and she did not ask. The other one only knew the hills were wide and the road in them was narrow, and a man would ride it at dawn.

The wind came up the cut, and with it the news. Cold fat. Bow-wax. Old ghee going rancid in a skin. Bronze, which smelled of nothing and so of something. Two men. No smoke.

She went up the back of the cliff by a chimney of broken laterite, belly low, and lay in the rocks twenty paces above the shelf.

The men had made a cold camp in a notch of the lip. Two bundled shapes in wool, backs to the stone. One sat awake with a bow unstrung across his knees. The other slept with his hand on a round thing wrapped in cloth. A mirror. The other one knew the shape, because Neela had been taught what a mirror was for. At first grey they would string the bows. When a column came along the shelf in single file, one of them would flash the count to whoever waited beyond the cut.

Neither had eaten. A man who plans to move at dawn does not spread his stores.

She lay there while the stars came out, and Neela made herself do the sum.

A warden with one good arm, riding at a walk on a ledge one horse wide, under two men who could not miss. Last night she had only needed to sit still while a fever did it. Tonight she needed only to lie still and let two strangers' arrows do it, with not a claw of hers on the work. The guild would call it the world's work and close the account. She would be a widow with a seal in her lap. No cage, no ache, no page read aloud at dusk.

It was the cleanest road she had been offered in twelve contracts.

Then the other side of the sum. The stone had stood empty last night. If she went to the narrows, it would stand empty again at the hour Ammu looked, a second cup with no mark in it, and Ammu would count that as she counted everything. Valli had not come to the gate. Whatever that meant, Neela would not be there to learn it. And Chathu's word hung over both: *after the writ lands, every day counts double.* Two days she could not spare, spent on a man she was paid to kill.

She counted it all, to the last coin. Every column said lie still.

The cistern chain came up faint from the fort, once, twice, and the stars wheeled. She did not move. Behind the sum came the names. They arrived without being asked, as they always did. Raiu and his cowlick. Unni, whose mother sold salt fish. Bhaskaran at the gate with his stubborn jaw. Eleven riders behind a man who would not send them where he would not go. Raman's nine-year-old, whom she had never met.

She had known every one of them by name for nearly two weeks. That was the trouble with her. The guild had given her a number, and she had spent her life refusing it.

The chain clanked again. Four hours to first light, then three.

*It is the contract*, she told herself. *He is mine. I will not have a stranger's arrow take what I was paid for.*

It was a lie, and it was a good one. She used it.

The watcher turned his head toward a loose stone that had not moved. The other one went over the lip.

It was short, and it was not clean. The man with the bow had time to open his mouth. The sleeper woke under her weight and got a hand up, and the blow took it away. A knife came out and scored her shoulder, hot and thin. Then there was no more knife. The wool heaped in the notch went still, and the wind took the smell of it down toward the ford.

A leather purse had burst under her forepaw. Coin ran across the rock, bright in the starlight, and the other one, mid-snarl, breathed it in.

New silver. Not the dull, thumbed smell of a coin that has been in a hundred pockets. This was sharp and clean, like a blade fresh from the stone, with oil still in the milling. She turned one over with a claw. A pepper sprig on one face. Varanad's sprig. The edge was cut so fine it nicked her pad.

Nobody had spent these. Nobody had even handled them.

She nosed the dead man's satchel open. More silver, wrapped in a rag. A scrap of paper. And a split-ash tally stick with a thumb of wax on its end, and from the wax, faint and unmistakable, clove.

She knew that smell. Janu's kitchen. The broken tally on the hall table, the day the patrol came in. Real council wax, poured to keep it from cracking in damp.

*Why would a raider carry that?* Neela asked, and the other one, who had no use for questions, took the strap in her teeth.

Neela did the thinking while the animal dragged. The warden had broken one tally open in the hall. One tally was a story; two were a case. Here was a second, and he could have it. That was one reason, and she let herself have it.

The other was colder. Left up here, the satchel would be found with two men mauled in a camp, and the fort would hear a cat-kill on its own hill and start asking what cat. A bag on the path made them raiders, and made the coin the story.

And it had a price. A bag lying where no bag should be told the warden someone had stood on this ledge before him. He was not a fool. He would ask who. She weighed that against a patrol riding past a dead camp and never learning what the scouts carried, and chose.

The path was a long way down. She dragged the satchel over the lip and down the scree, a hundred paces of slithering stone, and dropped it on the shelf at the exact place where the first horse would set its foot. Then she climbed back to the camp, and the bodies, and the bronze mirror still wrapped in its cloth. She left the mirror where it lay.

She meant to leave the men. The other one stood over them with her sides heaving.

*Yes*, said the hunger. It had no other word. It was the animal's, and so it was hers, and it came up through her like a tide.

*No*, said Neela.

It was the hardest thing she did that night. She turned her back on a meal still warm, put the wind in her face and walked away stiff-legged, with her jaws shut so hard her teeth ached. Every step was a thing she had to choose again. She had killed to keep a man alive. She would not also be the thing that ate them.

She went by the long way to the cleft in the rocks under the second spur, and on the way she passed the meeting stone. She stopped there, late, with blood drying on her shoulder, and dragged her cheek across the cup in its top. A scent-mark, no more. It would be a night old by the time Ammu read it, but it would be there.

The cleft held a bundle she had hidden that morning, because she had woken on the floor beside his bed and never meant to wake there again. A day cloth, a shawl, a water gourd. The guild taught it: *keep your dawn where you can reach it.* She lay down in the cleft with her nose in her paws, and shook, and let the night go by with its hunger unfed.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


The cut on her shoulder pulled first, a hot thread through the cold. Then the change took her.

She came back to her hands and knees on frozen rock, naked, with the sky going the colour of dishwater above her. Her teeth rattled. The knife cut had opened in the turning and bled a bright thin line down her arm. She dragged the cloth on over her head and wrapped the shawl twice round and could not feel her fingers on the knots.

There was nothing to eat, so she walked home.

Her feet bled quietly on the laterite between the pepper vines. A goat bit her hem. By the time the cistern chain clanked for the first time that she could count, she was on the east terrace, wrapped to the chin, with her back against the wall and the whole valley in front of her.

The narrows showed from here, a notch in the far hills like the bite in a flatbread. At the third clank, the line of riders went small and dark into it.

Then they stopped.

It was a long way off, and a small thing. A bunching, a spreading, one rider climbing off his horse. No flash came from the lip. Neela watched for it and it did not come, and the thought turned over in her belly. Whoever waited beyond the cut had been promised a signal. They would be wondering now why the mirror was dark. A man who wonders sends someone to look.

Birds were already turning above the lip of the cliff, slow circles, and then more of them. And on the far ridge beyond the cut, a dot moved. One man, coming over the skyline and down toward the dark camp while the column still stood bunched on the shelf. The scrub took him. She watched the place where he had gone and he did not come out.

She could do nothing. By day she was a woman on a wall with bleeding feet, and the ridge was a morning's climb away. Her hands shook so hard she sat on them.

The riders came home at midmorning by the cart road. She watched from the terrace above the gate yard as they rode through the padippura in a column that held its shape too carefully. No horn. No man down. Muthu rode in front with a leather satchel across his knees. Behind him Devan sat his horse with his bad arm strapped and his face the colour of ash. In his right hand he held something small and bright, turning it over with his thumb.

He did not look at the terrace. He lifted his head and looked at the hills. He looked at them for a long time, at the notch in the east where the kites were still turning, and then along the ridge toward the second spur, and then at nothing.

Neela stayed very still against her wall. A thin cut on her shoulder, a night of fasting in her belly, one clean road thrown in a ravine: she did not feel brave. She felt like a woman who had put her own foot through a floor.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


At supper she ate like a field hand.

Janu watched her go through the third plate and beamed. "There. Good. A wife should eat." She did not ask where Neela had been all day, and Neela did not tell her. The hall was loud with it anyway. The whole fort knew by noon.

"Two of them, mauled to the bone," said Muthu, tearing bread. "Bows, a signal mirror, a cold camp on the lip above the shelf. They were waiting for us, madam, I'd swear to it. And something got them first. A leopard, the men say, and I'll tell you, I've never seen a mark like—"

"A cat," said Devan.

He said it mildly, without looking up. Neela's hand stopped on the bread.

"A big one," said Muthu. "Fine. A *cat*."

"Left them, did it?" Janu said, and made a small sign against her chest. "Left the meat? Cats don't."

"This one did." Devan set a single coin on the table beside his plate. "Look at the edge."

Muthu picked it up, grumbling. Janu leaned. Neela did not need to lean. She knew what it looked like.

"Sharp," said Muthu. "So it's new. Loot, from some trader. Is that what you think?"

"I won't answer that."

Muthu opened his mouth and shut it. Devan took the coin back and turned it once under the lamp, and said nothing more. He had ridden home with a satchel and a second tally and a long silence, and he gave the hall an edge and a sharp coin and no more. A man laying out a trap does not describe it to the room. Neela's eyes went, without her leave, around the whole hall: Chinna at the kitchen door with her bad hip, the servers with their pots, the open door to the yard where anyone might stand and listen.

"So who killed the scouts, then?" said Muthu. "And why leave the bag where we'd walk on it?"

"I don't know."

"Well, if it's a friend, I'd like to meet him."

"I'd like to thank him," said Devan.

He reached for the dish of fried fish and, without a glance, pushed it toward Neela's side of the table. "The pepper-store cat has been busy," he said.

Janu snorted. "That ginger rogue? He hasn't caught anything since the fever year."

"He's been busy." Devan's eyes came up, level and brown, and rested on Neela for the space of one breath. "Someone should leave him a bowl of milk. He's earned it."

It was said lightly. His face was grave. The corner of his mouth might have moved, or might not.

Neela looked at him over the rim of her cup and turned the sentence like a coin to find its edge. A joke at the table, over Janu's fish. A cat, the whole fort's cat, the one who walked the parapet and was fed from his plate. Or something else, so lightly handed across that no one but her could have picked it up.

For twelve days she had read his face, and she knew most of its weather. This she could not read.

"I'll see he gets it," she said.

"Do," said Devan, and went back to his rice.

# Chapter 15: First Storm

The rain took the trail in the time it took to blink.

One breath, the other one had it: pony sweat, lamp oil, wet wool, two men and a pack animal threading the ridge track north of the narrows, a hundred paces ahead and in no hurry. The next breath the sky split from edge to edge and the water came down all at once. The smell went out of the world. Red mud, wet stone, torn leaf. Everything smelled of rain, which meant nothing smelled of anything.

She stood in the track and turned a full circle with her nose to the ground. Nothing.

Eight nights she had worked this ridge. Eight nights of lying in thorn above the cut, watching the beyond for whoever waited there with the other half of a bronze mirror's answer. Tonight, at last, the couriers had come out of the dark, a lamp hooded in a shirt, a pony stepping soft. They carried tokens. They had to. She had meant to follow them to the hand that took delivery.

Thunder cracked directly overhead. The other one flattened to the stones with her ears back and her whole hide twitching, and Neela, behind the animal's eyes, said the only dry thing she could think of.

*Well. That is the monsoon, then.*

She cast along the ridge, wide and then narrow. A hoofprint filled with water as she looked at it. Then another, and both were only holes. Frogs started up in every ditch at once, a screaming chorus. The pass would close with this. Not tonight, not this week, but the sky had said its first word, and she had twenty nights left to hear the rest.

The trail was gone, and it cost her something to admit it. She turned for home.

Then came the question of the hour, and she had nothing to measure it with. No stars. No moon. The cloud sat on the hills like a lid, and the dark it made was the same dark whether it was the second watch or the fifth. The animal had never kept time. Her own body kept it for her, a slow dull ache in the bones, and tonight the ache was drowned in cold.

Two hours of night left, she decided. Perhaps a little more.

Her cache lay under the second spur, in the cleft where she had hidden a cloth and a gourd. It was a long climb the wrong way up the slope, and by now the cleft would be a gutter running red. The ledge was nearer. Two hours was plenty for the ledge, she told herself, and she ran for it.

Down the ridge to the cut, across the scree on feet that skidded, along the base of the cliff where the shelf ran, and the water ran with her, ankle-deep and loud. Once she went down on her side in a gully, and the cold slapped the breath out of her. She heaved up and ran on. The fort came out of the weather as a darker block on dark. Laterite walls. A bar of weak yellow where the padippura lamp burned.

By the lantana it came. Not the ache now. A pull, low in the throat. A drag at the bones of her forelegs, as if someone far off had taken hold of a rope tied to her.

*No*, said Neela. *Not yet. Not yet.*

First light did not ask. Behind the cloud, past the east hills, the sun had come up on its ordinary schedule, and under the rain the sky had gone from black to the colour of wet slate. She had misjudged the hour by one, and then by more. The mistake was hers, and the spur was now a world away.

She went up the terraces low, as fast as the slope allowed. The pepper vines slapped her face. A goat shouted from the shed. Pepper, the white one, who bit. On the parapet above, the ginger tom sat out the storm under an overhang with his notched ear flat, and watched her go up the wall with deep personal contempt.

The change came on in earnest at the base of the wall. She climbed it with her claws going soft on the stone.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


The ledge was still a reach above her when her front paw became a hand.

The pad flattened and the claws drew in with a tearing ache, the knuckles lengthening, and she was clinging to the lip of the sill by fingers that were not quite fingers. Fur fell off her in wet clumps and stuck to the stone. The spine was the worst. It unbent from the middle, one bone at a time, so that she was half a cat and half a woman on the ledge outside the warden's window. She could not do this part faster. She could only get in out of the rain.

The shutter stood ajar, as she had left it. The third slat gave its little creak when she shouldered it wide.

She got her chest over the sill and her head up, gasping, and saw the room.

A lamp was lit. That was the first wrong thing. It was turned low on the chest at the foot of the bed, throwing a small yellow room inside the larger grey one. The second wrong thing was the bed. The quilt lay smooth, unslept. And on the edge of it, his boots flat on the boards and his forearms on his knees, sat Devan, fully dressed.

His eyes were open.

Not through the lashes. Not a sleeper's face. He looked straight at her, the way he looked at a ledger on a hard day, a thing to be taken in whole.

She could do nothing but finish.

The rest took perhaps a minute, and it was the worst minute she had ever spent in her skin. The fur went. The gold under it dimmed and shrank to skin, goosefleshed, shuddering. The cat's long jaw drew back into a face. Her breath broke from a rasp into something that nearly had words in it. She dragged herself over the sill and down onto the cold boards, on hands and knees, and the cloth she kept folded in the corner was an arm's length away and might as well have been in Kodiyur.

He said nothing. He sat very still, hands loose and open between his knees, and watched the whole of it without looking away. When the last of the fur came out of her wrist in one wet strand and fell, his eyes went to the strand and stayed there a moment. Then they came back up.

Only when she was wholly a woman, naked on the boards with her teeth knocking, did he speak.

"The cloth is where you left it," he said.

She took it. Her hands were not steady and she got it over her head on the second try, and when her face came out of the neck of it she found he was looking at the shutter. At the third slat. Politely, as a man looks away from a stranger changing in a coach.

Her tongue was a strip of dry leather. She asked the only question she had.

"How long?"

"Since the wedding night." His voice was low and even. "You went out as soon as I lay down. I was awake. I have been awake for every one you made here. Twenty days."

Neela sat on her heels and let the number go through her.

*Twenty.* She counted back and found the third slat in every one. Every dusk at this window, the bar lifted, the resin burning. Every dawn she had come home to it, with fur coming off in handfuls and him a body's length away, his breath slowed to a sleeper's. Not all the dawns. Some mornings she had turned back in the cleft and walked up the terraces in daylight, and he had been out at the pit or on the road, and she had thought herself clever. It made no difference. He had been awake for the dusks, and a dusk was enough.

The hitch. A third of the way through the long count, every time. She had filed it under chance, on the day it fell on the exact beat of her laugh, and had congratulated herself on her thoroughness.

And that supper eight nights ago, over Janu's fish. *Someone should leave him a bowl of milk. He's earned it.* The corner of his mouth that might have moved or might not.

She had turned that sentence over like a coin, looking for its edge. It had one. She had simply not been willing to cut herself on it.

"Why tonight?" she said. It was a coward's question, a way to stay on the doorstep of the answer. She asked it anyway.

"The rain," said Devan. "You were late. I wanted you to come in to a lit room."

She looked at the lamp. It stood on the chest where the dark should have been, turned low, the way one is left for someone coming in from weather. She did not know what to do with that, so she put it down beside the rest.

"You were asleep," she said.

"I was not."

The cold went all the way in.

Witnesses. She had never needed to count them. There were two, and they were hers: Chathu, who wrote her page in resin-ink in a locked room on the day she first turned, and Ammu, who was fourteen and shaking and held her hand through it. Chathu, who read pages aloud at dusk to women who owed him. Ammu, who had never said the word and never would. Two, for eleven years.

And a man on the edge of a bed, with his own sword lying on the floor on her side of it. Hilt toward her, every night. It had lain there so long she had stopped seeing it. Now she saw the worn grip, and the whole of the last twenty days tipped toward it.

*Three.*

Her body moved before her mind did. She rolled, shoulder and hip, along the boards to the end of the bed, away from the lamp, and her right arm went down into the gap between mattress and frame where the knife lay. Guild steel, plain, six inches. The grip came into her palm cold as the night it had lain through, and as familiar as her own wrist. She came up on one knee with it in her hand.

Devan let her.

She saw that as she rose. He had watched her cross the floor and had not shifted a boot. Now she was an arm and a half from him, near enough to count the pulse in his throat. The place under the ear. A marma strike could do it with no blade at all, one thumb, and she was close enough.

And then what? A warden dead in his own bed at first light, his bride naked in the room, was no leopard kill. It was a murder with her name on it. The contract would fail whatever she did next. She would hang, or run with the guild behind her and the crown ahead, and Chathu would read her page at dusk all the same.

That was the reason, and it was a good one. The second was smaller, and she did not look at it. His hands had stayed open between his knees, and his eyes, steady and brown, stayed on her face and not on the knife.

"I thought you might," he said. "Do it, if you're going to. I'll say what I came to say first."

It was a thing he might have said about breakfast. Her pulse went up behind her ears. She did not lower the knife. She did not lift it, either.

"Say it, then."

"Someone paid for my death." He said it without drama, as a fact about weather. "The council pressed this match on me, and a bride came out of Kodiyur with a ward's name and nothing else, and she counts exits in a room she has been in a hundred times. I never took you for anything but what you are."

"And what's that?"

"Sent." He let it sit. "Here is what I can offer. What I saw, I keep. To the household, to the fort, to anyone, at any hour. I swear it on Asha's name. And I say nothing in your hearing, ever, after dark. Not in anger. Not in my sleep."

Her mouth was open. She shut it.

"For a price," she said.

"For a name. Whoever bought it." He kept his weight where it was. "The raiders hit the coast villages before the pass shuts, and whoever sells them tokens sells them my road. Raman's boy is nine. His father is under that shelf. I need the man, and I'm giving you the only thing I have that you want. I will make the offer once. I won't make it twice, and I won't pretend it's kindness."

Rain drummed on the shutter, the cistern chain clanked over it, and her own breath rasped under both.

*There it is*, said the cold animal sense that had kept her alive through eleven contracts. *Not a blow. A bargain. A thread in the mouth.*

She had known it would come. Every witness she had ever had held the word, and every one of them had put it in a bargain sooner or later. Chathu's tin of jaggery sweets. *A gift is the start of a bill.* This one had only taken longer than most.

"I don't have a name," she said.

His face did not change. "The guild never tells."

"The guild never tells." She wet her lips. "You know that already. You knew it when you asked."

"I hoped."

"Hope is cheap." Her voice was hoarse but it did not shake, and she was grateful for that. "Suppose I did have it. Suppose it was written on my arm. I wouldn't buy my life from a man who can take it with one word."

The room went quiet.

Whatever he gave her, he could take. Whatever he kept silent about, he *kept*. She had been owned for twenty-one years by a man who called it mercy.

He looked at her a long while. Then he nodded once, slowly, like a man setting down something heavy.

"That is fair," he said.

"It isn't fair. It's only what it is."

"Then we both know where we stand."

Devan rose carefully, favouring the mended arm. She was braced against the bedpost with the knife held low before she knew she had moved. He saw it. He saw all of it, and made no show of seeing.

At a respectful distance, the way one gives room to a dog of uncertain temper, he went by her. At the door he stopped with his hand on the latch and said, without turning, "You should eat. After a night like that. Janu will have rice up soon."

"Do you want me fed so I'll be useful?" The words were out before she could swallow them.

"I want you fed," said Devan, and went out.

The door closed softly behind him. She heard his feet go down the passage at the same unhurried pace, never breaking stride. He had stepped over the sword where it lay and left it there. He would go to the kalari with nothing, as he did every dawn.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


Neela stayed where she was until her legs gave.

Then she went down onto the sill and sat with her knees against her chest and the knife in her fist and the shutters wide behind her. The rain blew in. It came sideways through the gap and over her shoulders and down the cloth, and she did not close them. A closed shutter had been a lie for twenty days, and she wanted, for a little while, to sit in the weather and be told the truth by it.

Her shaking was real, the great ugly shudder she had only ever had after a bad turning. It was not the cold. It was not the hunger either, though that sat in her hollow as always.

She made herself do it the way she did a lock.

*Exits.* The door he had gone through. The window, and a drop of thirty feet. The knife.

*Breaths.* She counted eight, lost the number, and began again.

*What do I know?*

That he had seen her turn, at least twenty times, and said nothing. That he had let her cross a floor with a blade and kept his hands open. That he had sat up through the last of the dark with a lamp lit, waiting for her to come home. That he had held his tongue until the last strand of gold was out of her and the sky was grey and the word could do her no harm. That he had *known* when that was.

A hunter knew that. A hunter would know to the minute, and wait, and have a kind of grace about waiting.

She tried the other reading and could not hold it. It slid out of her hand. The man who closed the shutters on her, the man who fed a cat. She could not afford it. Every kind thing that came to mind was also, from another side, a very good trap.

*He offered the thing once and I said no.* She sat with that. *And he said, "That is fair."*

It was the worst thing he could have said. She turned it over and found no seam. She looked at the third slat, at the sword hilt lying on the boards, and could not tell which of them had been keeping watch on her.

Under the rain, round the curve of the east wall where she could not see, something began. Bare feet on packed earth, a soft rhythmic scuff, then the slap of a palm on a bare shoulder. A thread of sesame oil came up the passage on the wet air, from a lamp lit somewhere below. She knew the sound. She had heard it in her sleep on the mornings she slept at all, and taken it for the dream-noises of a restless house.

He was drilling. In weather like this. With one good arm, and no one to strike at but the air.

The cistern chain clanked, and she counted it. She sat on the sill with the rain at her back and the knife in her hand and her whole life lying in another person's mouth, and could not tell whether the man in the pit was practising or waiting.

# Chapter 16: Terms Withdrawn

Devan stood in the middle of the pit with the practice staff in his good hand and could not finish the form.

He had come down after leaving her, with her voice still in his ears, and begun as he always did. Plant the left foot. Drop the weight. Let the staff speak. For an hour his body did it by rote while the rain beat the red earth to paste and the lamps guttered in their niches. He wondered, once, whether the slap of his palm on his shoulder carried round the curve of the wall to the west window. He did not know where she was. He did not let himself guess.

Then the rain thinned to a drizzle, and in the quiet something surfaced that he had not thought of in twelve years. His left foot stayed where it was, halfway through the third form. The strapped arm ached with each breath. That was not the trouble.

*I will make the offer once. I won't make it twice.*

He heard it in his own voice, level and unhurried, and then he heard it in someone else's.

At the border school, in his second year, an instructor had read the class a letter taken from a dead guild courier. It had been addressed to an odiyan who had balked. The letter was kind. That was what made the room go quiet. *Your silence about the matter pleases me, child, and I shall be pleased to go on keeping mine. I expect you will find a kindness of your own to return.* The instructor had folded it and said only, "That is how they do it. Nobody is ever struck. A debt is simply mentioned."

Devan had been twenty. He had thought it the vilest thing he had ever heard read aloud.

He set the staff's butt in the mud and leaned on it.

The offer had been meant as honesty. He had told her exactly what he would give and exactly what he wanted, and named it plain, so she could refuse it plain. And she had. *I wouldn't buy my life from a man who can take it with one word.* She had said it with a knife in her hand and the fur still drying on the boards. It was the truest thing anyone had said to him in eight years.

His silence for her name. A kindness, and a debt mentioned. A word kept behind his teeth for the day it became useful.

For eight years he had laid his hands on no living body, and he had told himself the rule was about his hands. Then came the west stair, the morning Janu found Asha, with Janu's scream rising from the court below and his sister's blood gone brown on the step. He had stood there and made a second vow, the one that stretched the first. *Whatever I am, I will not be the one who holds a throat.* A hand was not the only way to hold one. A word would do it, if it was the right word and you were the only man who could say it.

He had built a man around that vow. He had cut every kind of power off at the root so it could never be turned on anyone.

And at first light he had sat on the edge of his own bed, holding the one power he could not cut away, and put a price on it.

He had even been pleased with how fair it sounded.

"Warden?"

Muthu stood at the top of the north steps with a cloak over his head and a cheerful, careful face. "You've been stood there since the second clank. Gopan's laying wagers on whether you've died upright."

"I am considering."

"Considering what?"

"A mistake."

Muthu came down two steps. "Is it one of mine?"

"No." Devan looked at the lamps. "Mine. I'd like to mend it before dusk."

"Then I'll bring broth and not ask." Muthu went back up, and at the rim he stopped. "For what it's worth, Warden, you've the look of a man who has decided."

Devan hadn't. But when Muthu was gone he found he had planted his left foot. He ran the third form through once, badly, one arm tucked against his ribs, and stopped with his answer clear.

You cannot hand a woman her own cage back with terms attached. You hand it over, or you do not.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


At dusk the room was grey, and the shutters stood open on a sky the colour of wet ash. Rain tapped the sill. The cistern chain clanked once, far below.

She sat on the chest at the foot of the bed, her back to the wall, where she could see both the door and the window. Her hair was wet. She had eaten; the rice bowl was clean and set aside. The knife lay across her knee. The cork flask sat beside her, and she was not looking at it.

He stopped inside the door and did not come farther.

"Before the light goes," he said. "I have something to take back."

Her eyes were on his hands. He turned them palm out, empty, and then felt foolish and let them hang.

"This morning I offered you my silence for a name." He kept his voice flat. It was the only way he knew to say a hard thing. "I called it a bargain. It wasn't. It was what the guild does. A word held over a person, and a price named for not saying it. I heard myself doing it and I did not stop. I have been trying all day to find a gentler way of putting that, and there isn't one."

She did not move. The rain went on.

"I withdraw it. The whole of it. There's no name to pay, now or later, and no favour owed. I won't ask for the name as the cost of anything." He drew a breath. "What I saw, I keep. That isn't a gift with a string. It's yours. You don't owe me for it, and I won't count it against you."

"You can say it any night you like." Her voice was low and had no heat in it. She was laying the fact on the table like a card. "That's what it is. You know that."

"Yes." He did not look away. "That's the one thing I can't mend. I can't unwitness myself. I can only tell you I won't, and let you watch whether I do." He nodded at the window. "The shutters are yours. Barred, open, whatever you want. I won't touch them. I'll sleep, or not, and I won't watch."

He turned to the door. His hand found the latch.

"Why?"

It came sharp and quick from behind him, the question of a woman who had been holding it all day and had finally run out of room to hold it.

He stopped, his back to her and the latch cold under his thumb.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


"Because I don't want to own anyone."

Neela weighed the words, rapping at them the way she'd rap a wall for a hollow. They rang solid all the way through.

He had not turned round. He said it to the door, quietly, and then did turn, and she saw his face in the last of the light. It was the same face as ever, brown and spare and shut, with a tiredness at the eyes that had nothing to do with the arm.

"That isn't an answer," she said. "That's a motto."

"It's the whole of it." He shrugged with one shoulder. "I was a bad master this morning. I would rather not be a better one."

"Everyone wants something."

"I do." He said it at once. "I want the name. I'd be a liar to say otherwise, and I don't lie to you. I want it badly. But I won't buy it, and I won't get it by sitting on you."

"And if I never give it?"

"Then I'll go on wanting it." The corner of his mouth moved, and was gone. "It won't be the worst thing I've wanted and not had."

She waited for the rest. The clause. The small, soft *but.* In eleven contracts she had never met a gift that didn't arrive with one, and she watched his mouth the way she'd watch a lock for the click.

It did not come. He nodded, as if she had answered him, and went out. His feet went away down the passage, unhurried, as they had that morning.

She sat in the grey room with the knife across her knee.

*Reread it*, she told herself, and did, the way she'd go back over a house after a job. The first half. All of it, from the night of the wedding.

The shutters had gone up barred at dusk from the second night, so no servant came upstairs and saw what climbed the sill. She had called it a private man's habit. Bhaskaran's lads called it privacy, Gopan called it Janu's old tale, Janu called it the damp, and Chinna ran a wager on it at the kitchen door.

Then the hounds. Fourteen of them had run the yard from dusk to dawn since before he was warden, and he had kennelled them from the second night. A dog bays at a cat on a wall. She had noticed that, and linked it to *Rain* at dusk under a clear sky, the one thing he wouldn't say plainly. She had not followed the link to the end. The sentry above the window had been pulled a few nights on, so no lantern could hang two spans over the ledge.

There was the cat, too. *Someone should leave him a bowl of milk. He's earned it.* She had heard that line across Janu's fish and taken it for a joke. But he had said the word every night. In the fever he had said it over and over, with his lids half open and nothing in his head to guard him, and it was always *cat*. Never the other word, the one that locked a cage. A man could say *cat* a thousand times and no one in the fort would think to hunt anything on their own hill.

Raiu stood at the stair head after dusk, with the corridor lamp left unlit beside him, so that no one in the passage would see a shadow cross the window.

Last of all, Janu's stories. Janu had told one at supper once, of a man-eater of the pass that left a traveller's sandals on the road. She was halfway through it over the rice when Devan said, "Not tonight, Janu." He said it quietly, with his eyes on his bowl. Janu had gone red and stopped, and the tale had never come back.

And after every morning Neela came in hollow and shaking, a bowl of rice had been waiting. She had thought it was Janu being Janu. She had never once asked who told Janu.

Her breath had got short. She put her hand flat on the cold lid of the chest and counted until it was even.

Each of them was a kindness. All of them together were a fence built stone by stone around a single thing, so carefully that no one could see the thing without looking at the fence.

Or they were one other thing, if you stood where she'd stood for twenty-one years.

Chathu had never struck a new intake. She remembered that now. He learned which girl cried at night, and left a tin of warm sweets by her mat, and asked after her cough. He saw that she was fed, and kept her from the older girls, and was gentle for years. Every girl in the yard would have walked into a cage for him. Then he read the page.

A caged odiyan would guard a pass. A caged odiyan would be a weapon no council could argue with. The one thing in the fort worth more caged than killed was her.

*Twenty nights*, said the quiet voice. *Nineteen now. He needs only wait for one dusk when you are most useful, and say it.*

And today he had given back the one thing she could use against him. He had made himself harmless in her sight. If that was Chathu's craft, it was the finest she had seen.

She couldn't prove it. That was what she hated. She couldn't prove it, and she could not disprove it either, and he had stood in the door and said *I want the name* in the same level voice he had used to say *rain*.

So there were two mistakes open to her. If she trusted him and was wrong, she lost her life, and Ammu's behind it, and the cage for every sister who ever took a kindly word at face value. If she distrusted him and he was honest, she lost only this, whatever this was. A man at a door who asked for nothing.

The first mistake ended her. The other only hurt. She would make the one that hurt, and make it on purpose, and she would not call it anything else.

A single night could not prove mercy. A patient man would behave beautifully tonight too. But tonight his word could not touch her, because with no resin on her there would be no other one to cage. What she would test was her body: a woman lying down human beside a stronger man who was awake. Would the sword move? Would a hand come near her? Would he reach for the knife in the gap by her hip?

She picked up the flask and held it to the window. The light was nearly gone. The cork sat tight in the neck, the bitter smell of scorched tamarind leaking round it.

It had a price, and she made herself read it off. No resin meant no other one on the second spur, and no report left in the cup of the meeting stone. She had missed it once already, on the fever night, and her mark on the night of the narrows had come late. Chathu had said every day counted double now. A second bare cup would tell Ammu that something was wrong, and Ammu would come asking. Another night went unmarked against the moon, too, and the ache, nine or ten days off by her count of rationed nights, kept its own ledger.

She would pay it, and she corked the flask harder than she needed to and pushed it deep into the fold of her cloth.

The dark came on. The cistern chain clanked. The lamp stayed unlit on the chest. Somewhere in the passage a door closed, and some time after, the latch of their own door lifted, and she heard him come in.

She did not turn. She lay on her side, her face to the wall, in her day-shift, with her hands and throat clean. She had never felt so naked in her life. The knife lay in the gap between mattress and frame where it always did. She did not touch it, though her fingers wanted to, and she kept them curled under her chin.

Behind her the boards creaked. Something was set down on the floor with the soft knock of a steel pommel on wood. Hilt toward her. It had always been hilt toward her.

She could not tell any more if it was trust or a leash with a handle.

The bed shifted at the far edge. Sunset had come and gone, and her body noticed. It began as an itch deep in the old scars at throat and wrist, the skin expecting a burn it was owed. She pressed her wrists against her breastbone and breathed through it with her teeth together, and it faded. Craving, and nothing more. There was no resin on her for it to turn.

He lay down on his back, and the quilt rustled. She listened for the thing she had come to test. No hand. No creak of the sword lifted from the boards. No shift of weight toward the frame and the knife. He lay as far from her as the bed allowed, and he did not move at all.

His breath was too even. For a long time it ran smooth as a ruled line, with no catch in it anywhere, and she knew that was a man taking care. Then, a third of the way through a long count, the hitch came. Slow. Deliberate. Exactly where it had always been, and exactly as long.

The hitch had never been sleep. It had been him, all twenty nights, and now he was giving it to her on purpose, so she could count him.

So she counted. She lay still with her eyes open on the wall and counted that catch into the dark, over and over, and could not make the number come out to mercy or to patience.

Somewhere past midnight she counted something else. Grey was five hours off. At grey the watcher in the pass village would walk the hill's edge and read her posts, and find not one of them fresh. A second refusal, written down for Chathu in a stranger's hand. The cup at the stone would be bare, and Ammu would be climbing the spur by noon.

She closed her eyes on it, human, with her back to him and the knife untouched.

# Chapter 17: The Keeper's Letter

The first rule of the dark was that she could ask him anything.

Nobody had agreed to it. It had simply grown there on the second night, the way a path grows where enough feet have gone. She lay on her back with the lamp out and the shutters open to the rain, and he lay at the far edge of the bed with the sword on the boards on her side. For a long time neither of them spoke.

Then she said, to the ceiling, "Why is the hilt on my side?"

"So you never have to reach across me for anything."

She turned that over. It was exactly the sort of answer he gave. A fact, with a kindness folded inside it like a coin in a cloth, and no comment from him on either.

"You knew about the knife," she said.

"Under the mattress, your side. Since the second week." He breathed out. "I never moved it."

She had suspected as much. It still landed low in the belly. "Were you afraid? That first night."

"Yes."

She waited. In her old life, a man who said yes meant *no*, or *a little*, or *but I mastered it*.

"For about a breath," said Devan. "I know what the word does, and I thought, it will see me. Then it looked away, and went over the ledge like something that had done it a hundred times. After that I was afraid of the wrong thing."

"Which was?"

"That you'd look round."

Rain ticked on the sill. Somewhere below, the cistern chain clanked, and she counted it out of habit and lost the number.

"What would you do with the name?" she said. "If you had it."

"Take it to the high court at Kodiyur, with the seals in my hand." No pause. "I would not kill him. I'd like you to know that, since you will wonder."

"I wasn't going to."

"You were."

She had been. She let it go.

The third question she had carried all day, and it came out smaller than she had planned. "How did she die? Your sister."

The bed did not creak. His breath, which had the little hitch in it a third of the way through, did not hitch.

"I won't answer that," he said.

It was the second time he had refused her that question. Tonight the words sat in the dark as plainly as the sword on the boards.

She was glad. That was the ugly part. Something low in her went slack with relief, as if she had put a hand near a stove and been told not to. She didn't ask herself why. She lay and was ashamed of the gladness instead, which was easier.

"May I ask one?" he said.

"You can ask."

"I'm not asking for a price."

"I know." And she did, which was new. "Ask."

"What do you call it? When it isn't me saying *the cat*."

The room waited. The word sat in her mouth like a stone she could swallow or spit. She had never said it aloud, not to anyone, not to Ammu.

"The other one," she said.

"Thank you," said Devan, and no more. He did not turn to her. After a while his breath went long, then came the small hitch, and she knew he was awake and letting her hear it.

Two nights without resin, and she'd paid the ledger for both. No hunting the ridge. A cup at the meeting stone left bare. The ache would start in a week if she kept on, a slow fever in the nails first. She had made the sums on the sill that evening, and the sums said this was foolish.

She lay in the dark and spent the night anyway.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


Sudhi came up the hill on the ninth day with the royal post in an oilcloth bag, a steaming pony, and a face the colour of a boiled prawn.

Neela knew the pony's name, because she knew everyone's. Biscuit. The groom was Appu, nine years old and in love with it. She stood on the lower terrace with a basket of pepper clippings on her hip, watching the rider hand his bag to Unni at the gate. Muthu came out of the guardroom with his big laugh and a grey-sealed letter ready in his fist.

He was talking about his mother. He was always talking about his mother. Sudhi took the letter without a word, tucked it into the bag, and rode on to the stable to be fed.

*Plain grey wax, no mark.* She put it on the shelf in her head where she kept things she didn't yet know how to use.

Devan took his own post at the gate and said nothing all afternoon. At dusk he came up, shut the door behind him, and lit the lamp himself. He stood with one letter, three sheets, and a face that was too still.

"This is addressed to me," he said. "It speaks of you. I would rather you heard it in my voice than Janu's." He held it out so she could see the seal. "Council wax."

It was brick red, with the pepper sprig pressed deep. She caught the smell when he cracked it, a faint sweet note of clove.

"Read," she said. "All of it. Don't skip."

He stood by the lamp with the rain black in the window behind him, and read.

"*To my friend Devan, Warden of Kettamala, from his friend Ittan, Keeper of the Passes. Greetings, and may the rains find you well roofed.*"

Neela folded her arms. She began, without meaning to, to count.

"*My friend, I write in haste, which is why this runs to three pages. My friend, the council has voted what we all knew it must, that the Monkey Pass be sealed for the season. And I have asked, my friend, that I be the one to do it. A man ought to look upon the gate he locks. I shall climb to you before the last carts go down, a fortnight, perhaps less, if the roads allow. I ask only a dry bed and a plain supper, my friend. I know your Janu by reputation. I fear her plain suppers.*"

He read the joke flatly, as he read everything. It did not make it less of a joke.

"*I hear the pepper is heavy this year. I hear your hounds are well. My friend, I hear many things, and I keep them like an old woman keeps buttons, for no reason but the comfort of having them. And how does your bride, my friend? I think of her often. A young woman far from the coast, and the long rains coming on. Such a comfort to a house, my friend, a wife. Such a comfort, should anything befall the fort in the dark months, to know there is one within its walls.*" He turned a page. "*Give her my regards, my friend, and my hope that she is well and happy and not overwhelmed. Do not trouble to answer, my friend, for the roads are bad and I shall be with you soon enough. I remain, my friend, as ever, your friend, Ittan.*"

He lowered it.

"Eleven," said Neela.

"Eleven what?"

"*My friend.* Eleven times, and *your friend* at the close. Twelve." She tipped her head. "It's more than a man says to a friend, Devan. It's what he says to someone he's not sure of."

"Ittan writes like that to everyone. He's said *my friend* to the queen's cook." But he looked at the page again, and she saw him run his eye down it, counting. "Twelve," he agreed.

"And it asks after me. Not after the fort. After *me*, and what I am to the fort." She kept her voice level. "*Should anything befall.* Befall what? He doesn't say the warden. A man who writes three pages and doesn't write the word *you* there is choosing not to."
