"Or he is a fond fool with a lot of ink."

"Is he a fool?"

Devan was quiet. Then, slowly: "No."

"Who is he to you?"

"Keeper of the Passes. He has written me a kind letter every season for six years. Since the winter Asha died." He flipped the sheets over, as if the back might confess. "He has never once climbed this hill. The council sends a clerk to seal the pass, with a sack of lead wafers and a bad cold. Ittan says he is coming himself."

"Then I mark two things," said Neela. "First, that he climbs himself. Second, the comfort."

"The comfort."

"Comfort to whom? You don't send comfort to a man whose house is fine. You send it to a man whose house you've an eye on." She spread her hands. "I don't know what it means. I only know it was chosen."

He set the letter on the chest. For a moment she saw him weigh whether to say the next thing, and decide.

"Here is what I have. Three seals." He held up three fingers, the way a man tells off cattle. "The council keeps seven tally seals. Four are cut with a bar at the sprig's stem. Three are cut with a dot and a split under it. The forged tallies bear the dot. So it's one of three boxes, or a copy of one. Three keepers on the council. Each box passes through a clerk's hands, and clerks have wives and debts."

"And the keepers' names?"

"I have them." He didn't give them. She noted that he didn't, and noted too that he hadn't hidden it. "The Keeper's office holds one of the three."

She stilled.

"I'd be a fool to say that proves a thing," said Devan. "The Keeper's office holds a great deal. Boxes, ledgers, the key to the pass. If the pass is sold, it was sold from his desk or past it."

"And the fourth?"

"Fourth, a man called Sudhi."

She hadn't expected it from him in that voice. "The rider."

"He carries the palace post up and the fort's post down. He carried the sandalwood box. Anything that can be wrapped in a letter can ride in his bag, and no one looks in the bag of a man in palace green." He rubbed his thumb along the chest's edge. "I've watched his hands for three posts and seen nothing. He is a boy with a sore saddle. That doesn't make him clean."

"Muthu hands him letters."

"To his mother." Devan said it quickly, the way a man says a thing he has said to himself on a long night. "Muthu has stood at my back for eight years."

*And you, Devan, have not turned around to look.* She did not say it. She weighed it, though, with a small cold interest. He had no doubt of Muthu. It was the one place his eye did not go.

"A rider, or a keeper," she said. "Which do you think?"

"The seals. A rider is a thief. A keeper is a thing that has to be dug out."

"I'd say the letter." She nodded at the chest. "A man who sells passes by the cartload doesn't write. He sends a man with a purse and no signature. Only someone who wants it on record that he was kind would write *friend* twelve times."

"Or someone who is kind."

"Or that." She heard her own dry note and let it stand. "We'll see which, when he climbs."

Devan looked at her. She had expected the old, shut look. Instead he looked as if she had set a stone on a scale he'd been trying to balance for six years, and the needle had swung.

"We," he said.

She didn't answer, which was an answer of a kind.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


Later, past the middle watch, with the lamp out. The shutters open, the rain gone to a mist that smelled of red earth.

She stood at the window with the flask corked in her fist and did not open it. That made three nights. The cost of a fourth sat on her tongue like a bitter seed.

Devan lay with his eyes shut and his boots off, the whole bed between him and the sword, doing a very good impression of a man resting. She watched his mouth. She had caught herself at it all week, the way she watched a lock for the click, waiting for the lie to land.

"You haven't asked again," she said.

He didn't open his eyes. "Asked what?"

"The name. You said once. You said you wouldn't say it twice. Then you took it back and I thought you'd pick it up again as soon as I'd forgotten. It's been three days."

"I said no terms."

"You meant it?"

"I don't say a thing I don't mean."

"That's not an answer."

At last his eyes opened. They went to her, steady and brown, and stayed. "It is, though," he said. "If I ask, I'm asking for a price, however I put it. You told me what that is. I would rather lose the name than be the man you described."

She stood with the flask in her hand and felt a small, sharp disorder in the place where her plan for him lived. A hunter doesn't say that. A hunter says *take your time*. A hunter keeps the door ajar.

*Or says it, so you'll think he isn't one.*

She gave it up. She could not tell, and she was tired of not being able to tell, and the tiredness itself was a danger.

Out on the hill, thin and high, a sound began.

Three short yips. A pause long enough to count four. Then one long note, climbing, climbing, and held until it broke.

Her hand closed on the flask hard enough to hurt.

*Tomorrow. I will find you.*

She had made that call herself, at eleven, behind the sheds, with Ammu's skinny arm round her neck and the whole Resin House asleep. It was the only thing in her life that had been hers and Ammu's alone. Now it came over the terraces like a hook thrown across a river.

She did not move. Behind her, in the dark, Devan did not move either. His breath stopped for a count, then started up again, a shade slower.

She waited for him to ask. A man who knew what a jackal's yip was and what it was not, who had spent twenty nights learning the shape of her silences, would ask. And she had no lie ready. For the first time in eleven contracts, she hadn't built one.

"That's not a jackal," Devan said.

"No."

He said nothing more.

She turned around. He lay as he had lain, on his back, one arm across his ribs, eyes on the ceiling beams. He had the face of a man who had decided not to look at something and would see it through.

"You're not going to ask," she said.

"I'd rather you told me. If you tell me." He kept his eyes upward. "I'll want you to be able to say *I won't answer that* to me as easily as I said it to you."

"Even if it's about someone who could hurt you."

"Especially then."

"That's not sensible."

"No," he agreed. "It's a term."

She laughed. It escaped her, a short, startled breath, and she covered her mouth with the back of her wrist, bangle cold against her lip. It was not a pleasant laugh. It was the laugh of a person who has been handed something and cannot find the pocket for it.

"Tomorrow," she said, "I'll be gone from the terraces. If Muthu asks."

"You went to the market. Parukutty's girl has a cough." He said it at once, to the beams. "It's even true. She has."

She stared at him. He had given her the lie before she asked, and chosen one that wasn't a lie.

"Go to sleep, Devan."

"I'm trying."

She did not take the resin. She lay down on her side of the bed with her back to the wall and her face to the room, where she could see the lamp-black shape of him and the pale line of the sword. Seventeen nights left. Seventeen was also the number Chathu had written over her page.

The cistern chain clanked. The mist drifted in at the window and lay on the boards like milk.

She lay awake a long time, and the worst of it was the wanting. It was the plainest want she had known in years. She wanted to give him something. Not the name. She didn't have the name, and if she'd had it she did not know that she could. She wanted to put a thing in his hand the way he had put the lie for the market in hers. A clean thing, with nothing behind it.

She went through what she had. A flask. A leopard's tooth in the waistband, the guild's. Eleven contracts that had ended in the dark. Not one of them was a gift.

*Ammu will find me tomorrow.* She held the thought still and looked at it. Ammu would ask where the reports had gone, and what kind of woman sleeps three nights without resin beside a man she has been sent to kill. And Neela would have to say something, and in the whole of her life she had never had trouble with that.

So she chose, there in the dark, what Ammu would get. The seal boxes. The Keeper's letter and his coming. The hill, the pass, the rider. All of it true, and all of it hers to spend. She would not give Ammu the man.

Across the dark the hitch came, a third of the way through his breath.

She counted it. It came again. She went on counting, and for once the number did not come out to a trap. It came out to a man lying awake so she would know he was there, and a woman lying awake so he would know she was.

She slid her hand into the gap between mattress and frame and closed it on the hilt. Six inches of plain guild steel, kept there since the wedding. She drew it out slowly, so the slats wouldn't ring, and leaned across the dark. She laid it on the boards on his side of the sword, hilt toward him.

The hitch stopped. She heard him not breathe.

"It's the only thing I have that's mine," she said, to the ceiling.

He did not touch it. He did not say thank you. After a long while his breath began again, and the hitch with it, and she shut her eyes with her hands empty for the first time since she was six.

# Chapter 18: Kirri's Anklet

Ammu was already at the meeting stone, sitting on it with her heels drumming the laterite, and she did not look like a girl who had come to chat.

Neela climbed the last of the second spur at the slow pace of a trader's ward gathering wild pepper leaf. She had told Muthu she was going down to Parukutty's, and she had gone, and the child's cough was real. Then she had walked the long way round, up through the pala trees, with a basket on her hip and a count running in her head. Sixteen nights. Her nails had begun to itch at the quick, the way they did before the ache.

"You're late, chechi," said Ammu. "And thin."

"And you're loud."

"Nobody's here but monkeys." But Ammu dropped her voice anyway. Her cover was a coast girl's blue cotton and a pepper trader's sack, and under it she looked sharp as a split reed. Her eyes went over Neela's wrists, her throat, the grey under her eyes. "Three nights, the cup was bare. Four, if I count the night of the fever. I came to the gate yesterday and the gate said you were abed. I thought, she's dead, or she's in love, and I couldn't decide which one I minded more."

"Neither."

"Chathu minds neither. He minds the days." Ammu pulled a twist of paper from her sleeve, read it with her lips moving, and tucked it away again. "He sent word with the last cart. Sixteen nights and the account is open. Every day counts double now, and he is *patient*, and you should remember what patience costs. Then he sent the sweets."

She held out a small tin. Neela knew its smell before it opened: warm jaggery and sesame. It had come before every job. She took it and put it in the basket without looking.

"Not hungry?"

"Not for his."

Ammu laughed, too fast. Then she began to hum, low and tuneless, *pull and pull, the tide is coming in,* and stopped when she heard herself.

"You only hum that when you're frightened," Neela said.

"I hum when I'm happy."

"You haven't been happy since you were nine."

Ammu picked at a chip in the stone. For a moment the quick talk ran out, and what was under it showed, thin and tired.

"Kirri swore," she said. "Do you remember? The night before they released her. She said, *I'll have my boat by the first monsoon, and the first thing off it is my anklet, in a fish-basket, so you'll know it's real.* A little silver anklet with the bells cut off, the one she never took off, not to bathe. She'd send it to us. A sign." She looked up. "Two years, chechi. I've written four times now to the address she gave. Nothing. Not a note, not a bad one. Nobody could be that busy with a boat."

"Messages get lost."

"Hers don't. She was the best liar in the House. She could send a letter through a fire." Ammu's voice cracked and she bit it back to something light. "Maybe she's fat and happy and forgotten us. Maybe she's sitting on a beach laughing. That's what I tell myself. Then I ask Chathu, and he says, *Kirri is where she wished to be, child,* and pats my hand."

Cold ran down Neela's arms. She had been given the same pat, with tins.

"Did he say where?"

"That's it. He never says where." Ammu shook herself. "Never mind. Release is a good thing. It's what we're here for. Twelve contracts and your page burns, and then there's you with your own name, and me after, and no one with a word in his mouth. So. Tell me you're close."

This was the moment. Neela had known it was coming since the jackal call, and she had still built nothing. She stood with the basket on her hip and looked at the one face in the world that had ever known all of her.

*Devan has seen me turn. Devan has watched me since the wedding night and held his tongue.*

Ammu would have it out of her in a breath, because it was Ammu, and then Ammu would do the sensible thing. She had been raised to it. A second witness in the world was not a gift. It was a hole in the wall, and holes were stopped.

"I'm close," said Neela.

"How close?"

"The pass shuts in sixteen days. I know his guards, his hours, the shutter slats. He has a bad arm and a worse temper."

"That's the fort. I asked about him." Ammu tipped her head. "Chinna at the kitchen door says the bride was seen at the window at dawn in her sleeping cloth. So why three bare nights, chechi? Why human, with him, in the bed?"

"To learn his sleep."

"You can learn a man's sleep from a window ledge."

"Not his waking." Neela made her voice dry and bored. "He wakes at the middle watch to walk the wall, and goes back. I wanted the pattern. If I'd smeared up every night I'd have missed it."

It was a good lie because it was nearly true. She watched it go into Ammu's face and watched Ammu turn it over, the way they'd both been taught.

"And he hasn't noticed anything?"

"What would he notice?"

"A woman who goes out at dusk and comes back at dawn with fur in her hair."

"He sleeps like a log."

"Does he." Ammu leaned in, grinning now, the old teasing grin. "Then who told you the hounds were kennelled? Who shut the shutters? I'm Valli at that kitchen door, chechi. I hear the whole fort talking. Chinna has wagers. Chinna has one on whether the warden's wife has tamed him or he has tamed her."

"A man with a fear of damp." Neela shifted the basket. "Why are you asking me this? Do you think I'm lying to you?"

She had learned as a girl how to turn a question like a blade. She hated how easily it came.

It landed too well. The grin went out of Ammu's face as if a hand had wiped it.

"Because I'm scared," she said. It came out flat and fast. "Because three bare nights is how you start to be caught. Because if anyone but us has seen you, if one person in that fort has watched you turn, then every night you sleep beside him is a night he can say it." She caught Neela's wrist. Her fingers were cold. "We're safe as long as nobody but us has seen. That's all. That's the whole of it. It has kept us alive twenty years. Tell me nobody's seen."

Neela looked at the hand on her wrist, at the scars on the inside of Ammu's, pale and ridged like her own.

She could not argue with it. That was the cruelty. It was true. It had been true when two orphans hid in the sheds, and when Vellan's bear-shape stood over them in the yard, and when other girls went into the kennels. Nobody but them had seen, and so nobody had said the word. Ammu had never spoken hers in all that time, and Neela had spent every year of it living on that silence.

"Nobody's seen," she said.

The lie had a taste, exactly like the resin on a bad night. Bitter, then burning, and then you went on.

Ammu's grip loosened. She let out a long breath and laughed at herself, and the laugh was real, which was the worst of it.

"Good. *Good.* I'm a fool. I've been sat in an inn for three weeks with nothing to do but think." She wiped her eyes with the heel of her hand. "Leave me a mark in the cup tomorrow night. Anything. A stone. I'll know."

"I'll try."

"Don't try, chechi. Do." She slid down off the stone. Then, light again, Valli the pepper girl once more: "That's a pretty gold bangle. Is it real?"

"It's real."

"Keep it." Ammu was already going, down through the pala trees, a quick blue shape between the trunks. At the bend she hummed three notes of the fishing song and stopped.

Neela stood a long time by the stone with the sweets tin in her basket. Where was Kirri? Maybe the boat was real, and the sea had taken it, and Chathu's pat on the hand was only a kind man sparing two girls a grief. Or Kirri had never left the Resin House at all. She could ask. A line in the cup, a question in the next message. But a girl who asked where a released sister had gone was a girl who doubted release, and Chathu kept a ledger of doubts as carefully as he kept the other kind.

She would not ask. She knew what that cost, and she paid it anyway. She stood and listened for the small silver sound of bells that had been cut off, then made herself walk home.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


By evening the rain had gone, and the hall at Kettamala smelled of wet cloth and frying onions and lamp oil.

Janu had commanded it. "Nobody's done anything with their hands but the sword for a month," she told the long room, rapping a ladle on a pot. "Hands want something else. Seven wells, and I'll have the new lady learn it."

Neela had expected to endure it. She had learned long ago to sit out a household's games with a polite face, counting the doors. But the hall was loud and warm, and the old woman ordered people about in a way so plainly loving that it was hard to stand outside it.

The board was a plank scratched with seven small hollows in a ring. Each player took seven grey pebbles from a bowl. You tossed them one by one at the wells from an arm's length, and the first to seat all seven won, but only a pebble that struck a wall of the hollow and *then* dropped counted, and you could knock a rival's stone out with a good flick.

"Skill," Janu said. "And a clean conscience. Cheats get a rap on the knuckle."

"Has anyone ever been rapped?" Neela asked.

"Every one of them. Muthu most."

"Slander," said Muthu, from the bench, with the big laugh already coming.

Across the board sat Devan. He had his bad arm propped on his knee and a face of grave attention, as if the game were a council. He took his seven pebbles from the bowl and set them in a neat row.

"You've played this before," said Neela.

"Once. I was eight." He considered a stone. "Asha beat me."

It was the first time he had said her name in the hall. Janu's ladle stopped. Then he flicked his pebble and it clipped the rim of the first well, spun, and dropped. "She cheated," he added, in his flat voice.

"She did *not*," said Janu, outraged, and the silence broke.

The game ran. Neela threw badly at first, on purpose, to learn the board. Then she saw it: the plank rocked a finger's width on its trestle if you leaned your elbow on the near edge. A pebble thrown in time with the rock dropped dead into the well. It was a trick for a cheap fairground. She did not even decide to do it. Her hand simply went.

Three wells. Four. Muthu groaned. Unni, who had been drafted from the gate, cheered with his mouth full.

Five. She lined up the sixth and leaned her elbow.

"Your elbow," said Devan.

"I'm resting it."

"On the plank." His eyes had not moved from her face. They were bright, and for a moment she did not recognise what was in them. "You lean when the board rocks. You've done it four times. I have been counting."

"Counting what?"

"Your elbow." He leaned forward, and she saw the corner of his mouth go, and keep going. "It's very good."

The hall had gone still to listen. Janu had a hand over her heart. Neela put the sixth pebble down in its well with great dignity and said, "I don't know what you mean."

"Then you won't mind if I throw."

He took his own seven. He did not lean on the plank. He leaned on the *trestle*, with his knee, at the far leg, and the whole board tipped by a hair. His pebble hit the wall of the well and dropped. Then the next. Then he flicked a third at her sixth and knocked it, cleanly, out of its hollow and into the rushes.

A pot lid rattled on its pot and settled. Nobody breathed over it.

"That," said Neela, "is a foul."

"It's a rule. Hands want something else."

She stared at him. He was grinning. Not the corner of the mouth, not the slow dry thing she had learned to read. A real grin, wide and boyish and astonished at itself, as though it had been stored in a drawer for eight years and had come out creased.

She laughed. It came up before she could stop it, a rough peal, and she put her hand over her mouth, and then took it away and let it go.

The hall roared. Muthu banged the bench with both fists and bellowed that he had *seen* it, he had seen the warden *cheat*, and he wanted it written in the chronicle. Unni was crying with laughter. Janu thumped Devan on the shoulder with the ladle, hard, and he took it with his eyes shut, shaking. Behind them, Chinna's shrill voice called for the wager to be settled.

"Rapped," Janu declared. "Knuckles, both of you."

Neela held out her hand. Devan held out his. The ladle came down on each in turn with a flat wooden clack, like a shutter slat dropping.

Under the noise, across the board, he turned his hand over and showed her the red stripe across his knuckles. He did not say anything. Neela turned hers up beside it, the same stripe, a finger's width lower, and he looked from one to the other as if they had matched a lock.

She had never had this. Not once, in eleven contracts and twenty-seven years. A room full of people, and no one counting the exits.

She was still smiling when she looked down and saw her bangle shining in the lamplight, and thought of Ammu's cold hand.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


Later, in the chamber, she sat on the chest with the shutters open on the dark.

He had gone down to see to the wall. She had said good night to a dozen people by name. Janu was Janu. Raiu was Raiu. Kannan, the goat-boy, had given her a pebble to keep, white as a tooth. She turned it in her fingers now.

The laugh was still in her chest like a coal. It was warm, and she did not trust it.

She did what she always did. She wrote it down in her head.

*Today I told Ammu nobody has seen me. I have told her small lies before, to keep us out of trouble. This is the first I have told her to keep someone else safe.*

It sat in the ledger of her mind in a neat, small hand. She looked at the line beneath it, the one she did not want to write.

*Tonight I laughed in a hall with a man who has seen me. It was the first time I have ever laughed for nothing.*

She read the two lines together, and did not strike out either.

She set the white pebble on the sill. Beside it stood the corked flask, where she had left it that morning. *Leave me a mark in the cup tomorrow night.* A mark meant the other one, out on the hill, with Ammu watching. It meant resin at dusk, and turning where Ammu's eyes could find her, and leaving behind the shutters he closed every evening and the sword he laid hilt-first on her side of the bed.

Sixteen nights. She laid her hand on the flask, and the glass was cold, and she sat there with her fingers closed round it until the cistern chain clanked.

# Chapter 19: Resin on the Rampart

The split reed lay across the cup of the meeting stone at dawn, and Neela did not need a note to read it.

*Show me.*

In the Resin House a reed across the cup meant one thing. Not a report on paper, not a tale told over sweets. The work itself, done where the sender could watch. Ammu had waited three weeks at an inn for a woman who slept three nights human beside her mark. She was done being told.

So at sunset Neela stood on the east rampart with the flask open and the cork between her teeth.

Fifteen nights. The sun had gone behind the keep and left the wall in blue shade. Beyond the terraces the second spur climbed, higher than the rampart, to the pala grove, a dark line of trees against the last of the sky. Somewhere in that line, Ammu lay in wait.

Neela had watched the pairs all evening. They passed at the far corner and swung inward, and east of the cistern housing the wall made a low shoulder. There she would have thirty counts with no one to see her go over. She had checked it twice.

She tipped the flask. The resin was black and slow and smelled of scorched tamarind. She drew it across her throat with two fingers, then over the inside of each wrist, along the old ridged scars.

*Sixty.*

The count began the way it always did, without her choosing. Cold on her skin first. Then the faintest heat, like a lamp wick lit a long way off.

*Fifty-six.* A door scraped in the man-high stone housing beside her, and Devan stepped out with chain oil on his hands.

He saw the flask. He saw her wrists. He did not look surprised, which was somehow worse than if he had.

"The pair on this stretch are at the padippura," he said. "Bhaskaran has a cart he doesn't like. It will hold them until the next clank."

*Fifty-one.* "You arranged that."

"You watched the east wall all through supper. Twice you looked at the shoulder and your lips moved." He wiped his hands on a rag. "I'd rather be the man who made the gap than the man who found out how big it was."

She ought to have been angry. The skin of her throat was too busy for anger.

"And the cistern?"

"Gopan's chain jams in damp. I had the wheel apart, and that part is true." One corner of his mouth moved. "It's also a very good place to stand."

*Forty-six.* The heat had reached her wrists and begun to climb. She corked the flask with a hand that did not shake, and was proud of it.

"My sister wouldn't come up here after dusk," Devan said. He said it to the dark over her shoulder, easily, the way a man mentions weather. "Asha. She'd walk into a crowd of drunk soldiers with a stick and a joke, but she'd go the long way round by the lit court rather than cross a dark yard. She counted the steps on the stairs. Under her breath, all the way down. She said numbers kept the dark from filling in."

Neela said nothing. Forty-three.

"You do it too," he said.

"I don't."

"Your mouth moves. At the window, at table, on the stair to the hall. Fourteen steps, and you take the turn on the eleventh." He looked at her then, not at the flask. "I noticed in the first week. I didn't say so, because I thought it was private."

"Forty," she said.

It was out of her mouth before she could stop it. He frowned.

"Forty what?"

"I'm counting down." Her voice came out dry, almost light, and she held on to that. "I smeared this before you came out. It takes about a minute. We're at forty."

His hands stopped. The rag hung from his fist. "Then you should be on the other side of that wall."

"Probably."

"What do you count?" he asked. "The rest of the time. I'd like to know."

It was the plainest question anyone had ever put to her. No bargain in it. No hook. She reached for the usual answer, the one she built for strangers, and found she had not made one for him.

*Thirty-six.*

"Exits," she said. "Steps. How many strides it takes a man to cross a room. How many breaths I've been holding." The heat had reached the corners of her jaw, and she swallowed. "Lately I count yours."

"Mine."

"It hitches. A third of the way through, when you're lying awake and pretending you aren't."

He was quiet for two of her counts. "I didn't know you counted it."

"I do."

"Thank you for telling me."

That was all. He didn't ask what she meant to do with the knowledge. He took it as he took the sword hilt, the kindness and the fact together, and left it where it lay.

She looked at him. She had been looking for weeks and had called it study. The faint stripe across his knuckles where Janu's ladle had landed. The oil under his nails. The set of his shoulders, braced for eight years against a blow that never came, and tonight, in the blue light, not quite braced.

*Thirty-one.* Half a minute, near enough. Her bones would start soon, and if she stayed in reach the turning would begin against his chest. He had watched it from the bed and from the dark of the chamber more times than she had known to count. He had never watched it this close, with his hands on her.

*Thirty.* The grove. Ammu in the trees, who had laid a reed across a stone at dawn and gone to sit with a lifetime of rules in her head. *We're safe as long as nobody but us has seen.* Whatever Ammu saw tonight, she would carry home to Chathu with a face full of love.

*Twenty-nine.* His mouth. A good mouth, quiet and careful, and it held the one word that could close a door on her for good. A man lost in a kiss might say anything. He might say it with his eyes shut and mean only her name.

*A mouth that is busy cannot say much.* She thought it with the dry bitterness she kept for her own cleverness. It was also the most honest thing she had thought all week.

Three reasons, and the sum came out with no remainder. She wanted it anyway. No debt on the far side of it, no plan, no tin of sweets.

*Twenty-eight.* She did not go to him. She went the other way, two paces to the housing door, and stood in its lee, where the thick laterite flank and the low parapet shoulder met. She had judged it from the wall-walk, level with the housing, and from there it was a wall of stone. No watcher in the thorn would see a thing.

Then she crossed the three steps between them.

He stayed where he was. She put one hand flat on his chest and felt his heart going under the linen, fast and uneven, nothing like the calm he showed the world. His hand came up to her face and stopped a finger's width from her skin. Waiting. Asking in the only way he knew, without a word.

"Yes," she said, and kissed him.

His hand landed light, as if testing whether ice would hold. Then it held. He kissed her back with the same patient attention he gave everything, a kalari master learning a new form. He tasted of pepper tea and cold metal. His thumb found the pulse under her jaw, the one the resin had already begun to heat, and stayed there.

She stopped counting, and knew it only because the number came back like a slap. *Twenty. Nineteen.* His breath caught on the hitch, a third of the way through, in her mouth. She almost laughed.

Then the hitch was gone, and his breath ran ragged. His free hand fisted in the cloth at her back and dragged her an inch closer. Then he drew back a hand's breadth, and she knew that look, the look of a man about to be sensible. "Neela, the resin—"

She pulled him down again and he stopped being sensible.

Her wrists were burning now. The ache climbed her forearms like warm wire. His other hand closed on hers, on the scarred inner skin, and the touch should have been agony and was instead the opposite, a hand laid on a wound and not flinching from it. He knew what the ridges were. He did not pull away from them.

*Twelve.*

And then it came. The first burn, at the throat, a white line drawn tight across the skin as if someone had finally tightened a thread. It took the breath out of her.

*Not here. Not in his hands.*

She tore her mouth free and stepped back out of the circle of his arm. His hand was still open in the air where her face had been.

"Neela—"

She ran.

The east stair was a narrow flight cut into the thickness of the wall, ten paces along the rampart. She took it at a sprint, boots slapping stone, the burn moving down her spine like a hot nail. At the stair head she looked back, because she couldn't not.

Devan was exactly where she had left him, hands open at his sides. His boots were planted like the wall itself. He was breathing hard and looking straight at her, and he did not take a single step.

A man who wanted to catch a woman would have followed. A man who wanted to keep her would have caught her wrist. Devan had stayed where he was, as if his own feet were the lock on the door.

She could not tell whether that was the kindest thing done for her in years, or the coldest. She noted that she could not tell, and she went down into the dark.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


Four turns of stair. A niche at the second, where the wall made a shelf for a lamp that Gopan never lit. She tore the day cloth off her shoulders and shoved it in the niche with the flask on top, and the burn took her at the knees.

It was never gentle. The bones went first, a long grinding shift in the back and hips, then the skull drawing forward, then the teeth. The scars at her throat and wrists turned to bands of fire. She dropped to her hands on the cold step, and her hands stopped being hands, and the stair-dark that had been black went suddenly grey and sharp and full of smells.

Lamp oil. Wet laterite. Chain grease. Pepper tea, still faintly, from a mouth she had left behind.

The other one shook herself and stood.

She went up the stair on soft pads, and the rampart opened ahead of her in a new language of sound. Far off, the cistern chain turned its slow wheel. Above, on the spur, the pala grove breathed. On the walk, ten paces from the stair head, a man stood with his back turned to the stair.

Devan had faced the wall. Not the stair, not the dark where the cat would come from. He stood looking out over the terraces with his shoulders squared, and he did not look round when she passed behind him. His hands were flat on the parapet as if he were holding it down. She went by his boots close enough to feel the warmth of him, and he did not say a word.

In the animal way that comes before thinking, she understood what the silence cost him.

The shoulder of the wall took her in two bounds, and she crouched on the stone.

The night came in whole. The terraces lay below, ranked in pale steps, and beyond them the spur rose to the trees. At the top of it, at the edge of the grove, where the thorn thickened, something sat very low and very still, and two eyes held the last of the light.

Green-gold. Round. Set close and unblinking, at the height of a dog sitting on its haunches.

A jackal's eyes.

They did not move. They did not turn to follow her up the wall, as a watcher's would, to catch the clean line of the leap. They stayed fixed on the housing door, ten paces back along the rampart, where a man stood alone with his hands on the stone and his mouth shut. Fixed on the spot where, not two hundred counts ago, there had been two people.

She saw her mistake from up here, as plainly as a slate wiped clean. She had judged the lee standing on the wall-walk, where the housing's flank was a wall. But the grove stood higher than the rampart, and Ammu had been looking down. The housing was only man-high, and the low parapet shoulder beside it hid nothing from above. A stone wall had only hidden her from herself.

Ammu had asked to be shown the work.

She had been shown the kiss.

The eyes held a long moment more. Then they went out, the way a lamp goes when a hand closes over it.

# Chapter 20: The Name Given Back

Ammu was sitting in the cleft under the second spur with Neela's shawl folded in her lap.

Neela stopped at the mouth of it, naked and shaking, still tasting the hollow of the turn-back. First light had dragged her out of the other one's skin on a bare ledge of rock, and she had crawled the last stretch on her hands. Her thighs trembled. Her mouth tasted of ash.

"Sit," said Ammu. She held out the shawl. "Before you fall."

Neela took it, wrapped it, and sat. The cache cloth and the gourd were where she had left them. Ammu had touched nothing else. That was a message too.

"You followed my scent."

"I followed your—" Ammu stopped before the noun, as they all did, even by day. Her mouth twisted. "Your scent. Straight from the wall to here. You never even looked back." She did not smile. "I was in the grove, chechi. All night. I watched the east wall."

Neela caught the swerve and said nothing about it. "Then you watched me work."

"I watched you kiss the mark."

It was not an accusation. Neela had braced for one and it did not come. Ammu's voice was low and unsteady, and her hands, which were never still, lay clenched on her knees.

"I told myself I was seeing wrong," Ammu said. "The light was bad. But I know your back. I know how you stand when you want something, with your weight on the toes. You've stood like that once in your life." She swallowed. "You put your hand on his chest. You didn't use it to find a rib."

"It was a way in. He's careful. A man who's kissing—"

"Don't." Ammu said it gently. "Don't do the blade thing with me. I'm not Chathu."

Neela shut her mouth.

"A mouth is where the word lives," Ammu said. "Whatever else he is, a husband has a mouth. You know what the House does with a girl who loves her mark? It doesn't beat her. It waits. It lets her get fond. Then it sells what she's fond of back to her, one piece at a time." Her eyes were bright and furious. "I saw Kirri get fond of Chathu. I'm not watching it again."

"It isn't that."

"Does he know what you are?"

Neela weighed the question like a coin she could not afford. If Ammu learned that Devan had watched her turn, Ammu's own rule would take over: *safe as long as nobody but us has seen*. A witness she could not unsee was a page she had to write. And Chathu had other eyes on this hill. They would not forgive a sister who sat on it. The truth would not cost Neela alone.

So it was a first lie, and she told it tired and smooth.

"He thinks I'm a bride who sleeps badly and walks at night."

Ammu's eyes narrowed. "He didn't turn round, chechi. I watched that too. You went past his boots, close enough to touch, and he stood there like a post."

That one Neela had not been ready for. She built the second lie as she spoke, out of what Ammu could have seen from the trees. "He heard something go by behind him. He didn't turn. He said, 'Go on, then,' into the dark, the way you do. You were too far up to hear it." She made herself shrug. "The fort tom walks that wall every night. Half the garrison feeds it."

Ammu looked at Neela's hands. They were wrapped in the shawl and quite still. Then she looked at her face.

"He didn't even turn his head?"

"Why would he? It's a cat on a wall."

Ammu searched her for a long moment. Neela let her, and kept her eyes steady, and hated how easy it was. At the end of it Ammu let out a breath, and the clenched hands loosened, and the relief in her face was so plain that it hurt to watch. She wanted it to be true, and she chose it. She took the lie the way a cold child takes a blanket, and did not look at its seams.

"All right," she said. "All right. Then there's time."

"How much?"

"One week." Ammu held up a finger, shaking. "I write nothing for one more week. Not what I saw on the rampart, not the three bare nights, nothing. If Chathu hears it from his other eyes first, he'll know I sat on it. So a week. In a week you either have a plan that ends in a body, or I write what I saw and you tell me I'm wrong, and I'll be so glad to be wrong."

"A week."

"Don't thank me."

"I wasn't going to."

That got a short, cracked laugh. Ammu pulled her knees up and began to hum, under her breath, and it was not the fishing song. It was slower, a heavy, rocking tune that Neela half knew from the tapping sheds, the kind the old men sang to keep the knife in time. Ammu's voice slipped into words without her seeming to notice:

*Tap the bark when the sun goes low,*
*let the black sap run and the lantern burn slow.*
*What the dusk has taken, the dusk will say,*
*the name given back at the closing of day.*

She stopped. Her face went blank, like a girl who has stepped on a stair that was not there.

"I never heard that last verse," Neela said.

"You were always counting, not listening."

"What does it mean? Given back how?"

"It's a song." Ammu rubbed her nose with the back of her wrist. "The tappers made them up to stay awake. They don't mean anything. Half of them are about goats."

"Ammu."

"It's only a song, chechi." She stood and brushed the dust from her blue cotton. Her eyes went to the sky, to the sun clearing the ridge, and her voice went quick and bright and wrong. "You'll want to hear the real news. I didn't come here to sing."

Neela waited. She had learned a long time ago that the way to hear a thing was to let someone avoid it for a little while first.

"Chathu's sent word again." Ammu's mouth thinned. "It came in with the last cart, while you were out. If the account is still open at the new moon, he sends Vellan."

The cold went through Neela slowly, from the cleft floor up.

"Vellan."

"Nine nights. Chathu says it's not a punishment, only a kindness, so the work gets done before the pass shuts and you don't have to spend the winter under his roof explaining yourself. He says it like he's offering you a seat." Ammu's hands twisted in her cloth. "Vellan doesn't come to talk, chechi."

Vellan, who carved small wooden birds for the youngest girls and left them on their sleeping mats. Vellan, who had stood over two children in the yard, big as a hut, and let them live.

"I know what he comes for," Neela said.

"Then do it," Ammu said, very low. "Or do something. Anything. Just don't let him find you stalling." She leaned down quickly and kissed Neela on the forehead, which she had not done since they were girls. "I'll leave an unsplit reed in the cup on the seventh night. If it's there, I've kept my word."

She went out into the morning and did not hum again.

Neela sat with the shawl round her and counted the ridges in the cleft wall. Nine. She counted them again and got nine.

*Nine nights to the new moon. A week of grace.* A reprieve bought with two lies, and Ammu's face, relieved and trusting her. And a bear walking toward the dark of the month.

She looked at her shaking hands, and then she began to dress.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


By dusk she had worked out three ways to say it, and discarded all of them.

The chamber was dim. Neela had closed the shutters herself before the light went, slat by slat, with the third one creaking as always. The corridor lamp outside was unlit. The fort was settling toward night in small sounds: the chain, a dog, Janu's voice scolding someone in the court below.

Devan had said only, at the evening meal, in front of Janu, "You didn't eat," and had put a plate of rice and fried pepper in front of her without asking. Now he stood by the bed, undoing his sword belt, and she saw him decide to wait for her to speak.

She had the flask in her hand. She had not opened it.

"I want you to promise me something," she said.

He laid the belt down. "If I can."

"Never to say it." The rasp of her own voice surprised her. "Not in anger. Not drunk. Not in sleep. You say you won't, and I believe you mean it. I want you to promise it. Never."

He looked at her for a long time. In the dim, his face was hard to read, but she had learned to read his hands, and they were very still.

"No," he said.

Her chest went tight. "No."

"I won't promise that." He came a step nearer, and he spoke slowly, because he was saying something he had clearly turned over many times. "If I promise never, and I mean it with all I have, I'm still promising what I can't hold. A fever doesn't ask. I said *cat* a hundred times, Janu told me, while I was out of my head, and the only thing I've been grateful for in eight years is that was the word I had." He drew a breath. "Sleep doesn't ask either. I can promise to try. I can promise I'll never say it awake. But you'd be hearing a promise that breaks the first night I'm ill, and you'd hear it in the dark."

"That's the point of a promise."

"No. The point is that you can lean on it." His jaw worked. "I'd rather give you something that holds."

She felt the old cold in her chest, the shape of every kindly voice that had said *trust me* with a debt behind it. She waited for the bargain.

It didn't come. Instead he put his hand in the pouch at his belt and drew out something small, and held it out in his open palm.

A river pebble. Smooth, grey-brown, no bigger than a plum stone.

"Gopan taught me this when I was sixteen," he said. "On the cistern wheel, the winter watch. Forty nights awake on a post with nothing to do, and old men talk in their sleep standing up. The old watchmen hold a stone in the cheek." He tucked it behind his molars, between tooth and cheek, and spoke around it. "Like thish."

It came out a soft, wet smear of sound. She heard the shape of words in it and no word at all.

She stared. "Say it. Properly."

"Can't." Another smear. He took the stone out and set it on his palm. "A man can mumble round a stone. He can hum. He can't make a clean word that anyone could mistake for one. Not asleep, not in a fever. Gopan tested it on me with a bucket of cold water." One corner of his mouth lifted, and went. "It's no promise. It's a plug in the mouth. But it's a thing that holds whether I'm brave or not."

"Every night?"

"Every night you smear." He said it flatly, as he said facts. "From tonight. I'll sleep badly with it. It'll go in at dusk and out at first light, when you're back. I'd sooner choke on a stone than on that word."

She had a white pebble of her own, in the pocket of her day cloth, a gift from a goat-boy. She dug it out. It was a little rounder than his and a lot whiter, and she held it up between two fingers.

"Try mine."

"Kannan's?"

"It's cleaner."

He laid his grey stone on the sill, took hers, wiped it on his sleeve with ridiculous gravity, and put it in his cheek. Then he looked at her, grim as a judge, and said, "Cashh."

She laughed.

It burst out of her before she knew it was coming, the same rough peal as in the hall, and she bent over the flask with her free hand on her knee. He raised his eyebrows at her, bulging one cheek like a boy hiding sweets, and said something that might have been *Cat* or might have been *Ought* or might have been the name of a goat.

"Stop," she said. "Stop, I'll drop it."

He took the stone out. His eyes were laughing, and his mouth was not, and then both were quiet.

And so was she. The laugh stopped in her all at once, like the cistern chain caught mid-clank.

She stood in the near dark with a stopped flask in her hand and a man in front of her holding a damp white pebble on his palm.

Eleven years the word had hung round her neck, hers to carry and hide. Chathu held it as a leash. Ammu held it as a silence too dangerous to break. Everyone else's mouth was only the place it could fall from.

No one had ever said, *This is mine.*

"Neela," he said quietly.

"I'm all right."

"You've gone white."

"I'm all right." She had to say it a second time. "It's stupid. I'd just never thought of it as something you could carry."

"It's heavy enough," he said. "I'd rather carry my share."

He did not ask her to say what that meant. He only held the white pebble out to her, and waited.

She took it. It was warm and wet against her palm.

"About the rampart," he said.

She went still.

"I stood where I was because you ran. Not because I didn't want to follow." He said it to her face, plainly, as he said everything. "I won't ask for it again at dusk, with resin on you and a word in my mouth. When you want it, you'll find me. In daylight, if you like."

"In daylight."

"I'm told it's allowed."

Outside, the cistern chain clanked once. The slats of the shutter glowed a last dull orange and went dark.

She crossed to the sill and set the flask down on his side of it, next to the grey stone, close enough that his hand could reach it as easily as hers. Then she uncorked it. The resin smelled, as always, of scorched tamarind. She drew it across her throat and her wrists.

Before the first heat came, she took his hand, turned it palm up, and pressed the white pebble into it. "Keep Kannan's. It's the clean one."

He closed his fingers over it. He put it behind his teeth, and sat on the floor with his back against the bed, facing the door.

*Fifty-nine,* she counted, and the burn began. *The name given back at the closing of day.*

# Chapter 21: The Captain at Twenty-Four

"Why won't you spar?"

Neela asked it without looking up, the way another woman might ask about the price of thread.

Rain had shut Kettamala since before dawn. It came down the red walls in sheets and filled the kalari pit to the knee, and Devan had taken refuge in the tack room beside it, where the thatch leaked in two places and he had set a bucket under each. He was restringing a bow. Neela had come down the east path under a sack, with a pot of tea and no explanation, and sat on an upturned feed bin as if she had always sat there.

Muthu had left a quarter-hour ago, streaming water, laughing. He had asked for his round on the seventh day, as he had every seventh day for eight years, and Devan had said no, as he had every time. Neela had watched the whole exchange over the rim of her cup.

"I'm not going to answer that yet," Devan said.

"Then we need a rule." She set the cup down. "A question for a question. You ask, I answer. I ask, you answer. Either of us can say *I won't answer* once, and the other lets it lie."

"Once a day?"

"Once. Total."

He tested the string against his thumb. He had spent half his life refusing to be asked things, and he found he was curious what it felt like to be asked by someone who offered to pay.

"Start small," he said. "Then I'll know what the price is."

She considered. "Ask."

"What did they feed you at the Resin House?"

Her mouth twitched. "Boiled millet. Dried fish on feast days. Before a contract, a tin of jaggery-sesame sweets, still warm." The twitch went flat. "I don't eat them now."

"Mine," she said. "Who fills the kindi?"

"I do."

"Every night?"

"Since the second week. Spout toward the pillow, because you reach for it left-handed in the dark." He said it to the bow. "You thought it was Janu's girls."

"I did."

"They'd have put it on my side."

She said nothing for a moment. The rain drummed on the thatch, and a drip landed in the left bucket with a sound like a coin.

"That's two," she said. "Ask."

He asked what she had been afraid of at the House, as a child. She said the kennels, and a big man's shadow in the yard, and being the last one picked for anything. She said it fast and bare, a list.

Her questions came sharper. Which of Janu's stories had made him ask her to stop? He told her: *the one about the man-eater of the pass that left a man's sandals on the road.* Which scar was the oldest? Whether he slept.

"Badly," he said. "Eight years."

"Since Asha."

"Before Asha." He ran the string through his fingers. "Since the ford."

She let that lie. It was his turn, and he had been wanting to ask one thing since the first week.

"Why do you take the turn on the eleventh step?"

She had been reaching for her cup. Her hand stopped.

"The stair to the hall," he said. "Fourteen steps. You're light on ten, and on the eleventh your whole body turns, as if you'd heard something there. Every time."

"I don't know."

"You don't—"

"I don't know." Her lips moved, a small shaped count with no sound in it, and her thumbnail dragged along the edge of her forefinger nail, back and forth. "It bothers me. I've tried to stop and I can't. My feet do it." She saw him looking at her hands and put them in her lap. "That isn't a refusal. I've still got my one."

He thought of Asha, and let the thought go, since it was not hers. He nodded and went back to the bow. She breathed out, and he understood that he had been given something and had been wise enough not to pick it up.

She looked up at him then. She did not ask what the ford was. She set her cup on the bin, and he knew the next question was coming before she opened her mouth.

"Why won't you spar? Not *can't.* You could break any man in this fort. Muthu asks every seventh day and you look at him as if he'd offered you poison."

It was the cheapest-looking question of the afternoon. He had known since the first minute that it was the one she had come down the hill to ask.

He could say *I won't answer that.* She had given him the right, and she would keep her word. He laid the bow across his knees.

"I was twenty-four," he said. "Eight years ago, in spring. We took a raider captain at the lower ford after a village burned. Twelve dead, and children among them. He fought like a bull until Muthu opened his arm, and then he dropped his sword and knelt in the mud and put up his hands."

Neela did not move.

"That is the law. A man who yields is a prisoner. I'd taught it to every recruit in the fort. I was standing over him with a bleeding hand, and he looked up and spat on my boot. Then he said what he'd done in that village, in detail, in a pleasant voice." Devan turned the bow in his hands. "I put two fingers to the notch below his ear. A marma point. Done right, it stops the heart in the time it takes to say a short word. I knew where it was. I'd been taught what it was for."

The rain went on.

"He was dead before he finished falling. Muthu was ten paces off and saw all of it. Nobody said anything. The captain was in the wrong, and I was in the wrong, and the village was still burned." He kept his voice level, a report. "I haven't struck a living person since. Not in practice, not in anger. I drill alone. If I held a sword against Muthu, I'd know exactly where my hand wanted to go."

He waited for the words he had heard from Janu and from his own head. *You were provoked. Anyone would have.*

Neela said, "He was on his knees with his hands up. You hit the point under his ear with two fingers and you killed him. He had surrendered and you had the right to hold him, and you chose the strike."

It was exactly what he had done. Hearing it said plain was like stepping into the cold pit water.

"And then you stopped," she said. "You didn't do it twice. You didn't do it to the next man who made you angry, or the one after that. You've had that hand and that rage, and every day since, you've stopped." She tilted her head. "I'm not saying it makes it smaller. I'm saying I see both. He's dead and you stopped. Neither cancels the other."

Devan found his eyes were stinging, and he was irritated, and then he was not. Nobody had forgiven him, because he had not let anyone get near enough. Janu had tried. Muthu had tried with jokes. They had all wanted to lift the weight off. This woman had looked at it, named each part of it correctly, and left it exactly where it lay.

He picked the bow up off his knees. He found the loose end of the string he had abandoned and began to wind it, round and round the nock, and his hands were steady again.

"That's the first time anyone has said it that way," he said.

"It's the only way it's true."

He nodded. His throat was not quite his own.

"My turn," she said, "and I'll give you one before you ask. You think it's the hand you don't trust."

"Yes."

"It isn't." She spoke gently, which was worse. "A man who didn't trust his hand would spar with a padded stick and find out. You won't touch anyone, because you're afraid of what you'd learn. That it isn't about the hand at all. It's about wanting to."

He should have been angry. He sat very still and let it be true.

"That's an answer, not a question," he said.

"Then here's the rest, and it's not a question either." She wrapped her hands round the cup. "I count the doors in every room I sleep in. Twenty-one years. I've never once been asked why."

Nothing came to him. He thought of a man who lay down each night with his back to a wall and his sword on the floor, every door in the fort watched. He thought of her at the foot of a stair, counting. Neither of them said what the two had in common, and he saw that she had not expected him to.

He drew the knot tight and did not bargain over it. "What do they call you? At the House. Not the cover. Not the Chirayil ward."

"Nothing."

"You don't have to—"

"I'm not refusing. I mean nothing." She turned the cup in her fingers. "I'm Seventeen. The seventeenth girl in the House's book. I answered to it for twenty-one years, the way a goat answers to a whistle. There was a name before that. I was six when they took me, and I lost it somewhere on the road. Chathu has it in a ledger, I think. He's never offered."

Seventeen. He turned the number over and found that it sat in his chest like a stone he had swallowed.

"So I learned everyone else's," she said, and shrugged. "It's a bad habit in my trade. A woman who wants to disappear should leave every servant a stranger. But I kept finding out. Raiu's mother is dead. Chinna's got a bad hip. Kannan's goat is called Pepper and bites. Unni's mother sells salt fish." Her shoulder did not quite make it up. "Somebody should know them. Nobody ever knew me."

He had watched her do it since the first week. He had watched her say *Raiu* to the boy at the stair-head and see him straighten. He had taken it for craft, a spy's easy charm. She was collecting names for people the way other women kept birds, and every name was a thing she had never been given.

"Neela," he said.

She went quiet.

"That's yours," he said. "I know it's the cover's. I've said it every day since the wedding, and it's the one I'd answer to if it were mine." He stopped. "I'm saying it badly."

"You're saying it fine." Her voice was not steady. She picked up the pot and poured tea nobody had asked for. "Don't do that again until I've finished this."

He didn't. They drank in the sound of the rain.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


She did not take the resin that night.

She said so at dusk, in the dim of the chamber, with the flask stoppered and standing in the niche where it lived. "There's nothing to hunt in this, and the scent's washed off the hill. I'd just be wet." Then, more quietly, to the flask: "Another bare night. It goes on the count."

Her nails had been itching all afternoon. He had seen her pressing them against her thumb. He said nothing about that, and she gave him a look that thanked him for it. He kept a tally of her bare nights, as she kept her steps, without ever having decided to, and he knew what a full moon of them would do to her hands.

He kept the pebble in its pouch. Tonight, for once, he would not need the stone.

She slept first. She slept the way she did everything, by the numbers, flat on her back with one hand over her breastbone. He lay beside her and listened to his own breath hitch and to the cistern chain striking the third watch. The rain had thinned to a whisper on the slats.

Past the middle watch she woke. He knew it by the change in her breathing. She did not move, except that her right hand came up and pressed her nails hard against her thumb, one after another, as if testing them for cracks.

"Eight," she said, to the dark. Flat, a sum. "Eight nights to the new moon."

He did not know what ended on the eighth night. He knew that she did, and that she had said it to the ceiling and not to him.

The question was there at once, whole. *What happens then?* He could ask it. She was awake, he had asked her harder things that day, and the right to refuse was already spent on neither side. He lay with it behind his teeth. She had not offered it, and a thing taken from her in the dark was the leash she feared. He would hold it through the night, and through whatever came of tomorrow, and spend it only if she turned to him first.

It cost him. He felt every breath of it.

Presently her hand fell back to her chest, and she slept again.

Later, much later, she began to count something else.

It was barely sound. Her lips shaped it more than her voice did. "Four. Five." A pause, a catch. "Six."

He turned his head on the pillow, slowly, and did not breathe.

"Seven. Eight." The cadence was careful, a step and a step, a child going down into water. Her hand had slid off her chest and lay open on the sheet, the fingers curling. "Nine. Ten."

She stopped.

He heard her swallow. Then a small sound came out of her, high and wet, a sound he had heard once from a hound that had come home across the ford with a broken leg. Her brow pulled together.

"Don't," she whispered. "It's broken."

Her fingers closed in the sheet. A tremor went down her whole arm.

"It's *broken.*"

Devan held still. Every part of him wanted to reach for her, and he did not. A woman waking in a dream is a woman who does not know where she is, and he had no notion whether the hand that touched her would be his own in her mind. He thought of the Resin House. Of kennels, and the big man's shadow in the yard she had listed so fast, and a tin of warm sweets with a bill inside. Whatever was broken in there, the guild had done the breaking. Some stair in that yard, some girl's bone. She would have grown up counting steps toward the place where the bad thing waited.

After a time her breath came back down to a slow measure, and her fingers eased open. She did not say the number again.

He lay awake and watched the dark ceiling, and the thing he had refused to look at for eight years came up out of him and stood in the room.

Asha had counted stairs. She had said numbers kept the dark from filling in. Since the day she died he had gone to sleep every night with his back to the wall and his sword on the floor, every door in the fort watched and nobody at his shoulder. Six years of wall, laid over the two before it, over the ford. He had told himself that was strength. It was only the shape the grief had taken.

And she lay a hand's breadth away, who had counted at the bottom of the same dark. She had been paid to put a knife in him. She had pulled the strike in the pit, and killed for him in the narrows, and told him to his face what he was afraid of. She had done it with her eyes wide open, as if she had measured the whole of him and found him worth the telling.

He did not want a wall at his back. He wanted her there. He wanted her where he could hear her counting, near enough to hear her break, near enough to be told when he was wrong.

It frightened him more than the captain's spit ever had.

Beside him, very softly, Neela turned her face into the pillow and said a number he could not make out. He lay and listened, and he wondered what stair she was on, and who had broken it.

# Chapter 22: A Night Without Resin

At dusk Neela opened the shutters herself.

The third slat creaked, as it always did. She let it. The west lay open below the sill, laterite red going to brown, the pepper terraces stepping down into shadow, the sun a hand's width above the ridge. The flask sat stoppered in its niche. She had not touched it all day.

Two nights without. The ache would count them both, the way Chathu counted every debt, and send the bill at the end of the moon.

Seven nights to the new moon. And on the second spur, in the grove, stood a split slab of laterite with a cup cut in its top. Ammu had promised to read that stone every night of her week of silence. The other one's scent-mark in the cup meant *still working*. An empty cup meant a woman who had stopped, or been stopped. Tonight the cup would be empty, and Ammu would stand in the dusk and wonder which of the bad answers was true.

Neela looked up at the spur. The grove sat higher than the fort. She had worked that out on the rampart, with her mouth still warm from his, and it was still true: anyone under those pala trees could look down into this room. A lit window would be a lamp on a hill.

She knew exactly what it would cost. She had hidden for twenty-one years, and for once she wanted to be seen on purpose. She leaned on the sill and left the shutters wide.

The door opened behind her. She knew his step: heavy heel, then the pause where he checked the corridor. The pause came. Then it didn't finish.

"The shutters are open," Devan said.

"Yes."

"You haven't—"

"No resin." She did not turn. "Not tonight."

He came to stand a pace behind her. He did not touch her. The light lay red across the floor between them, and she heard him take the white pebble from his pouch and set it on the shelf by the door, softly, like a man putting down a tool he would not need.

"Why?" he said. Then, because he was who he was: "I'm not refusing it. I'd like to understand it."

"A question for a question."

"Ask."

She turned round. "Do you want to be here? Not guard, not warden. Here, in this room, with a woman who can't turn into anything tonight."

"Yes." No pause. His ears had gone dark. "That one was easy."

"Then here is mine. I want one night when the only thing in this bed is a woman." Her voice was steadier than her hands. "I want to be looked at by lamplight and not run."

He lit the lamp himself. A little sesame oil, a wick, the flame leaning in the draught from the open window. He set it on the chest so that it lit her and not him, which was thoughtful, and a mistake. She crossed the floor and moved it to the shelf between them.

"Both of us," she said.

He nodded. Neither of them knew what to do with their hands.

She was twenty-seven and had spent twenty-one of those years answering to a number. He was thirty-two, a warden who had not let anyone this close since the ford, eight years gone. Neither had ever stood in lamplight and let the other look.

She unwound her day cloth slowly, because if she went fast she would stop.

The scars were the first thing the light found. Pale ridges across the throat, two bands on each inner wrist, raised and shiny where the resin had eaten. He had seen them in the dark, she knew, and in the grey before dawn. He had never been given them to look at.

He looked. He did not flinch and he did not soften his face into pity. She had to look at the lamp for a moment.

"I'd like to touch them," he said. "I'm asking. You can say no and I'll still be glad of tonight."

"Which?"

"Whichever you'll give."

"Wrists," she said. "First. With your thumb. Nothing tight." She held out her right arm, palm up. "No one holds my wrists. Not once. Not even for play."

"No one will."

He took her hand as if it were something that could spill. His thumb came down on the inner ridge and traced it, the length of the band. His hands knew every pressure point on a body; she had watched him in the pit, and she knew what that thumb could do. She watched him choose, deliberately and visibly, to know none of it.

"Does it hurt still?" he asked.

"Not since the first year. Chathu held my wrist the first time. He said it would hurt less if I didn't pull." Her voice was dry. "It hurt exactly as much."

"A question," he said. "Why tell me?"

"Because you asked what the scars were, in the second week, and I said a fall from a cart." She held his eyes. "That was a lie. I'm tired of that one."

He lifted her wrist and put his mouth to the inside of it, to the worst of the ridges, so lightly that she only knew it by the warmth. Her whole arm tightened to the shoulder.

"Throat?" he said, against her skin.

"Last."

"Last, then."

She took his hand in both of hers and put it where she wanted it, flat against her ribs, and felt him breathe out. Then she unwound his mundu, because she wanted to see how he held still when she was the one deciding. He held still beautifully. He had a soldier's body, scarred along the left forearm, a puckered white seam at the ribs, the long muscle of a man who drilled alone before dawn every day for eight years. She put her palm against his chest and found his heart going hard.

"You're nervous," she said.

"I haven't done this since before the ford."

"Eight years."

"I'm out of practice." He said it gravely, and she laughed, and he smiled, and some of the stiffness went out of both of them.

They went to the bed, and it was not graceful. His knee hit the frame. She had to move his sword, which lay on the floor on her side, hilt toward her as every night, and she slid it under the bed with her foot. "You can have it back after," she said, and he said, "I won't want it."

He was careful with her, almost too careful. When his thumb found the wrist ridge again he stopped on it and stayed, motionless, until she nodded. At every step he waited for her answer, and she understood that the waiting was not doubt. It was a man who had spent years learning what restraint was for, and had finally been handed a use for it.

"You can stop asking," she said at last, breathless, her back against the pillows. "Ask me with your hands. I'll tell you if it's wrong."

He took her at her word. His mouth moved down over her collarbone, her breast, the soft place under her ribs, and when his hand slid between her thighs, slowly, she put hers over it and showed him the pressure, the pace, the angle that made her catch her breath. He learned it like a drill. He did not drop a single detail. When he said her name against her skin, the cover's name, which was hers now by use, something low in her gave at the sound of it.

"There," she said. "Don't change anything."

"I won't."

She came with her fist in the sheet and her heels dug into the mattress, quietly, in the end, with a low cry that surprised her. Her sight cleared slowly. He was watching her face as if he were holding something he had not expected to be given, and she pulled him up by the shoulders.

"Now you," she said.

"You don't have to."

"Devan."

He came over her on his forearms and she guided him in, her hand on his hip, the angle hers. He went slowly. She felt the effort in him, the shaking just under the skin. She wrapped a leg round him and said, "More," and he said it was her choice, and she said that was exactly what she was making. He moved, and she moved with him, and the lamp threw their shadows huge on the wall, two bodies with nothing hidden in them.

His rhythm broke before hers, and he put his face into her neck, a hand's width from the scarred place, and stopped there, not touching it. His breath warmed the ridges.

"Now," she said. "Please."

His mouth closed over the pale band at her throat, gently, and a long shudder went through her from scalp to heel. No one had ever been allowed to put anything there but resin. He kissed the scars one by one, as if reading a line he had to get right. She broke a second time with her hand tangled in his hair, and he followed her with her name in his mouth, and the shutters were open the whole time and nothing in the dark moved.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


Afterwards he lay with his head on her shoulder and she traced the seam at his ribs.

"Ford?" she asked.

"The captain's knife. Before he knelt." His voice was slow. "A question. What's the count tonight?"

She smiled at the ceiling. "Two nights. The ache will want its due."

"Then I owe it."

"It's mine to pay."

"Not alone."

She did not answer that. She said, in a voice she barely knew, "Stay till morning."

He lifted his head. He knew what it asked. A man had never stayed while she was awake and unguarded, and she had never asked one to. "Neela—"

"Please. I want to wake up here."

"I'll be here," he said.

He pulled the sheet over them both and did not put the stone in his mouth.

She woke once in the dark. The lamp had burned out. The shutters still stood open, and a cool wet wind moved across the sill, smelling of rain and laterite. Devan lay turned toward her, one hand open on the sheet between them, palm up, as if waiting. His breath hitched on its long count, the little catch a third of the way through, and she lay and listened to it.

Her nails ached at the quick, a dull, small warning.

*Two,* she thought. *Two nights.* She added the second one to the tally in her head and found she did not mind the sum. She laid her hand in his open one and slept.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


An hour after first light she went up the second spur alone, in her day kasavu with the shawl over it, and found Ammu sitting by the stone with her back to the slab and her arms round her knees.

Ammu was naked and grey and shaking. The turn-back had taken her at first light, and she had not moved since. Her hands were scraped raw, and burrs and red dust clung in her hair. Neela dropped the shawl over her shoulders.

In the cup of the stone, thick and fresh, lay a jackal's scent-mark. Ammu's own, laid in the dark and left to say *I came, and I looked.*

"You came," Ammu said. Her voice cracked on it. "I thought you'd got yourself killed."

"I'm sorry."

"Don't be sorry. Be clever." But her grin was thin and bright. Neela sat down beside her, pulled a rice cake from her sleeve, and put it into Ammu's scraped hand. Ammu ate it in four bites. The hollow after a turning was the same for every one of them.

"Tell me," Neela said.

"Well." Ammu licked her thumb. "The watcher in the pass village doesn't count faces, chechi. He logs scent-posts. Every dawn he walks the hill edge and reads which of your marks have gone fresh in the night. No fresh marks, no one out on your account. A refusal. And a refusal gets written to Chathu."

"The rain night," Neela said.

"Already went down as a gap. Your three bare nights last week went down too. And the writ's landed, so every gap counts double now." Ammu tipped her head. "Another run of gaps on top of the rain night is the one he'd act on. So I ran yours."

"Yours is the stone."

"Mine is the stone. And then I ran yours: the east wall, the terraces, the sentry's walk." Her smile was shaky. "I took the day cloth from your cache under the spur, in my teeth, and rubbed it on every post you touch. Your own scent, laid fresh. He'd read it as you." She winced. "It works once, chechi. The cloth's rags now, and the cache is empty. Don't count on it twice."

"Ammu."

"I know. I was right under the rampart when the guard changed, with the wind wrong, and a boy with a lantern nearly trod on my tail." She leaned her head on Neela's shoulder. "It was reckless. I'm good at reckless. You always said so." The hum started, small and absent, the fishing song. She stopped it herself. "The shutters were open. I saw from the grove. A lamp, and two people behind it, and nobody on the ledge."

"I know," Neela said. "I knew it would be seen."

"I know you knew." Ammu said it without heat. "I'm not going to say anything about that, today. I'll be too hungry."

Neela said nothing. The lie she had told at the cache, three dawns ago, lay between them, warm and wrong.

"I got you something," Ammu said. She reached under the shawl, to her waistband, and drew out a folded scrap of paper gone soft with sweat. "I shouldn't. It came up with the last cart. Chathu sends the carrier a balance sheet for every open contract so the man knows who to dun. I tore the strip clean off the foot of it, out of his satchel." She held it out and did not let go at once. "The carrier will blame damp, or rats. Chathu will too. Until somebody moves on whatever's written there. Then he'll ask who read that line, and only one jackal was ever near that satchel."

Neela unfolded it. Chathu's neat hand, resin-ink, tidy as a ledger:

*Balance due from the Keeper on the widow's seal.*

Nothing else. Not a name. She read it twice, and the cold came up through the soles of her feet.

*The widow's seal.* The charter line. If the warden of Kettamala died married, his wife held the pass-seal in trust until the council named another. Chathu's plans had made her that widow before she ever climbed the hill. The balance was due from the man who would come to take the seal from her hand.

Not the council. Not the queen. *The Keeper.*

The Keeper of the Passes. The office that held one of the three dot-and-split seal boxes. The office that sent the kind letter every season for six years and never once climbed the hill.

"Chechi?" Ammu said. "You've gone the colour of ash."

"It's nothing."

"It's not nothing. It's the first time you've ever lied to me badly."

Neela folded the slip along its creases and put it inside her bodice, against her skin.

*Ittan.* The name came up in her like a bell. Genial Ittan, my friend, the man whose letter had asked so warmly after the warden's bride.

Below the spur Kettamala stood red in the early sun, and in the warden's window, a small dark square, the shutters were still open. Somebody was in the pit under the east wall, drilling alone. She knew the shape of him at that distance, and the way he stopped in the middle of a form and glanced up at the hill.

She traced it through, step by step, as she would a route. Alone, the slip was nothing: a torn foot, blamed on rats. But Devan would not sit on a name. The day he moved on Ittan, the rats would stop being the answer. Chathu would hold the sheet up to the light and ask who had known that line. The carrier's satchel would lead to the one who had reached into it.

So the name was Devan's only chance, and giving it might be Ammu's cage.

Ammu was watching her, fierce and starving. Neela put the second rice cake into her fingers and said nothing at all.

# Chapter 23: The Keeper's Name

By the time the sun cleared the east wall, Devan had run the long form eleven times and still could not make his hands stop.

Last night was in them. Her wrist under his thumb, the ridges at her throat, her fist in the sheet. When the form ended the eleventh time, he stood in the red earth with his chest heaving and the lamps burning pale in their niches, and heard a step on the stairs cut into the north side.

He knew it. Light, even, taking the third stair a fraction short, as if shortening a stride could make her smaller.

Neela came down into the pit in her day kasavu with a shawl over it. There was red dust on the hem and a burr caught in the border. She had been up the hill. Her face was set the way it was when she was counting something.

"You've been at it since the dark," she said.

"Yes."

"The hands wouldn't stop."

"No."

She nodded, as though that were a reasonable answer. Then she put her hand into her bodice and drew out a small, soft, creased slip of paper.

She did not hold it out at once. She looked at it, and then at him. In a month he had learned to read the order of her decisions. She chose, and then she checked that the choice was still hers.

"You said you wouldn't ask," she said.

"I won't."

"I know. That's why." She crossed the earth between them and put the paper into his hand. Her fingers were cold. "Ittan. Councillor Ittan, the Keeper of the Passes. He bought the contract. He is the man who wants you dead."

Devan did not look down at the slip. His eyes stayed on her.

She had said it flat, the way she said sums. But her eyes were wide and a little wild, the eyes of a woman who has just stepped off a wall and not yet landed.

"I didn't buy it," he said. It came out stupid and he knew it.

"No. You didn't ask, either. I'm not trading, Devan. I'm owed nothing for this." She wrapped her arms round herself. "I just don't want you to die of a man I could have named."

Once, in a wet dawn, he had offered her his silence for this name, and she had refused it. He had never expected the name to follow him into the pit, unasked, in her hand.

"Thank you," he said. It was too small.

"Read it."

The slip opened under his thumb. A neat hand in black ink, tidy as an accounts book. *Balance due from the Keeper on the widow's seal.*

"Where did you get this?"

"A guild-sister tore it off a carrier's satchel. It's Chathu's hand. I won't say more about her." She said it quickly, then lowered her voice. "I'm sorry. That's the one thing I can't give you."

"I haven't asked."

"I know you haven't." Her mouth tilted. "It's very annoying."

He looked at the paper again, and the fort he had been raised in shifted under him.

*The widow's seal.* The old charter line, the one the marriage writ had returned to him in plain words.
