If the warden of Kettamala died married, his wife held the pass-seal in trust until the council named a new man. No warden in living memory had married in office. Someone had read that line and seen what it was worth.

Someone had *sent* him a wife.

The pieces slid, heavy and cold, one against the other, as if they had always lain in that order and he had been too close to see. The Keeper's office kept one of the three boxes that cut the dot-and-split mark. And the match itself had been pressed on him by the council, with Ittan's name first on the letter that proposed it. He had noted the name then. He had thought: *a man being kind.*

A bride to kill him in his own chamber, so that a woman who owned nothing would hold the one seal in Kettamala. And who would come to take it from her? The man who had arranged for her to have it.

"He doesn't want me dead," Devan said slowly. "Or not first. He wants the seal. Every cart that goes through the Monkey Pass on a wax tally goes through whoever holds it." He turned the slip over, as if the back might say more. "He wanted a widow, not a corpse."

"A widow who'd be given a kind man's help," Neela said. "A weeping girl from a pepper house, and a councillor who'd ride up to console her. Who'd take the seal off her hands for safekeeping."

"And if she'd refused?"

"I was to be gone by then. One more beast-kill on the hill road."

His grip closed on the slip. For a moment he did not trust his hands near her, and he put them behind him.

"Asha found the first token," he said. "Six years ago. She was dead within the month."

"Yes."

"He has been writing me kind letters for six years."

Neela did not tell him it was not his fault. She stood and let him have it. When he looked up she was a pace away, near enough to touch and not touching. It was what he had wanted from the beginning: to be told the thing, and not be soothed.

The stair took him.

He didn't decide to. His body was already on the north steps, the paper in his fist. Kodiyur is four days at a hard ride, he thought. A warden's writ. The queen's household. Muthu to hold the gate.

"Devan."

"Four days. I can be in the capital before the moon is dark."

"Devan, wait."

"If I put the slip before the council—"

"It's Chathu's hand on a scrap that says *the Keeper*." She had come up the steps behind him, her voice low and fast. "That's a title, and he has a dozen men under it. You'd stand there with a torn strip from a guild satchel, and they'd ask where it came from, and you'd have to say a woman of the Resin House gave it you. In your own bed."

He turned on the third step.

"And look at the road. The pass is sealed in eight days."

"Eight days," he said.

"Three days ahead of the rains. The council always seals early, and I've been counting to the rains, not the seal." She drew breath. "A warden who rides to the capital with the sealing at his back doesn't ride home in time. You'd leave Kettamala shut in with the sealing day coming and nobody but Muthu and a goat-boy. And you don't need to go to him. He's coming to us."

He stood on the step and let that settle.

"The sealing," he said.

"Every year a clerk climbs. This year he wrote that he'd come himself." She had stood by the chest while he read the letter aloud at the window, and she had counted the friends in it. "Why does a man who has never climbed suddenly want to seal the pass with his own hand? Because there's a balance due, and he doesn't trust a clerk to collect it."

*A man ought to look upon the gate he locks.* He had thought it kindly meant.

"He's coming to see whether I'm dead," Devan said.

"Or whether I'm still useful." She said it without pity for herself. "Either way he walks up the hill on his own feet."

The anger went out of him, not gone but turned, the way a sluice turns a river. He had wanted something to hit. He sat down on the stair, because his legs had gone strange.

Her thumb was pressed hard against the nail of her forefinger, and the skin was red at the quick.

"On the rain night you woke and said, 'Eight nights to the new moon,'" he said. "It's six now. I didn't ask what ends then. I'm asking now, because you've just given me everything, and I'd like to know what it costs you."

She was quiet long enough that the cistern chain clanked the hour.

"At the moon the account closes," she said. "If it's still open, the guild settles it another way. I won't say how yet." She kept her voice level. "The ache is already starting. Two nights I've skipped. My nails itch at the quick in my sleep."

"For me."

"For a bed with a woman in it." She shrugged, not much. "I'd do it again. I'm only telling you the count."

He took it as it was meant, without comfort. Six nights, then. The plan, whatever it was, had to live through them.

"There is a better place for this," he said. "The queen."

She went still.

"Not a slip. You. In person, before the court. The Resin House, the contract, the whole account of who buys. No one knows it as you do. If the queen hears it from an odiyan's own mouth—"

"No."

"Neela—"

"No." She did not say it loudly. She said it once, and it held. "Think what you're asking. A confessed odiyan before the crown, with no master to vouch for her. You know what the law calls that."

He knew.

"A rogue," she said. "Hunted by guild and crown both. The second I say what I am in that hall, I'm a thing your queen is entitled to cage. You'd have to choose between the crown and me, and I'd never do that to you." She flexed her hand. "And it isn't only me. Every page in that ledger is a life. If I stand up in the queen's hall and say *odiyan*, Chathu doesn't need to wait for the moon. He opens the book. My sister's page is on the next leaf."

She did not say a name. He did not reach for one.

Argument rose in him. Asha had written, in her fast, laughing hand, *The truth protects whoever finds it,* and Devan had built six years on it. Here was the truth, a woman who could lay out the whole crooked house, and she was telling him that in this country it was paid for with another person's neck.

*Two honest things,* he thought. *Neither of them wrong.* The law he served had a hole in it exactly her size.

"Your word is the one thing I can't take from you," he said. "I don't want it as evidence if that's the cost." He stopped. "I'm not saying I like it. I'm saying the stake is yours, and it's yours to name."

She let out a long breath. She looked at him as though she had braced for a blow and found a hand instead.

"You'd have made a good witness at the House," she said. "They'd have hated you for it."

"Caged me, I expect."

"Eventually." Her mouth tugged. "Thank you."

"Don't thank me. Help me. If I can't carry you to the queen and can't ride to the Keeper, I have to bring him here and catch him in front of my own men, under my own law. Tell me how."

She sat beside him on the stone step, with the oil lamps guttering and the red earth drying in patches.

"Your law," she said. "What's the oldest thing in it?"

He thought. It was good to think about something that was only a problem. "Border law. The pass stands under the warden's seal, and any cart at the padippura is the warden's to search. On sealing day it's a custom older than the charter: every cart that passes the gate that day, in or out, is opened. Nobody has ever been refused. Nobody has ever been a councillor."

"Does it say *every* cart?"

"It says every." He saw where she was going and felt the first spark of something he had not felt in a long time, a plan with a shape. "It doesn't say except the Keeper's."

"Then his cart comes up with his baggage and his clerks," Neela said, "and your men open every box. Not because you accuse him. Because it's the day, and it's the law, and it would be rude to wave one cart through." She smiled, small and hard. "In front of the whole garrison."

"And if he carries nothing?"

"A man who comes to collect a widow's seal brings his instruments. A seal box. Tallies for the right hand."

"The box proves nothing," Devan said. "His office keeps one of the three. It's the Keeper's by right. He'll say he carries it to seal the pass, and he'll be telling the truth."

"I know. I'm not looking for the box." She held up a finger. "I'm looking for what he carries with it. Whoever sells passes on the road can't pour wax in a ditch. If I know how guild couriers work, and I do, he brings them ready. Blank split-ash tallies, already waxed with the dot-and-split. Not in any clerk's book. Cut from the same split ash, sealed with the same mark, as the tally in the satchel from the narrows."

He saw it. An honest Keeper had no use for a tally that no one had written down. "That would hang him."

"In front of your garrison, with your own men holding the box." She turned her hand over. "And if he leaves them behind, then we've lost a day. But he's walked up a hill to see a dead warden and found a live one." She leaned her shoulder against his, briefly, an offer. "And I'll watch the road at night. I'll know who's coming before the gate does."

"Muthu will hold the gate that day," Devan said. "He'll open the councillor's boxes himself. I'll tell him it's the custom."

"It *is* the custom."

"Yes. That's why it's good."

They sat a while. The sun had come over the wall and lay in a strip across the pit. He was aware of her shoulder, the dust on her hem. She had given him a name unasked, and then the price of it, which she had not meant to give. He folded the slip smaller, once, twice, until it was a hard square in his palm.

"Neela."

"Mm."

"This doesn't bind you. If Ittan walks into the trap and we take him, nothing is owed from you. No second price. I'll say so before anyone."

"I know." She was quiet. "That's why I gave it."

She stood and brushed the dust from her palms. She glanced round at the earth, the lamps, the worn marks of eight years of one man's feet. Then she said it casually, as if asking about weather.

"Spar with me."

The words landed in his chest like a stone dropped in the cistern, and he listened for the bottom.

She hadn't asked to win. She had watched him from the east steps and the rim of the pit, and from a feed bin in the rain. She had named what he was afraid of. Now she set the offer down between them, lightly, and left the lifting to him.

His hands had gone cold. In his mind there was a mud-bank and a man on his knees and two fingers going to a notch beneath an ear. And beside it, patient, a woman who had said *you stopped.*

"Not yet," he said.

He heard how it sounded. He braced for the flat look he deserved.

She only nodded, slowly. He watched her hear the difference. *Not yet*, not *no*. He had said it in a voice that left the door unbarred, and neither of them had to remark on it.

"All right," she said. "Not yet."

She went up the steps. At the top she looked back, the sun behind her, the burr still caught in her border.

"Eat something. You've been at it since the dark."

He gave her the count of fifty, then climbed after, the slip in his fist. At the head of the stair the fort lay open to him, the cistern chain clanking its hour. Down at the padippura a rider sat a lathered horse that had no business there. It was not the ninth day, not post day. Captain Muthu stood at the stirrup with his big back to the yard, pressing a letter sealed in plain grey wax into the rider's glove.

He was not laughing. Muthu, who laughed at everything, stood perfectly quiet.

Devan knew that quiet. He had known it since they were boys in the kalari pit, when Muthu had broken a practice staff and sworn it was the damp. Devan did not move. Six nights to the moon, he thought. Eight days to the seal. The plan needed a gate, and the gate needed a man he could read.

He did not say what he knew. He opened his shirt and put the folded slip inside it, flat against his ribs, where no sleeve could give it up. Then he went down the stair in no hurry, as a warden would who had only noticed an early rider.

"Early post, Captain," he said. "What's it for?"

Muthu turned. For one breath, his face had nothing in it at all.

# Chapter 24: The Carved Bird

The second letter went into Sudhi's glove at the padippura an hour after sunrise, and this time Devan was close enough to see the wax.

Plain grey. No mark.

He had come down from the east wall with the cistern chain still clanking in his ears, meaning to check the carts for sealing day. Instead he stopped behind the gate pier, in the shade of the roof, and watched Muthu lean toward Sudhi's pony, a brown, bad-tempered beast called Biscuit, and say something low to the palace rider. Sudhi laughed. Muthu did not.

Two letters in two days. Muthu, who had told Devan every thought in his head since they were boys in the same kalari pit.

Devan waited until Sudhi had gone clattering down the Monkey road. Then he stepped out.

"Another one to your mother?"

Muthu turned. The big face was open and cheerful, and for a half-breath it stayed that way. Then something behind it went out, like a lamp carried past a window.

"Yes," Muthu said.

"She writes often."

"She worries." He rubbed the back of his neck. "I'm her only son. You know how it is."

Devan did know how it was. Muthu's mother wrote once a month, in a hand like hen tracks, and Muthu read the funny parts aloud over supper. Her letter had come and been answered on the last post day. Now there had been one yesterday, off post day, and one this morning. And Muthu stood in the sun with his hands hanging, silent as a post.

Devan had seen that silence before. At twelve, in the pit, Muthu had lost a bout he should have won, and when the master asked, he had gone exactly this quiet and said he'd slipped. He had not slipped. Muthu was loud when he told the truth. He only went still when he lied.

*Ittan's.* The thought was cold and complete. A kind man with a grey-wax hand in Kettamala, and a letter every season that never climbed the hill because it did not need to.

He might be wrong. He had been wrong about the oldest things before, about a girl who slept with her face to the wall, and a knife under her mattress that he had left where it lay. Accusing Muthu in the yard would warn whoever sat at the other end of the thread. Locking him up would blind Devan to everything the letters carried.

"Walk the wall with me," Devan said. "I want your eye on the east cistern before the chain jams again."

"Now?"

"Now. And on sealing day I want you at the gate. You'll open the Keeper's boxes yourself. It's an honour. The garrison should see my captain do it."

He said it lightly, as a kindness between friends. Muthu's laugh came late, and too big.

It was no kindness. If Muthu was Ittan's, then the Keeper's boxes were the one place he would want to fumble. So Devan would stand at the cart himself, an arm's length from those big hands, and Bhaskaran would take the second box whatever Muthu said. A man who meant to hide something could not hide it from both of them. And if Muthu's fingers hesitated over a lid, that would answer Devan better than any letter.

They walked the wall together. Devan kept his face easy and his own counsel. Below them the hounds ran the yard in the morning light, all fourteen, and for the first time in years he saw how few people in this fort he would have trusted with his back.

One of them was still asleep in his bed. He had slid her knife back under her side of the mattress the night after she gave it him, and she had let it lie.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


The stone was warm under Neela's hand, and the cup in its top was empty.

She had come up the second spur in daylight with a plan, which was a thing she did not often have. She would leave a mark. She would wait for Ammu, and give her a message for Chathu: *the warden is near breaking, give me the full moon.* Five nights to the new moon, and she needed every one of them.

Ammu was not there. A man sat on the far side of the slab with his back against the lightning scar, and he was whittling.

"Seventeen," Vellan said.

She had taken a breath to call out. She let it go. Nothing in her face moved. Eleven contracts had taught her that much.

He was near forty, and he had been grey at the temples since she was fifteen. A big man, with a gate's shoulders. His hands were the surprise, as they had always been: thick and scarred, and as careful with a knife as a goldsmith's. A shaving curled off the wood under his thumb and dropped to the red earth between his feet.

"The jackal-girl's at her inn," he said. "I told her I'd keep the stone today. She didn't argue." A grumble that might have been a laugh. "You never could teach her to argue with a man my size."

"What do you want, Vellan?"

"What Chathu wants. New moon." He did not look up from the wood. "He sent me up to see the state of things. I see a bride in a good kasavu with burrs in the border, who's slept soft and eaten well, and who hasn't put a hand to her husband's throat in thirty-odd days."

"The pass doesn't close until the rains."

"The rains aren't the deadline. The moon is." The knife turned. "New moon, Seventeen. Do it, or I do it, and you go home in a collar."

*Collar.* It was not a jest. She saw the kennels, the iron rings in the wall, Kirri's silver anklet with its bells cut off.

Neela made her voice dry. "He sleeps with a sword on the floor."

"Not on your side. He lays it hilt toward you. The watcher says." He glanced up, light eyes under a heavy brow. "A man who gives his wife a sword in the dark is either a fool or he's got something he wants you to see."

She said nothing. He went back to the wood.

But her mind had caught on the last thing he said and would not let go. The watcher walked the hill edge each dawn and read scent-posts. That was all he did. He had never seen the inside of a barred chamber, and he could not know where a sword lay on a floor. Someone inside the fort had told the guild.

She put that away, carefully, where her face would not find it.

"Give me till the full moon."

"No."

"One more cycle. He's closer than he was. I can—"

"I know what you can." There was no heat in it. "I have watched you for twenty years, girl. You've never missed. You've never been slow. Eleven for eleven, and then you stand on a hill and leave a mark in a stone and ask for time." His thumb ran down the grain. "Chathu counts. So do I."

"And if I say no?"

"Then I do it." Calm as weather. "I'll be quicker than you. And cleaner, and he won't know me when I come."

A small shape was coming out of the block under his hands now. A bird. A fat, short-winged thing with its head turned, no larger than a plum.

"You carve for the little ones," she said.

"Somebody should." He blew the dust from its back. "They sleep better with a thing on the mat. It doesn't matter what."

"And the ones you kill? Do they sleep?"

The knife did not stop. "A blade isn't guilty, Seventeen. Only the hand that throws it. I learned that young. It makes a man restful." He turned the bird in his fingers. "You carry it too, whether you say it or not."

She did. She had said it to herself on every road for twenty years. It tasted different in his mouth. In his mouth it tasted like the last thing a man says before the door is shut.

She sat down on the edge of the stone, because standing was beginning to show.

"Vellan." She kept her voice level. "Kirri."

The knife stopped.

"You've seen her. Haven't you? Ammu writes and gets no answer. Chathu says she's where she wished to be. You go to the Resin House more than any of us. Where is she?"

He did not answer. The wind moved the grove behind them, and far down the cistern chain clanked the hour. Vellan looked at the bird in his palm for a good while, then went back to the wood and took a very small curl from the wing.

It was the worst answer he could have given. She sat with it, and did not move.

"Three times," he said at last, to the bird. "Three times I've earned the release. Chathu says there's always one more." He closed his hand. "Go home, Seventeen. Eat something. Do whatever a woman does with five nights."

He was gone before she had counted ten. He did not seem to move. One moment the big shape sat against the lightning scar, and the next there were only shavings on the earth and a smell of scorched tamarind drifting on the wind.

She stayed until her hands stopped shaking. Then she went down the hill and did the thing she had not done for any person since she was six years old.

She broke a line.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


"His name is Vellan," she said.

It was dusk, and he had found her on the east rampart by the housing door, as he did now at the end of most days. He did not touch her. He had a way of standing at an angle that left the wall at her back and the open air at his.

"The guild's enforcer," she went on. "Near forty. Grey at the temples. If you see a very large man who walks without a sound, don't ask him his business." She stared at the terraces going dark. "He's the one who comes if I don't. At the new moon."

Devan was quiet a moment. "You met him."

"At the stone, this morning."

"And he let you walk away."

"He wants me to do it first. It's cleaner." She made herself say the next part. "He gave me five nights. He said, *Do it, or I do it, and you go home in a collar.*"

Something crossed his face that was not fear. It was older than fear. He looked at her wrists, at the pale bands of scar at the edge of her sleeves.

"What does he become?" he said.

She had known the question was coming. She had walked it up the hill and down again all afternoon.

"He's a—"

Her mouth had already shaped the first hard, rounded sound. She bit it off. Devan's eyes had gone, without his seeming to know it, across the yard to the west stair and the dark knob of the newel at its foot. They stayed there a moment. Then they came back to her.

"I can't tell you that."

"Can't."

"Won't. It's the one line I have left, Devan." She heard how her voice sounded. "Everyone in that House has a shape, and the only thing none of us has is the right to say another's out loud. You wouldn't carry the thing in your hands if I gave it you. You'd carry it in your mouth."

He took a breath, and she waited for the argument. It did not come.

"I won't ask," he said. "That one I understand." A pause. "You've told me more than I had any right to."

"There's more. He knew where your sword lies at night. Hilt toward me. He said the watcher told him." She turned from the wall. "The watcher walks the hill edge and sniffs at posts. He can't see through a barred shutter. Someone in your house talks to mine."

He went still. "Someone inside."

"Someone who's seen the floor of that room."

"Janu's girls. Raiu. Half the fort has carried water through it." His voice was flat. "I'll find out."

"I've told you his name and his day. He'll come on the new moon. He's no soldier. He's quiet, and he's patient, and he's done it for twenty years."

"I'll double the watch." He was already counting under his breath. "Two more on the west stair. Another pair on the wall. Raiu on your door tonight, and two men at the stair head."

"Watches don't stop him."

"Everything stops somebody."

"Not him. I've seen him walk through a sentried yard that was full of lamps and leave a carving on a sleeper's mat. Nobody saw him go or come. Double the watch if it helps the men sleep. But don't think a line of spears is a wall."

He looked at her while the dusk went blue behind him.

"Then what is?"

"I don't know. I wish I did." She almost smiled. "Someone inside who doesn't want him there. That's all I've ever seen stop him, and it wasn't me."

The wind came over the rampart. He weighed it, as he weighed everything, the way a man tests a plank bridge before he trusts his weight to it.

"Muthu has been sending letters," he said. "Grey wax, no mark. Two in two days. His mother writes once a month, and he goes quiet when he lies. I think he's Ittan's man."

She considered it honestly before she answered. "He could be. He could be the one who told them about the sword." It made her point harder to say, and she said it anyway. "But a man who writes letters is a man who talks, and talk is slow. Vellan doesn't write. If you spend five nights watching your friend's hands, you'll be looking the wrong way when the other one steps out."

He did not answer at once. His jaw worked, and his eyes went down to the yard, where a big shape crossed the lamplight with a laugh she could hear from here. Whatever he had built about Muthu, he had not built it lightly. He held his own wrist, hard, as if it were something he might drop.

"Then I'll keep Muthu close and watch the stair," he said. "Both. But I'll set the stair first, because you asked."

*Because you asked.* It was a plain thing, said plainly. She had given him a name she owed to nobody, and he had weighed it against a man he had known since boyhood, and it had come down on her side.

"Thank you," she said.

"Don't." But his ears had gone dark, and he looked away, down at the housing, where the lamp in its niche was just catching. "You'll be in the chamber tonight?"

"Yes."

"I'll bar the shutters. Raiu's at the door."


<center>\* \* \*</center>


The shutter bar went across at full dark. She heard it settle in its brackets, and the third slat gave its small creak, and Devan went to see the west stair for himself. He would be gone an hour. He had kissed her hand at the door, just the knuckles, and gone down the passage with a sword at his hip and fourteen hounds' worth of worry on his shoulders.

Raiu stood outside with his cowlick and his torch. She had heard him cough twice.

She took her time. She undid her braid. She set the kindi right on its side of the bed, spout to the pillow, as every night, and sat on the edge to unwind the border from her day cloth.

The pillow was wrong.

It was only a little wrong. The cloth lay as Janu's girl had smoothed it. But in the dip where her head would go, small and pale in the lamplight, sat a thing the size of a plum.

A bird.

She did not touch it. She sat very still and counted. The door: watched by a man she knew by name. The shutters: barred on the inside by a man she trusted. The third slat: untouched, or it would have sung. A chamber with one lamp and one bed and no place at all for a very big man to hide.

Then she looked, and she made herself see it.

Fresh-cut wood. The body round, the wing folded close, the head turned a little to one side, the way it had been turning in his hands on the hill. The beak was whole. Under it, on the cloth, lay one curl of shaving, still smelling of scorched tamarind.

He had finished it on the hill, under her eyes. Then he had carried it down and into the fort.

She laid her palm over the bird and then took it up. It was cold. So was the cloth beneath it. It had lain here a good while, in a bed no one had touched since morning.

*He sat in this room while I sat on his hill.*

Sometime between the hour she went up the spur and the hour she came back, in a fort of sixty soldiers and fourteen hounds, Vellan had walked through the warden's chamber and set a gift where she would put her head. He had come straight from her. He had not troubled to hide it. He had wanted her to find it. And he had walked in past someone, or been let in by someone, who knew where the sword lay.

She heard Raiu cough again, on the other side of the door. He was nineteen. He was still on his feet.

*Five nights,* she thought. And then, because she had counted all her life and did not know how to stop: *He means four.*

# Chapter 25: Claw Marks

The cistern chain clanked the hour, and Devan set his foot on the first step of the west stair as the last red went out of the laterite.

New moon. The east was already black.

He had doubled everything. Two men at the stair head, with orders to hold it whatever they heard. Pairs on the wall, every one of them mid-change on the hour chain. At the last light he had found the stair lamps drained, not burned dry: the wicks still white, the cups tipped, a thumb of oil left in each. Somebody inside the fort had wanted this stair dark. With every post tied to the chain, Raiu had gone down to the lower court for oil, and Devan took the dark stair himself, because it was the only way to her door.

Behind the barred chamber door, Neela stood at the shutter with her sleeves pushed back. She had not smeared the resin. The other one could not warn anyone, she had said, and she wanted hands tonight. One nail had split at the quick that morning. She had hidden it in her fist, and he had not mentioned it.

He passed the parrot newel, its beak worn smooth, and the shadow behind it stepped out.

He had never seen the man. He knew him anyway. Grey at the temples, a gate's shoulders, a body that made no sound on stone. Neela had described him on the rampart, and her description had been exact.

Vellan was human. His collar hung open and his throat shone black with resin.

"Warden," he said quietly.

The blade was simply there. Devan's sword was half free when it opened him across the ribs, left side, a clean hot line. He felt the edge slide, then the cold behind it. The steel was smeared dark, and a bitter smell came off it like scorched tamarind.

He got the sword up and caught the second cut on the guard. The third he took on the flat. Then he was climbing, backward, his heel finding the steps by memory. Four. Five.

"You don't swing," Vellan said. He sounded almost curious. "Eight years since you struck anyone. Your house keeps good count."

"She told me your name."

"Good girl."

Devan gave ground. He could have killed the man. His body knew where: the notch under the ear, the hollow below the breastbone. Two fingers would do it. His hands remembered every line of it, and went cold with the remembering. A mud-bank. A man on his knees.

Eleventh step. It rang hollow under his heel, over the drain slot, and he almost went down on it.

"Warden!"

Raiu's voice, from the lower court. Then boots, fast, and a torch snatched from a court bracket jerking light up the wall. The boy had dropped the can and come at the first sound of steel, a spear in his other hand. Above, a stair-head man took a step down.

"Hold the head!" Devan shouted. "Whatever you hear!"

The man stopped. Devan had given the order himself, and he heard what it cost.

"Stay back, Raiu!"

Vellan said something under his breath that might have been *pity*. He stepped down and away from Devan, toward the bend, where the wall took him out of the torchlight.

Devan did not see what happened there. The stair turned, and the wall hid it. He heard it: a cracking, a long grunt that rose and thickened. He smelled resin, sharp enough to sting his eyes.

What came round the bend was already a bear.

It was huge and shaggy and black, with a pale muzzle and claws as long as a man's fingers. It took the boy before the torch had finished falling. Raiu got the spear halfway up. The bear struck once, straight across the chest, and the boy went back against the rail with a short, broken sound.

The torch hit the step and rolled. Its flame lay along the stone like a cut.

Devan was down the steps before he had decided. His legs were wrong, heavy, and the cut at his ribs had gone from fire to ice. He did not strike. He put his body between the boy and the beast. The bear swung a paw at his head, and the claws raked the rail beside his ear and took a fist of pala wood out of it.

He thought: *Not a word. He has not said a word.* A man in shape could not speak. Beside that was another thought, small and cold. *I did not see it turn. The wall hid it. I am nothing to him but a man he is trying to kill.* The word would have been only a word.

Then he saw the boy's chest.

Raiu lay across the third step, blinking at the sky. His tunic had been laid open in four parallel furrows from collarbone to belt, deep and wide-set, the edges dragged ragged by something that did not cut but tore.

Devan knew those marks. He had stood over them at twenty-six, in the grey before dawn, with Janu holding a lamp and his hands going out to the cloth over Asha's chest.

*Four ruts. Wide as this. A bear, in the walls. On this stair.*

The numbness had already reached his fingertips. He could not feel the rail under his hand. It rose up his side like cold water up a post, and as it came the thought went through him whole. *Asha. Asha, on this—*

And the poison put it out.

It took the thought as a flood takes a lamp. He could not hold it. He knew only that he had been about to know something, and that it had gone out of him, and that his tongue felt thick.

He braced himself on the rail. The bear reared over him, jaws open.

"Down!"

Her voice came from above, flat and carrying, the voice of someone giving an order on a ledge. "Down! Both of you! Now!"

Light feet on the stair. Even, quick, taking nothing short.

She came out of the dusk at the head of the flight with her day kasavu hitched to the knee and the plain guild knife in her hand. Her hair was loose, her face bare. She was human, down to her breath. She had heard the clash through the shutters, and she had unbarred the door, and she had come.

Behind her the two stair-head men broke from their post at last, spears levelled, lamps swinging high, and light poured down the steps.

The bear saw them. It dropped to all fours. Its head swung up the stair, from lamp to lamp, to the spearheads and to the knife, and the little eyes in the great skull went flat. Three blades, and light. It did not roar. It turned, in one heave of its shoulders, and took the low wall at the landing in a single scrabbling vault. It was gone into the drop and the dark, with a sound of gravel going down.

Neela did not follow. She was already on her knees beside Raiu.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


The boy was nineteen.

Devan had taken four months and asked twice to learn his name. He had stood in the yard and said it wrong, and Raiu had laughed, and it had been the best sound that week. A cowlick that never lay flat. A mother he spoke of at the fire.

Neela had both hands pressed on the torn chest, and the blood came up through her fingers. She said, "Raiu." Her voice was perfectly level. "Raiu, look at me."

He looked at her. His eyes went to Devan, above her shoulder, and some question was in them.

"You held the door," Devan said. It came out thick. "You held it, Raiu."

"Raiu." She said the name again, as steadily as a woman counting. "It's all right. Raiu."

The breath went out of him slowly. She said the name the whole time, until there was nothing left to say it to. Then she bowed her head over the boy's chest, and her hands did not move.

The two men stood on the steps with their lamps, not knowing where to put them. After them came Muthu, enormous and white about the mouth, shouting for the physician. Devan heard it as through water. He had sat down on the step without meaning to.

"Don't," Neela said, not looking up. "Don't try to stand."

"I'm awake."

"You're grey."

She was across the step in a moment, her bloody hand at his side. She opened his tunic with a jerk. The line across his ribs was thin, and the flesh at its lips had already gone the colour of a plum. A black thread was crawling out from the cut toward his belly.

He watched her see it. Her face did not change. Only her mouth went small.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


Eravi came with his case, stooped and wheezing, and hung his lamp from the bedpost.

They had carried Devan up between them. Muthu took the head and a soldier took the feet, and he had let them, because the stair had begun to slide. Now he lay on the bed with his tunic cut away. Janu stood at the foot with her hands knotted in her apron. For once she had nothing to say.

The old physician sniffed the cut. He sniffed it again, and drew back.

"Blackbark gall," he said.

No one answered him.

"I read of it in my master's books. It is not a fort poison. It is..." He looked from Devan to the ceiling, not at Neela. "A sap, rendered, mixed with a vegetable gall. It does not kill quickly. It goes into the blood and walks slowly to the heart. I cannot draw it. I have not the knowledge, and I do not think anyone in this country has."

"How long?" Devan said.

"Three days. Perhaps a little more, if you lie still and are kept cool." Eravi's mouth worked. "And if you are very lucky."

Muthu made a low sound. Devan looked past him. Neela had come to the foot of the bed, beside Janu. Blood was drying on her wrists. She did not look at the physician.

"It's the guild's," she said. "Chathu's, from the groves. There's only one antidote, and he makes it himself. It's at the Resin House. In the tapping shed, in a cupboard with a lock I know how to open."

"That is a day's hard ride," Muthu said. "Through the wood."

"A day there. A day back. I have three." She said it as a sum. "I'll be back before the third dusk."

"No."

It was Devan's voice, and it was louder than he had meant. The room turned.

Neela only looked at him. She wore the face she wore when she had already counted the exits.

"Eravi," she said, "give him whatever will keep him still, and keep him cool. Janu, vinegar and cold cloths, and don't let the fever frighten you. It doesn't kill. Muthu." She paused, and he saw her choose, and he saw her check that the choice was hers. "I need to talk to you outside."


<center>\* \* \*</center>


He should have stayed on the bed.

Eravi had put something bitter in a cup, and Devan had drunk half and spilled half and, when Janu turned to the basin, got his legs under him. The room swung. He went down the passage with a hand on the wall, the left side of his body a piece of cold wood. He kept hold of what he had: a torch rolling on a step, something dark at the wall. Where the rest should have been there was a gap, smooth as a swallowed stone. He reached for it and it slid away.

The stable smelled of dung and hay and lamp oil. A single lantern hung from a beam.

She was saddling the grey mare. She had changed into riding clothes, a dark mundu hitched high and a shawl crossed over her chest. Her knife was in her belt. Her hands moved on the girth with the speed of someone who had done this in the dark a hundred times. As he watched, she pulled the strap through, and her thumb flinched from the buckle, and he saw that three nails had split to the quick where there had been one this morning.

She did not look round.

"Go back to bed."

"You're going to the Resin House."

"Yes."

"The guild's house." His mouth did not want to make the words. He made them anyway. "The place that sold you, and made you, and wrote your name in a book. They know you didn't kill me. Vellan knows. By now, Chathu knows."

"I know."

"Then you know what you're riding into."

She turned. The lantern put gold on one side of her face, and the other side was dark. She looked at him for a long moment, at the way he leaned on the doorpost, at the colour of his lips.

"I do," she said. "I've been in that yard since I was six."

"Neela."

"Muthu will keep you cool. I'm trusting him with your body, not your secrets, and I hate it, but I have nobody else. I've told him what to do if I'm late. Don't argue."

"Muthu," he said. It was all the breath he could spare for the name he had been afraid of since the yard.

"I know what you think of him. I think it too." She tightened the strap. "He'll still not let you die in front of him. Whatever else he is."

"I don't want you—"

"I know what you want." There was no heat in it. She had gone gentle, and that was worse. "You'd have me sit here with your hand in mine and be the woman who could have gone. I won't be her. Nobody taught me this one, Devan. I'm choosing it."

She gathered the reins. He took a step, and the floor tilted, and he crossed the straw anyway. He caught the girth strap where her hand had been and touched one of the split nails. It was rough as bark. She let him.

He could not stop her, and he reached for her anyway.

His hand found her wrist. The pulse beat under his fingers, quick and living. The bone of it was narrow, the pale ridged scar there, and the gold bangle was warm.

"If you go there," he said, "they'll cage you."

He felt his grip begin to go, the way a rope goes slack in a wet hand. He tried to close it. His fingers would not answer. The word he had meant to say next lay on his tongue, and the dark came up over it.

"Neela, they'll ca—"

His hand opened.

# Chapter 26: The Kennels

He did not finish the word. His hand opened on her wrist, and Neela caught his weight before the straw could.

"Muthu!"

The captain came at a run from the stable door, where he had been standing with his arms folded and his big face gone quiet. He took Devan under the shoulders without being told. Between them they got him down flat on a hay bale, his cheek against the sack.

"He's only gone under," she said. "The poison does that. Keep him cool. Vinegar, wet cloths, and the shutters open, whatever Janu says. Don't let Eravi bleed him again. He has no blood to spare."

Muthu nodded. He was not laughing. In five weeks of barracks noise she had never seen his mouth so still, and she knew what the stillness meant, because Devan had told her. She looked at him anyway, straight, and said the rest.

"If I'm not back by first light on the second day, put him in a cart and start up the wood road. Meet me on it. It saves a day he hasn't got."

"Neela—"

"First light. Not later." She pulled the girth tight, and the mare blew. "I'm trusting you with something I can't spare. Don't make me sorry."

"I won't." He said it low, with his eyes on Devan's face, and she could not tell what it was worth. She had no one else to ask.

She swung up. Bhaskaran opened the wicket at the padippura without a word. Then there was only the road and the dark, and the cistern chain clanking the hour behind her like a door being shut.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


Rain began before dawn.

She rode the Monkey road downhill with the mare's breath smoking and her nails throbbing at the quick. By morning the ruts ran like streams. By noon the water had found her spine. She counted milestones, then breaths, then the mare's strides between breaths. It kept her from counting how many hours Devan had left.

Three days, Eravi had said. Perhaps.

She trusted no inn. She walked the mare through the fords and ate the heel of bread Janu had pushed into her saddlebag. A fever she had not given in to for weeks began to climb, and she let it climb, and rode.

By late afternoon the road forked at a leaning stone, and she took the narrower way. The trees changed. They went black at the foot, as though soaked in ink, then all the way up. Their bark hung in plates, and the plates wept. The smell reached her before the shadows did: bitter, scorched, like tamarind left on a fire, with pine sap running underneath. She had breathed it every day from six years old to twenty-seven. It was the smell of her bed and her bowl and the first time a man held her wrist still.

*Blackbark Wood,* she thought. *Home.*

She had never hated a word so much.

She tied the mare in a thicket a mile out and went in on foot along the old drain path she and Ammu had used as children, when the cook was asleep.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


The Resin House rose out of the wet wood in tiers of dark roof, with tapping sheds along the east side and the long training yard lying open between them like a swept palm. No lamps burned in the yard. They never had. Chathu said light was for those with something to show.

She went over the wall by the old fig and crossed the dark without a sound. The third shed had a loose hasp and a lock she had learned to open at fourteen, on a dare, with a pin and a held breath. It still opened for her.

Inside, the smell was thick enough to chew. At the back stood the cupboard, plain deal, with a brass lock the size of her thumb. She worked her knife tip in and felt the wards give one by one. Shelves of small clay vials, stoppered with wax, each tagged in the same fine hand she had once copied letters from. *Fever, young. Fever, old. Black gall, counter.* Chathu labelled everything. She had never found it funny until now.

She took the last one, no longer than her finger, with a pinch in the wax where his thumb had pressed. She pocketed it and locked the cupboard behind her. Her skin crawled to the roots of her hair.

*Eleven for eleven,* Vellan had said. *You've never missed.* She had walked in, found it and taken it. It had all been too quiet and too easy.

She stepped out into the yard. The rain had stopped. A smell came across the wet earth that was not resin. Wet fur and old musk, it sat much lower in her mind than a smell had a right to, a long way down, where she kept being six.

The bear stood in the middle of the yard.

It was upright, which they seldom were, its black shoulders roped with wet, the pale Y of its chest showing. Its head swung slowly to find her. *It was on the wall that night,* she thought. *He ran to be here first.*

It did not charge. It stood and let her see it.

Behind it, lamps opened, and a dozen men came out of the long shed with hooked poles and nets and iron rings on chains. She knew three of them by name. She had eaten beside Pachan at the long table when she was ten, and he had given her his sweet.

She had the knife out before she had decided anything. She took the first man at the wrist, the second at the knee, the third under the nose with the heel of her hand. She was fast. She was always fast. But she had ridden a night and a day on bread and fever, her hands were shaking, and a net dropped across her head from behind like a sheet.

They pulled her down. A knee found her back. Someone wrenched the knife away, and someone else tore at the fold of her shawl. She heard herself make a sound as the vial came out and was held up in the lamplight.

"There it is," said Pachan. He did not sound unkind. "Master said she'd come for it."

*Master said.*

The bear dropped to all fours and went into the shadow without looking back.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


The kennels lay under the oldest roof, behind the cook-house and a shuttered well. She had been told once what they were for. She had never been inside.

They carried her in by the arms, the net still tangled in her hair. A lamp burned at the end of a long aisle between iron cages, floor to roof, with straw and a bucket in each. A deer lay curled in the nearest, its hide gone patchy. Beyond it were a hare, a mongoose, something striped, and a very thin fox that did not lift its head.

Their eyes shone as she passed. They did not follow her. That was the worst of it. They looked at the lamp and the hunters' feet and the wall, as if they had once been looking for something and had forgotten what.

The hunters unlocked a cage near the end, put her inside, and knotted a leather cord from her wrists to a ring in the wall, high enough that she had to stand. Pachan tested the knot.

"I'm sorry, Seventeen," he said. He meant it, and it made no difference. They took the lamp away.

She waited until her eyes found the dark. Then she looked at the cage beside hers.

It was lower and smaller, the straw fresh that day. In it, curled with its nose against its tail, lay a civet: grey-brown, long-bodied, a dark mask across the face. It watched the corner of the cage, where nothing was. Round its hind leg, worn smooth and thin, was a silver anklet with no bells.

"Kirri," she said.

Nothing moved. She said it again, louder, and then in the voice she had used at eight, across the yard behind the sheds, calling to a twelve-year-old Kirri who was far too clever for her own good and who had always answered, *What?* The civet's ears turned a hair toward the sound. That was all. They turned back.

*Released.* She had lived twenty years on that word. Eleven contracts, and one more, and the page burned and a name given back. She had believed in it the way other people believed in rain.

Kirri had been the best liar in the House. Kirri had wanted a boat.

She hung there by her wrists in the smell of straw and old fear and bitter resin, and said quietly to nobody, "Well. That's the price, then."

She counted the cages. Forty-two, and one of them was hers. She counted them again.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


Chathu came at dawn.

She had not slept. Grey light reached the aisle through a high grille, and then came a step she would have known blind: soft, unhurried, a little uneven on the left foot where he had once broken it. The lamp in his hand swung a slow circle on the straw.

He was an old man, round-shouldered, in a clean white mundu, a plain wooden comb in his hair. A big ledger in black cloth was tucked under his arm, and in his free hand was a tin. His eyes were wet.

"Child," he said. "You look half dead."

"I rode a long way."

"I know. I'm sorry for it." He set the tin on the cage bar. "Jaggery and sesame. I had the cook make them warm. Eat."

She did not look at it. She had learned, around the fifth contract, what a gift was.

"No. Never, when you're frightened." He sighed, sat on an upturned bucket, and laid his palm on the ledger the way another man would stroke a sleeping dog. "I want to tell you how it comes out, Seventeen. People are better for knowing. It is only a matter of accounts."

"Chathu—"

"You came for the antidote. It was well done. You always had a hand." He nodded as at a good sum. "I made that vial, and I know how long it takes. Your husband will be dead by the end of tomorrow, and not before. At dusk I will put the resin on you myself, as I did the first time. I will open this book to your leaf and read it aloud. Then you will be in the cage next to Kirri, and by the next rains you will not remember your own name."

He said it as a physician might say that the bone had to be set.

"Ammu is coming back to me," he added. "I have recalled her. She will carry the resin pot at dusk, and she will stand and watch what comes of a covered scent-post. A lesson is best learned with the eyes."

Neela said nothing. Her throat had closed.

"And Vellan will finish your warden after. Tonight, when the cart comes in at my gate. He has only to wait for it."

*The cart.*

She went cold from the scalp down. Nobody had said a cart in Chathu's hearing. Only two people alive had heard it, in a stable, over a sick man's body. She remembered Muthu's stillness, and *I won't,* said low, and how she had not been able to tell what it was worth.

"Who told you about a cart?"

"A house pays its debts in news, child." He smiled, sadly, and did not say more.

So the order had been hers. *Meet me on the road,* she had said, and had named the hour, and someone had carried it out of Kettamala faster than she had ridden. She had built the trap herself and sent a dying man to walk into it.

"You sold her," she said. Her voice, when it came, was hoarse. "Kirri. You told her it was release."

"I told her what she needed to hear to do her work." He did not flinch. "And when she was tired, and the work had cost her more than she could pay, I gave her a place. Do you know what happens to an odiyan with no ledger? The crown hunts her. A rogue dies in a ditch with a rope round her neck. Here she is fed. She is kept dry. She is not afraid."

"She doesn't know her own name."

"No. But she is not afraid." He turned the ledger. "Twenty-one years I have kept you, Seventeen. Rice, oil, salve, teachers. A tutor for your letters. I have written down what you cost me. I weep when one of you is lost, and I mean it, and the book balances all the same. Without a leash you would all be hunted dogs. The ledger is mercy."

The old voice was gentle as ever. And she saw, for the first time, that it had never once been lying. He believed it. That was the worst thing about him. He had believed it at the table when he gave her sweets, and he believed it now, at the bars.

"The twelfth contract," she said. "Release."

"Is a lovely word." His smile was sad. "Eleven you did. The twelfth you failed. Release has always been a word for what comes after."

He rose, tucking the book under his arm, and lingered looking down at her. She thought for a moment he would reach through the bars. He did not.

"You are thinking you were owned. You were. You are a clever girl, you have always known it." His head tilted. "But it comes to me you think this book is the whole of it."

She said nothing.

"The ledger isn't the only book, child," said Chathu softly. "It never has been."

He took up the lamp and went away down the aisle with his uneven step. The tin stayed on the bar, the steam still lifting from the lid.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


They came for her an hour later and bound her better.

Three hunters, and Pachan not among them. They took off the soft cord and put on hard, wet leather that would shrink in the heat. They pinned her arms behind her at the elbows, tied her wrists together, and hauled the knot to a staple in the floor. They did not speak. One ran his thumb down the inside of her wrist, over the pale ridged scar, as though it were a mark on a cheese.

Then they left her alone with the light climbing the grille.

*Dusk,* she thought. *Maybe ten hours.*

*Second dusk is tonight. Muthu will have started at first light, as I told him. If he doesn't meet me, he keeps coming, and he reaches the gate at tonight's dusk.*

That was the arithmetic. A sick man in a cart, at the gate of a yard full of hunters, and Vellan waiting. She worked at the knot until the leather bit. It held.

Chathu had seen her turn at sixteen and written her into a book, and called it mercy. Devan had seen her ten times and more. On the nights since the rampart he had held a river pebble in his cheek, so that in sleep or fever the word could not slip, and he had never asked her to thank him for it.

Both of them held the same word. One kept it in a ledger. One kept it behind his teeth.

If the resin took her at dusk, she would not go into the straw with her mouth shut. She would say it herself, aloud, to someone who was looking. She did not know why that mattered. Only that the word should be hers, once.

Boots came across the yard outside, and a voice she knew, quick and low and out of breath. Not words. Only a tune.

*Pull and pull, the tide is coming in.*

The door at the end of the aisle opened. Light fell down the straw, and Ammu stood in it.

She was mud to the knees, in the plain brown cloth of Valli the pepper trader's girl, her braid half down. A hunter held her elbow, though she was not struggling, and Chathu's voice somewhere behind her said, *Let her see.* She had ridden all night, and her face was grey with it.

She looked down the aisle at the cages. At the fox. At the deer. At the small grey shape in its straw with the silver on its leg.

Then she looked at Neela.

Twenty years. Neela had seen that face laugh and lie, tease and steal and cry itself sick over a dead sparrow. She had never seen it like this: still, and white, with its mouth shut hard over something it was not going to say. Her lips began to shape a line of the tappers' verse, *the name given back,* and stopped before the sound.

Across all the bars, Ammu's eyes held hers, and did not look away.

# Chapter 27: Leopard

They forced the resin on her before sunset, so she would have time to bleed.

Four hunters held her in the cage, and Pachan was one of them. He kept his eyes on the straw. A fifth man took her chin in his fist and tilted her head back until the bars bit her skull. Ammu stood at his elbow with the resin pot in both hands, as Chathu had ordered. Her face gave away nothing.

"Hold still, Seventeen," Ammu said, in a voice that was not hers.

The paddle came down cold across the pale scar at Neela's throat.

She had smeared that resin herself a hundred times and chosen every one. The scars knew her hand. They did not know this. The old ridges split like seams. Blood ran warm down her neck and into her collar, and the cold went to fire. They did her wrists next. She heard a sound and knew it was hers.

It would take her at dusk. She could scream or sing or lie dead still, and it would make no difference. Already a low hum had started behind her teeth.

"The cart is at the gate road," said a hunter at the door. "Master wants everyone."

"Ammu stays," Pachan said, thumbing his belt. "Master says a lesson's better with the eyes. Watch her."

Ammu nodded. The boots went away across the yard, and the latch dropped on the aisle.

Neela counted. At ten, Ammu set the pot down on the straw, and her hands stopped shaking.

"Pachan's belt ring," she said, holding up a key. "He still leans like a man among friends."

She took a flat blade from her braid and cut the wet leather. The strips fell. Neela's hands came free, red to the elbow, and would not close.

"Here." Ammu pressed something small into her palm: a finger of clay, with a thumb-pinch in the wax. "Out of Chathu's sleeve. I've been picking that sleeve since I was seven."

The antidote. Neela stared at it. "Ammu. Your hair. You'll be—"

"Dead, probably. Listen, chechi, I haven't long." The old teasing was gone from her voice, and the speed stayed. "There's a way out. I told you once there was, and you didn't ask, because you never do. Once in a life, to one person who is looking at you, during the dusk turning, you say it yourself. Aloud, in your own voice."

"Say what?"

"What you are." Ammu's mouth shook. "The name given back. That's the verse. That's all the verse ever was."

The song. Neela had sung it a thousand times, tapping bark, and never heard it.

"It voids everyone else," Ammu said. "Chathu. Me. The witness page goes dead in his hands. After that it takes two voices to cage you, and one of them is yours. Nobody can do it without you."

"What does it cost?"

"Everything." Ammu laughed once, breathless. "You share wounds with that person for life. A cut on him opens on you. If he's dying, you go with him. Your entry in the ledger burns, so the guild knows, and you're rogue. The crown hunts you. And you can do it once, ever, to one person, so you'd better mean him."

Neela said nothing for a breath. "You've known for years."

"Since I was fourteen. An old master whispered it drunk, and I swore I'd never tell. I didn't tell you because it costs, chechi." She wiped her face with the back of her wrist. "And because hiding kept us alive. It did. Twenty years. I'm not sorry."

"I'm not asking you to be."

"Good." Her chin came up. "You know who."

"I've known since the stable."

"I know you have. I stopped believing your tom cat a week ago." Ammu pulled her up by both arms. "I'm a bad liar when it's you."

At the cage door she stopped. She dug two fingers into the resin pot and drew them down her own throat, then across each wrist, quick as a woman straightening a collar. She hissed once through her teeth. The black smear shone wet above her cloth.

"I'm not waiting for anyone to hold my chin," she said. "Come on."

They went out past the cages. Neela stopped at the small one. The civet lay curled around the silver on its leg and did not look up.

"I wrote her and wrote her," Ammu said, very low. "She's past the door, chechi. Come."

Neela put two bloody fingers through the bars and touched the grey fur. It was warm. She took her hand back.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


Last light lay red along the yard wall.

The shouting came from the gatehouse before they reached the corner of the cook-house. Steel on steel, and a big voice roaring words she could not make out. A horse burst out of the arch with its rider slumped forward on its neck, iron-shod hooves skidding in the wet. Muthu had not brought a cart. A cart was a day Devan did not have. A very large man with a sword slapped the horse's rump and turned his back on the yard.

He did not look round. Hunters came at him in the arch, and he took them there, one at a time, with the stone at his back.

The horse stopped in the middle of the yard, blowing. The rider slid sideways and hit the mud.

She ran. She went down on her knees in the wet beside him, and her hands found his face.

He was grey. His lips had gone the colour of ash, and the black thread had crept past his belt and up under his ribs. His eyes opened when she said his name.

"Neela." It was barely a sound. He spat something into his palm and closed his fingers over it, a small wet river pebble. "You didn't come back."

"I'm here. Swallow."

She dug the wax stopper out with her teeth. She tilted his head and put the vial to his mouth, and he took it as a child takes medicine, trusting and slow. Some ran down his chin. She held his jaw shut with her hand.

"Swallow."

He did. His throat moved.

"Now listen," she said. "The sun is almost down. I'll change. The resin's on me. Don't look away. Whatever happens in the next minute, keep your eyes on me."

"I've watched every turning you let me see." His mouth shaped the ghost of something dry. "I never once looked away."

"This is different."

"Then I'll look harder."

The shed latch scraped.

Chathu stepped out of the long shed with his lamp in one hand and a black cloth packet in the other. His step was uneven on the left foot. He saw her kneeling, and the open kennel door beyond the cook-house corner, and he stopped. For one moment she saw the old man's face come apart. He looked like someone who had found a hole in a floor he had walked for twenty years.

"Seventeen," he said. "Ammu. Oh, child."

Ammu was already gone. She was a shadow at the cook-house corner, and then she was not.

The sun touched the wall's edge. The hum behind Neela's teeth became a drum.

She stood. She put herself between Devan and the last of the light, so that he would see her and nothing else. Her wrists were running. Her throat was running. The first bone moved.

"Look at me," she said.

He was propped on one elbow in the mud. He looked. Not at her throat or her hands, but at her face, as if it were a thing he meant to keep.

Behind Chathu, a big shape came out of the shed shadow at a walk. Vellan pulled a flask from his collar and smeared his throat as he went. She saw it at the edge of her eye and did not turn her head.

The burn came up her spine like a fist opening. Her knees bent the wrong way. Her mouth filled with the taste of bark and copper, and the yard tilted and swam with a thousand new smells, and every one of them was prey. She had a minute, perhaps less, before the voice went.

She had never said it. Not in eleven years, not to a mirror, not alone on a hill. The other one, she had called it, afraid of its name as she had been afraid of her own.

To him, then. Her voice cracked and held.

"Leopard."

It went out of her like a thrown stone. Something in her chest unlatched and latched again, and settled with a click she felt in her ribs. Devan's face did not change, except that his eyes shut for the length of a heartbeat and opened.

Then her jaw lengthened, and her voice was gone.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


Chathu began to read.

He had the page out of its cloth, a single ivory leaf, brown at the edges, black lines crowding its face. A narrow slip was stitched to its corner with coarse thread. His voice was steady and kind, the voice that had read her to sleep once. "Seventeen. First turning witnessed in this house, Chathu, master. Night-shape—"

He stopped. He took a breath, because the word had to be said.

"Leopard."

Nothing happened.

She stood on four feet in the red mud, the whole yard a bell of smells, and waited for the cage to close. It did not come. There was only the click she had already felt, lodged in her ribs.

"Leopard," he said again, louder. "Seventeen. Leopard."

The ink moved on the page.

The witness lines blackened one by one, the way a leaf blackens in a fire, from the middle outward. A thread of smoke rose from the fine hand she had once copied her letters from. The entry curled and glowed in his fingers, and he cried out. The slip tore free at its stitches and dropped face up in the mud, whole and unscorched. *Intake, sold at six years,* it said in a different hand, with two more lines beneath that she could not read.

The rest of the leaf burned in the mud and was gone.

Chathu stood with his hand held out in front of him. Then he stooped, quick and careful, and took the slip out of the wet. His thumb covered what was written on it. His eyes were wet.

"What have you done?" he whispered. "What have you done to my book?"

Then, over his shoulder, flat and cold: "Vellan."

The turning was done in him. She heard the wet crack of it and smelled scorch and sap, and the bear came on at a run.

Devan was still propped in the mud on one elbow. The antidote had gone into him a minute ago, no more. It had not reached his legs. He could not stand.

It was not a fast beast, but it was a heavy one. Its mass shook the ground. It came straight at the downed man and the cat beside him, forepaws raised.

Neela threw herself at its flank.

It was all she had. Her shoulder hit the black ribs, and the bear's rush turned half aside. Its weight flung her off. She hit the mud and rolled, winded, and the ache in her new limbs said she would not do that twice.

The bear swung back for the man on the ground.

Devan got a knee under him and no more. His arm shook. He had no sword. The great black shoulder came down at him, near enough that the stink of it filled his face, and his right hand lifted from the mud with the fingers crooked.

It stopped in the air.

She saw him find the place. She saw him think of the ford, and the eight years. His fingers crooked tighter and hesitated, the way they had when he told her about the raider captain. His face went very still. He did not close his eyes.

The hand went in, hard and exact, to the hollow of the shoulder.

The bear's foreleg folded. The whole huge body pitched and skidded in the mud with a grunt like a tree coming down, and ploughed to a stop an arm's length from his knees. It lay there bellowing.

Devan pitched forward on his hands and stayed there, his head hanging.

A hot tearing line opened along her own ribs.

*A cut on him opens on you.* Ammu had said it not an hour ago. His old wound had split under the strike, and now it was hers as well. She saw him sway and clap a hand to his side.

He lifted his head. He looked at what he had done, and at her.

He did not look sorry. That was the thing. She had been braced for sorry.

Chathu's voice rose behind them. "Leave the cat! She'll keep till the bear's done. The jackal has Pachan's key. Take the jackal!"

Then she heard it from the cook-house roof. Three short yips, a pause, and one long rising note.

*Tomorrow, I will find you.*

She had practised that at eleven and nine, behind the sheds, until Chathu said the House had no use for dogs.

A fawn shape dropped from the thatch onto the bear's back, raked its ear with a flash of teeth and sprang off. Ammu landed in the mud with her hackles up and her tongue out, grinning. It was a ridiculous look on a jackal. It was exactly her face.

She turned and ran for the trees.

The bear heaved up on three legs. It roared, and the sound shook the yard. Then it went after her, limping, furious. The hunters followed with their poles and nets, spilling past the cook-house. Chathu's voice rang, thin and high, behind them.

*No,* Neela thought. The cat in her bunched to follow.

Devan's hand found her shoulder. It did not grip. It rested.

"Don't," he said. "They want you to follow."

She stood trembling under it. The cat's whole body said run. She weighed it the way she weighed a wall. Ammu was thirty strides ahead, and the nets were thrown for a leopard as much as a jackal. His tunic was black and wet down the left side where the old line had split, and the same fire lay along her own ribs. If she ran and a spear found her, he would bleed for it, with no blood to spare. If she ran, she ran alone, and left him in the mud of a yard that still had hunters in it. If she stayed, Ammu ran alone.

Nobody ordered her. His hand stayed light, and he did not say it twice.

She stayed. She hated it.

Every sound reached the cat's ears, a hundred times sharper than hers. The crashing in the brush and the bear's wounded breath. Then a stretch of nothing but running feet, and a yip that cracked, and then another, too far.

A man shouted, a single word. A spear thudded.

Then the cry. It was not the call. It was short and high and surprised. It rose, as if she meant to say something at the end of it.

It stopped.

The wind turned and came back across the wood. It carried the smell with it: jackal blood, hot and sharp, and nothing living under it.

At the gate, the steel noise had gone quiet too, and somewhere in the arch, a big voice was swearing hoarsely and alive.

Chathu's lamp passed along the yard wall and went into the dark. Neela did not look at it.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


She could not turn back before dawn.

Nothing in the world could change her till the sky did. She was a cat, and there were hours of night to get through, and a body that did not know how to weep.

Devan had not got up again. He lay on the wet earth, his breath shallow and fast, and the antidote had begun to work in him, or he would be dead. The stain on his tunic had spread, low on the left, and she felt the answering sting along her own side.

She stepped over him. She lowered herself, heavy and warm, across his chest and belly, and put her chin on his shoulder. She could feel his heart under her. She counted it.

His hand came up and found her ruff and stayed there, loose. He said nothing. The night came down, and the rain began again.

She did not leave.

# Chapter 28: Wound-Share

The sky went grey, and the cat let go of her.

It began in the spine, a pull like a wet rope drawn through her bones. Fur sank into skin. The great warm weight across Devan's chest thinned to a woman's, lying in red mud with the rain gone cold on her back. She had no cloth and no voice yet. Her teeth were chattering too hard for either.

Then the cold stopped mattering, because her side was on fire.

She pushed up on one hand and looked down. A welt ran along her left ribs from under the breast to the hip, raw and red, beaded with blood along its length. She had not taken this wound. Nothing had cut her side. Her own blood was at her throat and wrists, where they had held her.

It lay exactly where the black stain showed on his tunic.

"Neela." His voice was a scrape. He was looking at her face, only her face. "Is that my wound?"

"It's yours." She pressed two fingers to it, and he hissed through his teeth at the same instant, and his hand flew to his ribs. They stared at each other.

"On me," she said. "It came up when I turned." She could hear her own voice climbing. "Ammu told me. A cut on you opens on me. She didn't say it would feel like this. Every time. Every cut you take. For the rest of my life, Devan."

"Yes." He did not soften it. "And you'll cost me the same."

He touched his own throat. His collar was stiff and dark. Beneath it, when he pulled the cloth aside, ran a thin red line where an odiyan's scar would be, though he had none, and another showed at each wrist where his sleeves had ridden up.

"The resin," she said. "They tore me open. It's on you."

"I thought it was the fever." He looked at the lines, then at her wrists. Her fingers had swollen overnight and still shook. "They were bleeding through the turning?"

"Still open when I said it. That's when it took." She held them out before he asked. "Here."

He did not say he was sorry, or that he had done it to her. He tore a strip from the hem of his tunic with his teeth and one hand, and bound her left wrist, and then her right, quick and tight, not touching the open parts.

"You can't wrap yours," she said.

"Then I'll bleed in pairs." The corner of his mouth moved. "Seems fair."

It nearly made her laugh. The laugh came up through her chest and caught, and she found she was crying instead, without any sound. He said nothing. He moved his arm and let her put her face against his shoulder where the mud was thinnest, and he held her head.

Muthu found them like that. He stood a way off with a horse blanket, his face grey under the stubble and one sleeve black to the elbow, and made no noise at all. He set the blanket down on the mud and walked back to the gate.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


The yard was empty. That was the first wrong thing.

No hunters, no lamps. The long shed stood open, and a net lay trampled by the cook-house door. Chathu had taken his people and gone into the Blackbark Wood in the night, and he had taken the book. A burned leaf was one leaf. Every other page he held was still a leash, and he knew it.

Vellan had gone with him. A bear's track ran from the yard into the mud at the tree line, three clear prints and a fourth that only dragged, a long furrow where the ruined foreleg trailed. It led straight into the trees.

She found Ammu where the trees began.

Ammu lay on her side under a black-barked giant with her knees drawn up, small, a woman again after a night as a jackal. The spear had gone in under the ribs. Her face was turned to the earth, and her braid had come all the way down. Muthu had put a coat over her, and it was too big. The sleeve covered her hand.

Neela knelt. She had thought there might be something to say.

"Ammu," she said.

That was all there was. Twenty years, and it was a name. She turned the shoulder gently, and the face was not afraid. It looked cross, a little, as if Ammu had been interrupted halfway through a joke. Her mouth was open on the next word.

Neela closed the eyes with two fingers. She did not cry again. There was none left to do it with.

At the foot of the tree, in the roots, lay a small brown bundle: the cloth Ammu wore as Valli, the pepper trader's girl. Muthu had found it at the cook-house corner, where she had shed it, and laid it at the tree. It was folded in thirds, as Ammu always folded things. Beside it was a cord pouch with a thumb of black resin in it, the last she had held back for herself. Neela took it and tied it at her waist. She had no master now, and no ration, and she would not see the House's groves again except over a blade. The pouch was no gift. It was a debt, and she meant to spend it well.

Muthu had found a spade in the tapping sheds. He dug without being asked, a long way into the soft red ground, and she did not tell him to stop. Once she looked at his back. She remembered a stable, and an order, and Chathu's *a house pays its debts in news*. He had come on horseback, held an arch with a sword against twelve men, and slapped a horse's rump to send a sick man past him. A man could do both things.

She did not ask. She thought, *Later.* She was not sure she was brave enough for later.

When the grave was deep enough, Devan was there, upright by some stubbornness, a hand on a tree. He had washed his face at the cook-house well. He looked down at the small shape in the coat, and his lips moved once.

"Ammu," he said.

She turned to him. He said it again, carefully, like a man learning a name he had come to too late. "I'd have liked to know her."

"She'd have cheated you at seven wells," Neela said. "In the first game."

"Good."

"She hummed when she was afraid. A fishing song. *Pull and pull, the tide is coming in.*" Her mouth would not go on. He did not ask her to. He took hold of one end of the coat, and Muthu took the other, and they laid her down.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


She would not leave without the kennels.

Devan did not stop her. He lowered himself onto the cook-house step and said, "I'll be here," as though that were an ordinary thing to say, and it was the kindest she had ever heard.

The aisle was as it had been. The deer. The hare. The mongoose, the fox that did not lift its head. Someone had left the end door open, and the light lay along the straw. A hunter's lamp had burned out in its bracket. The troughs were dry, and the racks were bare. No one had come to feed them.

She stood a moment. Then she put her bound hands to the shuttered well behind the cook-house and tore the boards off it, and it hurt, and she did it anyway. She hauled the bucket up and down the aisle until every trough held water. She forked fodder from the loft into every occupied cage, forty-one of them, and carried the cook-house scraps to the fox and the mongoose.

Then she went down to the small cage and stopped.

Kirri slept. The civet was curled nose to tail, and a thread of grey light lay on the silver round its leg.

*Open it,* said the part of her that had been the best at locks. *Open every one. Run them out into the wood.*

And then what? These were not captives with a bolt to draw back. Their minds were going out like lamps. A cage could be unlocked. A caging could not. The deer would stand in the open and not know what the open was. Kirri would walk into the trees and forget why.

And one trough would not last them. Water and a forkful of hay were a day, three at most. Forty-one animals shut in an empty compound would starve long before she was a week down the road.

*No caging is ever undone.* She had known it all her life, as she knew the number of the stairs. She had never before known it with her hand on the bars.

She put her fingers through and touched the silver anklet. It was worn so thin she could have bent it.

"I can't take you out," she said. "I'd like to tell you I can. I'd like to say it was the cage." Her voice cracked. "I can't give you your name, Kirri. But I'll keep it. I've got yours and Ammu's now."

The civet did not stir.

"You wanted a boat." She swallowed. "I'll find the sea. I'll send the anklet in a fish basket, like you said. Do you hear? I'm coming back. For you, and for the deer, and the fox, and all forty-one." She counted them with her eyes down the aisle, and her voice dropped. "Every one. If it takes years. If I have to take the House down plank by plank."

Nothing answered her. She stood until the light climbed the grille.

Then she propped the end door wide with a stone, wiped her face on the back of her wrapped wrist, and walked out into the morning.

Devan was on the step. Muthu stood a little way off, leaning on the spade.

"They'll be dry again in three days," she said. "I can't open them. It wouldn't free them, it would only put them out to die."

"No." Devan did not look at the kennels. He looked at Muthu. "The first cart goes as soon as the wood road will take a wheel. Four of Bhaskaran's men and fodder. If the rain holds them, they'll lose a day, not more. Then every week the roads allow, until we know what else can be done."

Muthu looked at the spade. "I'll go myself," he said, hoarsely.

"After the sealing." Devan's voice was mild. "I want you at the gate."

Muthu's hands went still on the spade. "Aye," he said.

It was not enough. It was all that was on offer. Neela nodded once, and Devan nodded back.


<center>\* \* \*</center>


They rode all day.

Muthu had Devan before him on the big bay, one arm round his waist. She followed on the grey mare, who had stood in the thicket since the first dusk and now rolled a reproachful eye at her. Neither man spoke much. Devan slept in snatches, and she watched his back go slack, wake, and slacken again.

The road ran up out of the wood and the trees lost their black. Behind her lay the only home she had had, and it was closed. Her page was ash. The House would call her rogue. The crown hunted rogues, and no one could now vouch for her at any gate in Varanad except the one she was riding toward.

She had not a guild-sister left to lie to. She had not a master. The only people on her side of any wall who knew what she was were a captain with a locked mouth and a sick man who had followed her through a gatehouse.
